er-diagrams-cardinality-weak-entities · 12
DB-U1-Q01MCQeasy

In an entity–relationship diagram, a weak entity set is depicted by

  1. a single rectangle.
  2. a double rectangle.
  3. a single diamond.
  4. a double diamond.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ER notation. A weak entity set has no key attribute of its own; its existence depends on an owner entity set, so it is drawn as a double rectangle. Its identifying relationship is drawn as a double diamond, its partial key is underlined with a dashed line, and its total participation in the identifying relationship is shown by a double line.

Step by step.

  1. A weak entity set cannot be identified by its own attributes, only by (owner key + partial key).
  2. The standard Chen notation distinguishes it with the double outline: double rectangle for the set, double diamond for the identifying relationship.
  3. Hence the correct symbol for the weak entity set itself is the double rectangle.

Answer: Option (B) — a double rectangle.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A single rectangle denotes a strong (regular) entity set.
  • (C) A diamond denotes a relationship set, not an entity set.
  • (D) A double diamond denotes the identifying relationship of a weak entity set.

Common misconception. Confusing the double rectangle (weak entity set) with the double diamond (identifying relationship). They are different symbols for different things and a question may ask for either.

Faster method. If the option mentions a diamond at all, it is describing a relationship, not an entity set.

Takeaway. Fix the four ER symbols once: rectangle = entity set, double rectangle = weak entity set, diamond = relationship, double diamond = identifying relationship.

DB-U1-Q02MCQmedium

Entity set A has a many-to-one relationship R with entity set B. The participation of A in R is total and the participation of B in R is partial. Which one of the following is necessarily TRUE?

  1. Every entity of B is related to at least one entity of A.
  2. Every entity of A is related to exactly one entity of B.
  3. Every entity of B is related to exactly one entity of A.
  4. R must be merged into B because B's participation is partial.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Cardinality ratio plus participation constraint. “Many-to-one from A to B” means each entity of A is related to at most one entity of B, while one entity of B may be related to many entities of A. Total participation of A adds “at least one”, and the two bounds together give “exactly one”.

Step by step.

  1. Many-to-one A → B: an A-entity can be linked to at most one B-entity.
  2. Total participation of A: every A-entity is linked to at least one B-entity.
  3. At most one and at least one give exactly one B-entity for every A-entity.
  4. Partial participation of B only says some B-entities may have no A-entity at all; it constrains nothing about A.

Answer: Option (B) — every entity of A is related to exactly one entity of B.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) B's participation is partial, so a B-entity need not appear in R at all.
  • (C) Many-to-one allows many A-entities to share one B-entity, so B is not related to a unique A.
  • (D) A relationship is merged into an entity set only for a one-to-one or one-to-many case without loss, and partial participation of B does not force it.

Common misconception. Students read “partial participation of B” as if it weakened the constraint on A. Participation constrains only the entity set it is attached to.

Faster method. Total participation on the “many” side of a many-to-one turns “at most one” into “exactly one”.

Takeaway. Separate the two facts: the cardinality ratio bounds the count from above, participation bounds it from below. Combine them for the answer.

DB-U1-Q03MSQeasy

In a Chen-notation ER diagram, which of the following are drawn with a double outline or a double diamond? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A weak entity set.
  2. The identifying relationship of a weak entity set.
  3. A multivalued attribute.
  4. A derived attribute.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ER notation distinguishes four special constructs. Double rectangle = weak entity set, double diamond = identifying relationship, double ellipse = multivalued attribute, dashed ellipse = derived attribute.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A weak entity set is drawn as a double rectangle — true.
  2. (B) The identifying relationship is drawn as a double diamond — true.
  3. (C) A multivalued attribute uses a double ellipse, not a double outline on a set or a relationship.
  4. (D) A derived attribute uses a dashed ellipse.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) uses a double ellipse, which is a different shape.
  • (D) uses a dashed ellipse.

Common misconception. The double ellipse for a multivalued attribute is confused with the double rectangle for a weak entity set. Both are “double”, but the shape differs and so does the meaning.

Takeaway. Remember the pairing shape-to-meaning: double rectangle / double diamond belong together as the weak-entity pair; double ellipse is an attribute-level symbol.

DB-U1-Q04NATmedium

Entity set E owns weak entity set W through an identifying relationship R. E has 4 entities, R permits at most 3 W-entities per E-entity, and every E-entity participates totally in R. What is the maximum possible number of entities in W?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A weak entity set's cardinality is bounded by the owner's cardinality times the per-owner limit. Total participation of the owner gives a lower bound, the per-owner limit gives the upper bound.

Step by step.

  1. Every E-entity must own at least one W-entity (total participation), so |W| ≥ 4.
  2. Each E-entity may own at most 3 W-entities, so |W| ≤ 4 × 3 = 12.
  3. The upper bound 12 is attained when all 4 E-entities own the full 3 W-entities each.
  4. So the maximum is 12.

Answer: 12.

Common misconception. Adding the two numbers instead of multiplying, or forgetting that a weak entity is owned by exactly one owner — which is what makes the count a product rather than a sum.

Takeaway. For an identifying relationship the owner side is one-to-many, so multiply the owner count by the per-owner limit; the owner's participation decides the minimum, the limit decides the maximum.

DB-U1-Q05MCQhard

In ER modelling, aggregation is used to

  1. combine several attributes of one entity set into a single composite attribute.
  2. treat a relationship set together with its participating entity sets as one higher-level entity set that can then take part in another relationship.
  3. replace a weak entity set by a strong entity set.
  4. convert a ternary relationship into three binary relationships.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Aggregation is the ER construct that removes a limitation of plain ER diagrams: a relationship set cannot participate in another relationship. Aggregating a relationship plus its entity sets yields a single abstract entity that can.

Step by step.

  1. Plain ER forbids a relationship set from being an endpoint of another relationship.
  2. Example: a project is done by employees using machines. “Uses” relates the (employee, project) pair to a machine, not an employee to a machine directly.
  3. Aggregation groups Employee–Works_On–Project into one entity set and lets that set participate in Uses.
  4. So aggregation abstracts a relationship together with its participants into a higher-level entity set.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a composite attribute, drawn as an ellipse with sub-ellipses.
  • (C) is not an ER operation at all; a weak entity set stays weak unless the schema is redesigned.
  • (D) is a modelling decision about arity, not aggregation.

Common misconception. Aggregation is confused with generalization (which factors common attributes of entity sets into a superclass) and with the composite-attribute notation. All three “combine” things, but at different levels.

Faster method. If the sentence mentions “a relationship participating in another relationship”, the answer is aggregation.

Takeaway. Aggregation = relationship-as-entity. Generalization = entity-as-subclass. Composite attribute = attribute-of-attribute.

DB-U1-Q06MCQeasy

A multivalued attribute in an ER diagram is represented by

  1. a single ellipse.
  2. a double ellipse.
  3. a dashed ellipse.
  4. a rectangle.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Attribute notation in Chen's model. A multivalued attribute can hold a set of values for one entity, and is drawn as a double ellipse. A derived attribute is drawn with a dashed ellipse; a key attribute has its name underlined.

Step by step.

  1. Multivalued means several values per entity, e.g. phone numbers of a person.
  2. The extra value needs an extra outline, so the symbol is the double ellipse.
  3. A derived attribute (computed from others, e.g. age from date of birth) uses a dashed ellipse instead.

Answer: Option (B) — a double ellipse.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A single ellipse is a simple, single-valued attribute.
  • (C) A dashed ellipse is a derived attribute.
  • (D) A rectangle is an entity set.

Common misconception. The double ellipse is confused with the double rectangle of a weak entity set. Note that the doubling pattern is applied to the base shape: rectangle for sets, ellipse for attributes.

Takeaway. Learn the four attribute shapes together: single ellipse (simple), double ellipse (multivalued), dashed ellipse (derived), underlined name (key).

DB-U1-Q26MCQeasy

In Chen notation, a relationship set of degree three is drawn as

  1. a single diamond joined by three lines, one to each of the three participating entity sets.
  2. three diamonds, each joined to two entity sets.
  3. a double diamond joined to three entity sets.
  4. a rectangle joined to three diamonds.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The degree of a relationship set is the number of entity sets that take part in it. Chen notation draws one relationship as one diamond, with one line per participating entity set — so degree three is one diamond with three lines.

Step by step.

  1. Count the entity sets that take part: three.
  2. Draw one diamond — the relationship itself is a single object, not one object per pair.
  3. Draw one connecting line from the diamond to each of the three entity sets.
  4. Label each line with that entity set's cardinality contribution if the diagram shows it.

Answer: Option (A) — one diamond with three lines.

Common misconception. Reading a ternary relationship as three binary diamonds. Three binary relationships record three independent pairwise facts and cannot express the single triple fact that a ternary relationship records.

DB-U1-Q27MCQmedium

Entity sets A (m entities) and B (n entities), with m, n ≥ 1, take part in a many-to-many relationship R, and both participations are total. The number of tuples in the relation that represents R lies

  1. exactly at m · n.
  2. between max(m, n) and m · n, inclusive.
  3. between m + n and m · n, inclusive.
  4. exactly at m + n.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two independent constraints bound a many-to-many relationship: the ratio gives the upper bound and total participation gives the lower bound.

Step by step.

  1. Upper bound: each of the m A-entities may pair with each of the n B-entities, so at most m · n distinct pairs.
  2. Lower bound from A's side: total participation means every A-entity appears in at least one pair, so at least m pairs.
  3. Lower bound from B's side: every B-entity appears in at least one pair, so at least n pairs.
  4. Pairs are distinct tuples, so the count is at least the larger of the two bounds, i.e. at least max(m, n). Both bounds are attainable.
  5. Hence max(m, n) ≤ |R| ≤ m · n.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Adding the cardinalities (m + n) or assuming the maximum is forced. The maximum m · n needs every A-entity to pair with every B-entity, which the m:n ratio permits but does not require.

DB-U1-Q28MSQmedium

W is a weak entity set owned by entity set E through the identifying relationship R. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. W has a partial key (discriminator) that distinguishes W-entities owned by the same E-entity.
  2. The participation of W in R is total.
  3. The primary key of the relation built from W is the primary key of E together with W's partial key.
  4. A W-entity may exist without being related to any E-entity.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A weak entity set has no key of its own. It is identified by its owner, and its existence is dependent on that owner — existence dependency is exactly what 'weak' means.

Step by step.

  1. Because W has no key of its own, it carries a partial key (drawn with a dashed underline) that only has to be unique within one owner's set of W-entities — so (A) is true.
  2. A weak entity cannot be identified without its owner, so it must always take part in the identifying relationship — so (B) is true.
  3. The identifying relationship is one-to-many from E to W, so the owner's key together with the partial key is unique over all of W — so (C) is true.
  4. (D) contradicts existence dependency: an owner-less weak entity has no identity at all — so (D) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Thinking the partial key alone identifies a weak entity. It does not: two different owners may each own a W-entity with the same partial-key value, which is why the owner's key must be prefixed.

DB-U1-Q29NAThard

An ER schema contains entity sets A, B, C, D; a weak entity set W owned by A; a multivalued attribute phone on B; a 1:N relationship from A to C; a many-to-many relationship between B and D; and a ternary relationship among A, B and C.
Applying the standard ER-to-relational mapping — one relation per strong or weak entity set, a separate relation per multivalued attribute, a 1:N relationship absorbed into the relation on its many side, and a separate relation per m:n or n-ary relationship — how many relations does the schema produce?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The ER-to-relational mapping is a fixed recipe; the question is which constructs cost a relation and which are absorbed into an existing one.

Step by step.

  1. Strong entity sets A, B, C, D → 4 relations.
  2. Weak entity set W → 1 more relation (it becomes a relation whose key is A's key plus W's partial key) → 5.
  3. Multivalued attribute phone → 1 more relation (owner key + phone value) → 6.
  4. 1:N relationship A → C → 0 new relations; it is absorbed as a foreign key in C → still 6.
  5. m:n relationship B–D → 1 more relation → 7.
  6. Ternary relationship among A, B, C → 1 more relation → 8.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Charging a relation for the 1:N relationship, or forgetting that a weak entity set still gets its own relation. The 1:N side is the free one; the weak entity set is not.

Takeaway. Only relationships whose cardinality cannot be carried by a foreign key — m:n and n-ary — need a relation of their own.

DB-U1-Q30MCQhard

Which one of the following statements about a ternary relationship R among entity sets A, B and C is TRUE?

  1. R is always equivalent to three binary relationships among A, B and C.
  2. R can record a combination of an A-entity, a B-entity and a C-entity that no collection of binary relationships can record.
  3. R must be many-to-many in all three directions.
  4. R requires every entity to take part exactly once.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A relationship of degree three is an association among three entities simultaneously, not three associations among pairs.

Step by step.

  1. A ternary relationship instance is a triple (a, b, c).
  2. Three binary relationships would instead store (a, b), (b, c) and (a, c) separately. From those three pairs you cannot tell which triples actually occurred: the pairs (a1, b1), (b1, c1), (a1, c1) are consistent with both (a1, b1, c1) and with (a1, b1, c2), (a1, b2, c1), …
  3. So the ternary relationship carries information the binary decomposition loses — (B) is true and (A) is false.
  4. Nothing forces the ratio to be m:n in every direction, and nothing forces single participation — (C) and (D) are false.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming a ternary relationship is just 'three binary ones glued together'. It is not, and that is precisely why the ER-to-relational mapping gives an n-ary relationship its own relation.

DB-U1-Q31MCQeasy

Entity set A has a one-to-many relationship R with entity set B — that is, one A-entity is related to many B-entities. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. Each B-entity is related to at most one A-entity.
  2. Each A-entity is related to at most one B-entity.
  3. Each A-entity must be related to at least one B-entity.
  4. Each B-entity must be related to at least one A-entity.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cardinality ratio constrains how many entities on one side may be associated with an entity on the other. It says nothing about whether an entity must be associated at all — that is participation, a separate constraint.

Step by step.

  1. 'One A to many B' means the function runs from B to A: each B-entity picks at most one A-entity.
  2. So (A) is true.
  3. The same ratio allows an A-entity to have any number of B-entities, including zero, so (B) is false.
  4. (C) and (D) assert total participation, which the question never states. Both are false.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading 'one-to-many from A to B' as 'one B per A'. The 'one' side is the side that is unique in the other direction: one A per B.

relational-algebra-select-project-join-division · 16
DB-U1-Q13MCQeasy

In relational algebra, the division operator R ÷ S is used to express

  1. universal quantification — the “for all” condition.
  2. aggregation of a numeric attribute over groups.
  3. an outer join that preserves unmatched tuples.
  4. elimination of duplicates from a bag.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Division is the relational-algebra counterpart of universal quantification. R ÷ S keeps those A-values of R(A, B) that are paired in R with every B-value appearing in S.

Step by step.

  1. Write R(A, B) and S(B). R ÷ S = { a | for every b ∈ S, (a, b) ∈ R }.
  2. The “for every b ∈ S” is exactly a ∀ condition, so division expresses queries such as “students who took all courses”.
  3. It is not primitive: R ÷ S = π_A(R) − π_A( (π_A(R) × S) − R ).

Answer: Option (A) — universal quantification.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Aggregation is not an operator of the basic algebra at all; it is added as γ in extended algebra.
  • (C) Outer joins are extensions of ⋈, written ⟕ / ⟖ / ⟗.
  • (D) Duplicate elimination is implicit in every algebra operator, not the job of ÷.

Common misconception. Division is mistaken for ordinary arithmetic division or for a join. It answers “for all”, not “how many”.

Takeaway. Read division as a nested quantifier: keep a only if nothing in S is missing from a's row.

DB-U1-Q14MCQmedium

Let R(A, B) = {(1, 10), (2, 20), (3, 10)} and S(B, C) = {(10, p), (20, q), (30, r)}. How many tuples are there in R ⋈ S (natural join)?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A natural join equates the common attribute B and concatenates matching pairs. Its size is Σ_b r_b · s_b over the common values, where r_b and s_b are the multiplicities of b in R and S.

Step by step.

  1. Common attribute is B.
  2. B = 10 appears once in R and once in S → 1 × 1 = 1 tuple: (1, 10, p).
  3. B = 20 appears once in R and once in S → 1 tuple: (2, 20, q).
  4. B = 30 appears only in S → contributes nothing; B = 10 also occurs twice in R, so (3, 10, p) is a second tuple for B = 10.
  5. Total = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 tuples.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 tuples.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) counts distinct B values instead of tuples.
  • (C) and (D) invent matches for B = 30, which is absent from R.

Common misconception. Students assume one output tuple per distinct value of B (giving 2), forgetting that repeated B values in R each match. The join is a cross product restricted to equal B.

Takeaway. Count multiplicities: for each common value b, multiply its count in R by its count in S and sum. Here 1·1 + 1·1 = 2 for B = 10 and B = 20, plus the second B = 10 row, giving 3.

DB-U1-Q15MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about relational algebra operators are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Selection σ filters tuples and projection π filters attributes.
  2. Projection π removes duplicate tuples from its result.
  3. R − S and S − R are always equal.
  4. R ∪ S is defined only when R and S are union-compatible.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Relational algebra is set-based, so every operator produces a relation, i.e. a set with no duplicate tuples, and the binary set operators require matching schemas.

Step by step.

  1. (A) σ keeps rows satisfying a predicate, π keeps a chosen list of columns — true.
  2. (B) Because the result is a relation (a set), π collapses duplicates — true.
  3. (C) Set difference is not symmetric: R − S keeps R's tuples absent from S, which is a different set from S − R.
  4. (D) Union requires the same number of attributes with compatible domains in the same order — true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) fails whenever the two relations are not identical: {1} − {2} = {1} but {2} − {1} = {2}.

Common misconception. The duplicate elimination of π is the usual surprise: SQL's SELECT does not remove duplicates, but relational algebra's π does. Mixing the two semantics is a standard error.

Takeaway. Remember: algebra = sets, SQL = bags. Every algebra operator, including π, returns a set.

DB-U1-Q16NATmedium

Let R(A, B) = {(a₁, b₁), (a₁, b₂), (a₂, b₁), (a₃, b₁), (a₃, b₂), (a₄, b₃)} and S(B) = {b₁, b₂}. How many tuples does R ÷ S contain?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. R ÷ S keeps an A-value only if it is paired with every B-value of S in R. Check each A-value against the full set {b₁, b₂}.

Step by step.

  1. a₁ is paired with b₁ and with b₂ → qualifies.
  2. a₂ is paired only with b₁ → does not qualify.
  3. a₃ is paired with b₁ and with b₂ → qualifies.
  4. a₄ is paired only with b₃, which is not even in S → does not qualify.
  5. So R ÷ S = {a₁, a₃}, two tuples.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Requiring only “at least one” match, which would keep a₂ and a₄ as well. Division demands every value of S, not any one of them.

Takeaway. Group R by A and check set-equality against S. Here a₁ and a₃ both map to exactly {b₁, b₂}.

DB-U1-Q17MCQhard

Consider the schema Doctor(did, dname, dage) and Patient(pid, rdid, pname, page), where rdid is a foreign key referencing did. Assume every doctor has at least one patient. Consider the relational algebra query
π_did(Doctor) − π_did( Doctor ⋈(did = rdid) ∧ (dage ≤ page) Patient )
The query evaluates to the set of dids of doctors whose age is greater than that of

  1. some patient.
  2. all patients.
  3. some of his/her patients.
  4. all of his/her patients.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read the query as a set difference: start from all doctors, then remove the doctors that have a patient whose age is at least the doctor's. What survives is exactly the doctors for whom no such patient exists.

Step by step.

  1. Doctor ⋈(did = rdid) ∧ (dage ≤ page) Patient keeps pairs where the doctor's age is ≤ some patient's age, i.e. the doctor is not older than that patient.
  2. Projecting on did gives the doctors having at least one patient who is at least as old as they are.
  3. Subtracting this from π_did(Doctor) leaves the doctors who have no such patient.
  4. “No patient is at least as old as the doctor” means the doctor is strictly older than every patient — all of his/her patients.
  5. The assumption that every doctor has at least one patient matters: without it, a doctor with no patients would also survive, and the query would not mean “older than all”.

Answer: Option (D) — all of his/her patients.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) describe the set that is removed, not the set that survives.
  • (B) would require a ∀ condition expressed with division, not a single join.

Common misconception. Answering “some patient” by stopping at the join instead of completing the difference, or reading the join's ≤ as if it removed patients rather than doctors.

Faster method. Any “−” over a join that encodes “exists a violating tuple” is the standard pattern for a universal statement: removing the violators leaves the ones that satisfy “for all”.

Takeaway. Translate each operator in turn: ⋈ with a condition = “there exists”, π = “the did of”, − = “but not”. Composing them gives “doctors for whom there is no patient at least as old”, which is “older than all”.

DB-U1-Q18MCQeasy

The relational algebra operator that returns a relation containing only a chosen subset of the attributes of its input is

  1. σ (select)
  2. π (projection)
  3. ρ (rename)
  4. ⋈ (join)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The six primitive operators act on different aspects of a relation: σ on rows, π on columns, ρ on names, and ×, ∪, − on whole relations.

Step by step.

  1. σcondition(R) keeps the rows that satisfy the condition, leaving the schema unchanged.
  2. πattr list(R) keeps the listed columns and discards the rest, leaving the rows filtered by duplicate elimination.
  3. ρ renames a relation or its attributes; ⋈ combines two relations on a condition.

Answer: Option (B) — π (projection).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) selects rows, not columns.
  • (C) only changes names.
  • (D) combines two relations.

Common misconception. σ and π are swapped. A quick check: σ for select rows (horizontal cut), π for project columns (vertical cut).

Takeaway. Picture the table: a horizontal cut removes rows (σ), a vertical cut removes columns (π).

DB-U1-Q19MSQmedium

Let R(A, B) and S(A, B) be union-compatible relations. Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. R ∪ S contains every tuple that appears in R or in S or in both, with duplicates removed.
  2. R ∩ S can be written as R − (R − S).
  3. R − S = S − R for all R and S.
  4. R × S contains |R| + |S| tuples.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Set semantics. Union, intersection and difference behave as ordinary set operations once the schemas match; the Cartesian product multiplies cardinalities.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of set union; since the result is a relation, duplicates collapse.
  2. (B) R − S removes from R everything shared with S; removing those again from R leaves exactly the common part, so R − (R − S) = R ∩ S.
  3. (C) fails for any unequal pair, e.g. R = {t₁}, S = {t₂}.
  4. (D) is wrong: |R × S| = |R| · |S|.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) Difference is directional.
  • (D) The Cartesian product multiplies, it does not add.

Common misconception. Intersection is thought to need its own operator. It does not: ∩ is derivable from − (and from ⋈). Similarly ∪ is derivable from − and ×, which is why only six operators are primitive.

Takeaway. Memorise the derivations: R ∩ S = R − (R − S), and |R × S| = |R| × |S|.

DB-U1-Q20NAThard

Relation R(A, B, C) has 10 tuples and relation S(C, D) has 8 tuples, with C the only common attribute. The tuples of R use exactly 4 distinct values of C, and the tuples of S use exactly 5 distinct values of C. What is the maximum possible number of tuples in R ⋈ S?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The size of a natural join is Σc r_c · s_c over the common attribute, where r_c and s_c are the multiplicities of c in R and S. Under fixed totals the sum of products is largest when the two distributions are aligned and as concentrated as the distinct-value counts allow.

Step by step.

  1. Let r_c ≥ 1 for 4 values of C with Σ r_c = 10, and s_c ≥ 1 for 5 values of C with Σ s_c = 8. Then |R ⋈ S| = Σ_c r_c · s_c.
  2. Because there are 4 and 5 distinct values, at least 1 tuple must be spent on each of them. That leaves 10 − 4 = 6 extra R-tuples and 8 − 5 = 3 extra S-tuples to concentrate on one shared value c₀.
  3. Put all the extras on c₀: r = (7, 1, 1, 1) and s = (4, 1, 1, 1, 1).
  4. Matching the largest counts together gives 7 × 4 = 28 on c₀, and the three remaining shared values contribute 1 × 1 each: 28 + 3 = 31.
  5. No other distribution does better — by the rearrangement inequality the best alignment pairs the largest r_c with the largest s_c, and concentrating mass maximises the largest product.

Answer: 31.

Common misconception. Multiplying the totals, 10 × 8 = 80, or the distinct counts, 4 × 5 = 20. Both ignore the hard constraint that each of the distinct values must carry at least one tuple.

Takeaway. Subtract the compulsory one tuple per distinct value first; only the leftovers can be concentrated. Here 6 and 3 leftovers give 7 × 4 = 28 plus 3 forced unit products.

DB-U1-Q38MCQeasy

Relation R has 4 tuples and 3 attributes; relation S has 5 tuples and 2 attributes. They share exactly one attribute B, which is a key of R and a foreign key in S that is never NULL. Every tuple of S matches exactly one tuple of R. The number of tuples in R ⋈ S is

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 9
  4. 20
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A natural join pairs tuples that agree on the common attribute. When the common attribute is a key on one side, each tuple on the other side has at most one partner.

Step by step.

  1. Each of the 5 tuples of S has a B-value that is a key value of R, so it matches exactly one R-tuple.
  2. That produces exactly one joined tuple per S-tuple: 5.
  3. Some R-tuples may match nothing and simply do not appear — the join is not an outer join, so R's 4 tuples do not add to the count.

Answer: Option (B) — 5.

Common misconception. Multiplying 4 × 5 = 20, which is the size of the Cartesian product and only an upper bound for a join. Because B is a key of R, no S-tuple can have two partners.

DB-U1-Q39MCQmedium

For union-compatible relations R and S, which expression is equivalent to R ∩ S?

  1. R − (R − S)
  2. (R − S) − S
  3. (R ∪ S) − R
  4. πall(R) − πall(S)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Intersection is not primitive in the core algebra; it is defined from set difference.

Step by step.

  1. R − S keeps the tuples of R that are not in S.
  2. Removing those from R leaves exactly the tuples of R that are in S: R − (R − S) = R ∩ S.
  3. (B) simplifies to R − S; (C) is S − R; (D) is just R − S again.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Choosing (R ∪ S) − R, which looks like it 'removes the overlap' but actually returns S − R — the opposite set.

DB-U1-Q40MSQeasy

Consider Works(eid, pid) and Projects(pid). Which expressions return exactly the eids of employees who work on every project? (Select all that apply.)

  1. πeid(Works) ÷ πpid(Projects)
  2. πeid(Works) − πeid((πeid(Works) × πpid(Projects)) − Works)
  3. πeid(Works) − πeid(πeid(Works) × πpid(Projects))
  4. πeid(Works ⋈ Projects)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 'For all' is division. Equivalently, it is 'difference of difference': take everyone, subtract those who miss at least one project.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the division operator applied directly: it returns the eids whose set of projects contains the whole of πpid(Projects) — true.
  2. (B) builds the set of missing pairs — every (eid, pid) combination that is not in Works — projects it to eid, and subtracts it from all employees. What survives misses no project — true.
  3. (C) subtracts all employees who work on anything at all, which returns only employees with no project — false.
  4. (D) returns every employee who works on at least one project — that is 'exists', not 'for all' — false.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Reading (D) as the answer. A join with the projects relation answers 'works on some project'; the universal quantifier needs division or the double difference.

DB-U1-Q41NATmedium

Let R(A) = {1, 2, 3} and S(A) = {2, 3, 4}. The number of tuples in R ⋈R.A < S.A S is

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A theta join pairs every tuple of one relation with every tuple of the other that satisfies the condition — here a strict inequality, not an equality on a common attribute.

Step by step.

  1. List the pairs (r, s) with r ∈ R, s ∈ S and r < s.
  2. r = 1: s = 2, 3, 4 → 3 pairs.
  3. r = 2: s = 3, 4 → 2 pairs.
  4. r = 3: s = 4 → 1 pair.
  5. Total 3 + 2 + 1 = 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Joining on equality of A, which would give only the pairs (2, 2) and (3, 3). The condition is <, and both relations are kept whole — the attribute is not merged.

DB-U1-Q42MCQhard

Let S(B) be a relation with zero tuples. For a relation R(A, B), the division R ÷ S is

  1. the empty relation.
  2. πA(R).
  3. undefined, because division needs a non-empty divisor.
  4. R.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Division asks: which A-values are paired with every B-value in S? With an empty S the requirement is vacuously satisfied by every value.

Step by step.

  1. By definition, R ÷ S = { a | for every b ∈ S, (a, b) ∈ R }.
  2. When S = ∅ there is no b to check, so the condition holds for every a that appears in R.
  3. The values of a that appear in R are exactly πA(R), so R ÷ ∅ = πA(R).
  4. Confirm with the derived formula: πA(R) − πA((πA(R) × ∅) − R) = πA(R) − πA(∅) = πA(R).

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Answering 'the empty relation' by pattern-matching 'empty input, empty output'. Universally quantified conditions are vacuously true over an empty range, so the result is the largest possible set, not the smallest.

Takeaway. This vacuous-truth case is the same reason a universal statement over an empty domain is true, and it is a favourite GATE trap.

DB-U1-Q43MCQeasy

Which of the following is not a unary relational algebra operator?

  1. σ (selection)
  2. π (projection)
  3. ⋈ (natural join)
  4. ρ (rename)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Arity of an operator = number of relations it consumes. Selection, projection and rename each transform one relation; a join combines two.

Step by step.

  1. σcond(R) filters the tuples of a single relation — unary.
  2. πattrs(R) keeps a subset of the attributes of a single relation — unary.
  3. ρ renames a relation or its attributes — unary.
  4. R ⋈ S needs both R and S — it is binary, so it is the answer.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Classifying the join as unary because 'it acts on the attributes in common'. It consumes two relations, which is what arity measures.

DB-U1-Q44MSQmedium

Let R(A, B) and S(B, C), where B is a key of R and a foreign key in S that is never NULL. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. R ⋈ S has exactly as many tuples as S.
  2. R ⋈ S has attributes A, B and C.
  3. R ⋈ S can contain more tuples than |R| × |S|.
  4. The natural join is associative.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A natural join equates the common attributes and keeps one copy of each. When the common attribute is a key on one side, the join is a lookup, not a product.

Step by step.

  1. Every tuple of S has a non-NULL B-value, and referential integrity guarantees a matching R-tuple. Since B is a key of R, that partner is unique — one joined tuple per S-tuple, so the counts are equal — (A) is true.
  2. The common attribute appears once, and the remaining attributes of both sides are retained — (B) is true.
  3. A join can never produce more than the Cartesian product |R| × |S|; it selects a subset of those pairs — (C) is false.
  4. (R ⋈ S) ⋈ T = R ⋈ (S ⋈ T) for natural joins, and it is commutative too — (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing a natural join can blow up the row count. Duplication on the common attribute is what multiplies rows, and a key on one side makes that impossible.

DB-U1-Q45NATmedium

Relations R(A, B) and S(A, B) are union-compatible, with |R| = 12, |S| = 9 and |R − S| = 5. The number of tuples in R ∩ S is

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. R splits into two disjoint parts: the tuples it shares with S, and the tuples it does not.

Step by step.

  1. R = (R − S) ∪ (R ∩ S), and the two parts are disjoint.
  2. So |R| = |R − S| + |R ∩ S|.
  3. Substituting: 12 = 5 + |R ∩ S|, hence |R ∩ S| = 7.

Answer: 7

Common misconception. Using |R ∪ S| instead: that would give 12 + 9 − 7 = 14, a different quantity. The stated set is the difference, not the union.

relational-model-constraints · 12
DB-U1-Q07MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) has primary key (A, B) and a foreign key C that references S(C). Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. A and B cannot be NULL, but C may be NULL.
  2. C cannot be NULL, but A may be NULL.
  3. Any of A, B, C may be NULL.
  4. A alone must be unique.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Entity integrity says no attribute of a primary key may be NULL; referential integrity says a foreign key must either be NULL or match an existing referenced value. The two rules apply to different attributes here, so both can hold at once.

Step by step.

  1. Entity integrity: every attribute of the primary key (A, B) is NOT NULL. So A and B cannot be NULL.
  2. Referential integrity constrains C: each C-value must be NULL or equal some S.C value.
  3. Nothing forbids C from being NULL, because C is not part of R's primary key.
  4. Hence (A, B) are mandatory, C is optional.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the two rules.
  • (C) contradicts entity integrity.
  • (D) A is only part of the key; A alone may repeat.

Common misconception. Students assume a foreign key can never be NULL. It can, provided the FK attributes are not part of the primary key — that is exactly how “optional” relationships are modelled.

Takeaway. Check whether the FK overlaps the primary key. No overlap → NULL is allowed. Overlap → entity integrity forbids NULL.

DB-U1-Q08MCQmedium

Let R(A, B, C, D) satisfy the functional dependencies {AB → C, C → D, D → A}. How many candidate keys does R have?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A candidate key is a minimal set of attributes whose closure is the whole relation. Compute closures of each attribute alone, then of the promising pairs.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺ = A; B⁺ = B; C⁺ = C D A = ACD (using C → D and D → A); D⁺ = D A = AD. None is the whole relation.
  2. AB⁺ = A B C D = ABCD. AB is a key, and neither A nor B alone is, so AB is a candidate key.
  3. BC⁺ = B C D A = ABCD. BC is minimal (B⁺, C⁺ both fail), so BC is a candidate key.
  4. BD⁺ = B D A = ABD, and AB → C applies, so BD⁺ = ABCD. BD is minimal, so BD is a candidate key.
  5. AC⁺ = ACD, AD⁺ = AD, CD⁺ = ACD — none is the whole relation. The answer is 3.

Answer: Option (C) — 3 candidate keys (AB, BC, BD).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) misses BC and BD.
  • (B) misses BD.
  • (D) over-counts; AC, AD and CD are not keys.

Common misconception. Stopping at AB, or counting AB and BA as different keys. Also easy to miss BD, which needs the two-step closure D → A followed by AB → C.

Faster method. Here C and D never appear in a closure that contains B, so B is in every key. That forces you to test AB, BC and BD — exactly three.

Takeaway. Write every single-attribute closure first. Any attribute absent from all of them must appear in every candidate key; then test the minimal pairs containing it.

DB-U1-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about keys of a relation are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every relation has at least one candidate key.
  2. A superkey that has no proper subset which is also a superkey is a candidate key.
  3. A relation can have at most one candidate key.
  4. A primary key attribute may contain NULL values.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Keys are defined through functional determination on the whole set of attributes. The set of all attributes of a relation is always a superkey, so minimal superkeys always exist.

Step by step.

  1. The full attribute set functionally determines itself, so it is a superkey; shrinking it until it is minimal yields a candidate key. Hence at least one candidate key always exists.
  2. A candidate key is precisely a minimal superkey — that is the definition given in (B).
  3. A relation may have several candidate keys; one of them is chosen as the primary key.
  4. Entity integrity forbids NULL in any primary key attribute.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is false — a relation may have several candidate keys, and exactly one is picked as primary.
  • (D) is false — entity integrity requires primary key attributes to be NOT NULL.

Common misconception. The word “candidate” is read as “optional”, suggesting there might be none. Every relation has at least one; what varies is how many.

Takeaway. Two facts to keep: the full attribute set is always a superkey (so keys exist), and minimality is what separates candidate keys from superkeys.

DB-U1-Q10NATeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies the functional dependency A → B and no other non-trivial dependency. How many superkeys does R have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A superkey is any attribute set whose closure is all of R. With only A → B, the closure of a set S is S ∪ {B} if A ∈ S, and S otherwise; a superkey must therefore contain A and C.

Step by step.

  1. The candidate key: A⁺ = AB, so A alone is not a key; AC⁺ = ABC. Hence AC is the only candidate key.
  2. Every superkey must contain the candidate key AC.
  3. The only attributes available to add are the remaining ones, here just B, giving the supersets AC and ABC.
  4. So R has exactly 2 superkeys.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Counting only AC and forgetting that a superkey may contain extra attributes, or counting A and C separately as keys.

Takeaway. Find the candidate key first; every superkey is then a superset of it, so the count is 2 raised to the number of leftover attributes.

DB-U1-Q11MCQhard

Let R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfy the functional dependencies {A → BC, CD → E, B → D, E → A}. How many candidate keys does R have?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Candidate keys are minimal sets with full closure. Because the dependencies form a cycle (A → B → D → E → A together with C on one edge), the relation has several minimal keys and every attribute belongs to at least one of them.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺: A → BC gives B, C; B → D gives D; CD → E gives E. So A⁺ = ABCDE. A is a candidate key.
  2. E⁺: E → A, then A → BC, then B → D, then CD → E. So E⁺ = ABCDE. E is a candidate key.
  3. BC⁺: B → D, CD → E, E → A, A → BC. So BC⁺ = ABCDE, and B⁺ = BD, C⁺ = C — BC is minimal.
  4. CD⁺: CD → E, E → A, A → BC, B → D. So CD⁺ = ABCDE, and C⁺ = C, D⁺ = D — CD is minimal.
  5. B⁺ = BD, C⁺ = C, D⁺ = D, BD⁺ = BD — none is the whole relation, and every other set contains one of A, E, BC, CD. So there are exactly 4 candidate keys.

Answer: Option (C) — 4 candidate keys (A, E, BC, CD).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) misses BC and CD.
  • (B) misses one of E, BC, CD.
  • (D) over-counts: BD⁺ = BD is not a key.

Common misconception. Missing E or CD because the cycle makes closure look “too easy”. The dependency cycle A → B → D → E → A means closure from any member reaches all the others, so several attributes become keys in their own right.

Faster method. Any attribute whose single-attribute closure is the whole relation is a key by itself; here that is true of A and E, and the C-bearing pairs add two more.

Takeaway. When FDs form a cycle, compute closure from each single attribute first — every attribute that closes to ABCDE is already a key and needs no partner.

DB-U1-Q12MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B) has A as its primary key and B as an ordinary nullable attribute. Which one of the following tuples, if inserted into R, violates a constraint implied by this schema?

  1. (NULL, 5)
  2. (1, NULL)
  3. (1, 5)
  4. (2, NULL)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Entity integrity: no component of a primary key may be NULL. B is not part of the key, so NULL is allowed there and only uniqueness would ever reject it — and NULLs do not clash with each other under this rule.

Step by step.

  1. A is the primary key, so every tuple must supply a non-NULL value for A.
  2. (NULL, 5) supplies no key value, so it violates entity integrity.
  3. (1, NULL) and (2, NULL) are fine: the key values 1 and 2 are present, B is nullable.
  4. (1, 5) is fine as long as 1 is not already present.

Answer: Option (A) — (NULL, 5).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (D) have a valid key value and a NULL in a nullable column.
  • (C) has a valid key value and no NULL.

Common misconception. Students reject (1, NULL) thinking no attribute may be NULL. Nullability is a schema property; only primary-key attributes are forced to be non-NULL.

Takeaway. Look at where NULL sits. Inside the primary key → rejected. Outside it → allowed unless the column is declared NOT NULL.

DB-U1-Q32MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) has exactly one candidate key, {A}. The number of superkeys of R is

  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 15
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A superkey is any attribute set that contains a candidate key. With a single candidate key {A}, a set is a superkey exactly when it contains A.

Step by step.

  1. Any superkey must contain A — otherwise it cannot determine the whole tuple.
  2. Each of the remaining three attributes B, C, D is then free to be in or out.
  3. That gives 2³ = 8 superkeys.
  4. Check: {A}, {AB}, {AC}, {AD}, {ABC}, {ABD}, {ACD}, {ABCD} — eight.

Answer: Option (B) — 8.

Common misconception. Counting 2⁴ = 16 by letting A vary too. A is compulsory in every superkey, so only the other three attributes are free.

Takeaway. With one candidate key of size k in a relation of n attributes, the superkey count is 2^(n − k).

DB-U1-Q33MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies the functional dependencies F = {A → B, BC → D, D → E, E → C}. The number of candidate keys of R is

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A candidate key is a minimal attribute set whose closure is all five attributes. Enumerate closures, then drop any set that contains a smaller key.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺ = {A, B} — BC → D needs C, so nothing further fires. A alone is not a key.
  2. {A, C}⁺: A → B adds B; BC → D adds D; D → E adds E. Closure is all five — {A, C} is a key.
  3. {A, D}⁺: A → B; D → E; E → C; then BC → D. All five — {A, D} is a key.
  4. {A, E}⁺: A → B; E → C; BC → D; D → E. All five — {A, E} is a key.
  5. No set without A closes: from {B, C, D, E} the rules BC → D, D → E, E → C never produce A.
  6. So the candidate keys are {A, C}, {A, D}, {A, E} — three, all minimal.

Answer: Option (C) — 3.

Common misconception. Stopping at the first key found, or counting {A, C, D} as a fourth key. A superset of a key is a superkey, never a candidate key.

DB-U1-Q34MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about functional dependencies are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A → B is trivial exactly when B ⊆ A.
  2. If A → B and B → C, then A → C.
  3. If A → B, then AC → BC for every attribute set C.
  4. If A → B and C → B, then A → C.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Armstrong's axioms: reflexivity, augmentation and transitivity generate every dependency implied by a set, and nothing else does.

Step by step.

  1. Reflexivity: A → B holds automatically when B ⊆ A, because a set always determines its own subsets. Such a dependency is called trivial — (A) is true.
  2. Transitivity: A → B and B → C give A → C — (B) is true.
  3. Augmentation: adding the same attributes to both sides preserves the dependency, so A → B yields AC → BC — (C) is true.
  4. (D) is the converse of transitivity and is not a valid rule. Two different sets may each determine B without determining each other — (D) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating A → B, C → B ⊢ A → C as transitivity. Transitivity needs the same set on the right of one dependency and the left of the next; here the shared attribute is on the right of both.

DB-U1-Q35NATeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) has exactly one candidate key, {A, B}. The number of superkeys of R is

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every superkey must contain the whole candidate key; the attributes outside it are free.

Step by step.

  1. A superkey must contain {A, B}.
  2. The two remaining attributes C, D are each free to be included or excluded.
  3. That gives 2² = 4 superkeys: {AB}, {ABC}, {ABD}, {ABCD}.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Answering 8 by treating the key as size 1, or 16 by letting A and B vary independently. Neither can be dropped.

DB-U1-Q36MCQhard

Relation R(A, B, C) has exactly two candidate keys, {A} and {B}. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. R has exactly 8 superkeys.
  2. {A, B} is a superkey but not a candidate key.
  3. Every attribute of R is prime.
  4. R has exactly two superkeys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Candidate keys are the minimal superkeys. Any larger set containing a key is still a superkey, but minimality is what a candidate key adds.

Step by step.

  1. {A, B} determines every attribute (it contains the key {A}), so it is a superkey.
  2. It is not minimal, because the proper subset {A} is already a superkey. So it is not a candidate key — (B) is true.
  3. Superkeys are the sets containing A or containing B: {A}, {B}, {AB}, {AC}, {BC}, {ABC} — six, so (A) and (D) are both false.
  4. C belongs to no candidate key, so it is non-prime — (C) is false.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Adding 4 + 4 = 8 superkeys by counting the supersets of each key separately. The set {A, B, C} is counted twice that way; inclusion–exclusion gives 4 + 4 − 2 = 6.

DB-U1-Q37MCQeasy

Which one of the following violates entity integrity?

  1. A tuple whose primary-key attribute is NULL.
  2. A tuple whose foreign key is NULL.
  3. A tuple whose non-key attribute is NULL.
  4. Two tuples that agree on a non-key attribute.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Entity integrity: no component of a primary key may be NULL, because the key is what identifies the tuple. Referential integrity, by contrast, is about foreign keys and explicitly allows NULL.

Step by step.

  1. A primary key identifies a tuple; a NULL is 'unknown', and an unknown value cannot identify anything — so (A) violates entity integrity.
  2. A NULL foreign key means 'this tuple refers to nothing', which referential integrity permits — (B) is legal.
  3. Non-key attributes may be NULL unless a NOT NULL constraint says otherwise — (C) is legal.
  4. Only key attributes must be unique; duplicates in a non-key attribute are ordinary data — (D) is legal.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing entity integrity with referential integrity, and so rejecting a NULL foreign key. That is exactly the case referential integrity allows.

tuple-and-domain-relational-calculus · 10
DB-U1-Q21MCQmedium

The tuple relational calculus query { t | t ∈ Student ∧ t[age] > 20 } is equivalent to

  1. π_age(σ_age>20(Student))
  2. σ_age>20(Student)
  3. σ_age>20(π_age(Student))
  4. Student ⋈ σ_age>20(Student)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In tuple calculus the free tuple variable ranges over all tuples of the relation named in the membership clause; a conjunctive condition on its attributes corresponds exactly to selection, and no projection is involved because t carries all attributes.

Step by step.

  1. t ∈ Student fixes the range of t to the tuples of Student.
  2. t[age] > 20 is the selection predicate.
  3. The result keeps whole tuples, so the algebra translation is σ_age>20(Student) with the same schema.
  4. Projection would be needed only if the query returned t[some attributes], not t itself.

Answer: Option (B) — σ_age>20(Student).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) changes the schema to a single column, which the query does not ask for.
  • (C) projects before selecting, which may drop attributes the predicate needs.
  • (D) joins Student with itself; the calculus query has one variable.

Common misconception. Students add a projection because the predicate mentions age. The predicate filters; it does not change the output schema. Only an explicit attribute list on t changes the schema.

Takeaway. Membership clause → which relation. Conditions on attributes → σ. t[list] on the left of the bar → π of that list.

DB-U1-Q22MCQeasy

In tuple relational calculus, the expression { t | ¬(t ∈ R) } is rejected as unsafe because

  1. it is syntactically invalid.
  2. it can denote an infinite relation, since t may take values outside the active domain.
  3. negation is not permitted in relational calculus.
  4. t is a free variable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A calculus expression is safe when every value it can produce comes from the active domain — the set of values actually occurring in the database. { t | ¬(t ∈ R) } ranges over the infinite universe of all possible tuples, so it is not a finite relation and cannot be evaluated.

Step by step.

  1. The comprehension { t | ¬(t ∈ R) } admits every tuple not in R, over every possible attribute domain.
  2. That set is infinite (or at least unbounded), so it is not a relation in the database sense.
  3. Safety requires each answer tuple to be drawn from dom(R) extended by constants in the query.
  4. Negation itself is perfectly legal — only its unrestricted use is the problem.

Answer: Option (B) — it can denote an infinite relation.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The syntax is well formed.
  • (C) Negation is allowed; unsafe negation is not.
  • (D) t is free by design — the free variable names the answer tuples.

Common misconception. Negation is blamed. The calculus allows ¬ freely; what it forbids is a query whose result is not confined to the active domain. { t | t ∈ R ∧ ¬P(t) } is safe, { t | ¬(t ∈ R) } is not.

Takeaway. Ask of every calculus query: which relation bounds the free variable? If nothing does, the query is unsafe.

DB-U1-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about relational calculus are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Tuple relational calculus and domain relational calculus have the same expressive power.
  2. Every relational algebra expression can be written as a safe relational calculus expression.
  3. Relational calculus is procedural: it states how the answer is to be computed.
  4. The safety condition confines a query's values to the active domain of the database.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Relational algebra and the two calculi are all relationally complete: the algebra is procedural, the calculi are declarative, and all three express exactly the same class of queries once safety is imposed.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRC and DRC are inter-translatable, so neither is more expressive — true.
  2. (B) Codd's theorem gives translations both ways between algebra and safe calculus — true.
  3. (C) Calculus says what the answer is (a set of tuples satisfying a formula); the algebra says how. So calculus is declarative, not procedural.
  4. (D) Safety restricts quantifiers and free variables to the active domain, which is what makes the expression finite — true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) reverses the definitions — calculus is declarative, not procedural.

Common misconception. Calculus is assumed to be the “stronger” formalism. In fact it has exactly the same power as the algebra; what it lacks is a fixed evaluation order, which is why SQL compilers translate to algebra.

Takeaway. Two things to keep straight: procedural = algebra, declarative = calculus, equal in power; and safety is the condition that makes calculus finite.

DB-U1-Q24NATmedium

Let R(A, B) = {(1, 5), (2, 5), (2, 7), (3, 5), (4, 9)}. The domain relational calculus query
{ ⟨a⟩ | ∃b (⟨a, b⟩ ∈ R ∧ b = 5) }
returns how many distinct values of a?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A domain-calculus query projects the free variables of its formula. The existential quantifier on b makes b a bound (projected-away) variable, so the answer is the set of a-values that co-occur with b = 5.

Step by step.

  1. The formula requires a pair (a, b) in R with b = 5.
  2. Pairs with b = 5 are (1, 5), (2, 5) and (3, 5).
  3. Their a-values are 1, 2, 3 — three distinct values.
  4. (2, 7) and (4, 9) are excluded because their b is not 5.
  5. In algebra this is π_A(σ_B=5(R)), which has 3 tuples.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting tuples rather than distinct a-values, or being distracted by the repeated a = 2. The result of a calculus query is a set, so a = 2 contributes once.

Takeaway. Free variables = output columns. Here only a is free, so translate to π_A(σ_B=5(R)) and count.

DB-U1-Q25MCQhard

Which one of the following queries cannot be expressed in relational algebra, nor in safe relational calculus?

  1. Names of employees who earn more than their manager.
  2. Names of all employees who are, directly or indirectly, subordinates of a given employee.
  3. Names of employees who work on every project.
  4. Names of employees who work on no project.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Relational algebra and safe calculus are relationally complete but not Turing complete: they can express any finite first-order query over the stored relations, and nothing that needs an unbounded number of iteration steps. Transitive closure is the standard query outside that class.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is a self-join of Employee with itself on manager = eid plus a comparison — first-order, expressible.
  2. (C) is division: π_eid(Works) ÷ π_pid(Project) — expressible.
  3. (D) is a difference: π_eid(Employee) − π_eid(Works) — expressible.
  4. (B) asks for the transitive closure of the “manager of” relation. Its length is unbounded, so no fixed finite expression of the algebra or of first-order calculus can capture it.
  5. This is the classic reason SQL needed recursion (WITH RECURSIVE) to compute closure.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) needs one join and one comparison.
  • (C) is a textbook division query.
  • (D) is a set difference.

Common misconception. “Indirectly” is read as “one more join”. One extra join only reaches depth two; no finite number of joins reaches every depth, which is precisely why closure is not first-order expressible.

Faster method. Whenever a query says “directly or indirectly”, “any number of levels”, or “shortest path”, suspect transitive closure — outside relational algebra.

Takeaway. Distinguish the quantifier patterns. “For all projects” is a bounded ∀ and division handles it; “any number of steps” is an unbounded iteration and needs recursion.

DB-U1-Q46MCQeasy

In the tuple relational calculus formula { t | ∃s ∈ R (t[A] = s[A]) }, the variable t is

  1. bound by the existential quantifier.
  2. free.
  3. quantified over the domain of A.
  4. unsafe.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A variable is free when it is not in the scope of a quantifier. The free variables of a formula are exactly the columns of the result.

Step by step.

  1. s is introduced by ∃s ∈ R, so s is bound.
  2. t is never introduced by a quantifier — it appears on the left of the bar and inside the formula as a free variable.
  3. Free variables supply the output columns, which is why the result is a set of t tuples — so (B) is true.
  4. Quantification over the domain of A is domain calculus syntax; here the range is a relation, so (C) is false. Nothing about safety is violated, so (D) is false.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming the variable on the left of the bar is bound because it 'belongs to' the query. Being on the left of the bar is precisely what makes it free.

DB-U1-Q47MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the domain relational calculus are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A query has the form { ⟨x₁, …, xₙ⟩ | P(x₁, …, xₙ) }.
  2. Its variables range over domain values rather than over whole tuples.
  3. Safety confines the result to values drawn from the active domain.
  4. It is strictly more expressive than relational algebra.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Domain calculus is the value-based counterpart of tuple calculus: the variables are single values, and the result is a set of value-tuples.

Step by step.

  1. The result is a set of value-tuples ⟨x₁, …, xₙ⟩ satisfying P — (A) is true.
  2. Each xᵢ ranges over the domain of an attribute, not over tuples — that is the distinction from tuple calculus — (B) is true.
  3. Safety restricts every variable to the active domain — the set of values actually occurring in the database — which is what makes the result finite — (C) is true.
  4. Domain calculus, tuple calculus and relational algebra are all equivalent in expressive power (Codd's theorem), so (D) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing one calculus is stronger than the other. Both are exactly as strong as relational algebra — and all three fall short of transitive closure.

DB-U1-Q48NATmedium

Let R(A, B) = {(1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 2), (5, 3)}. The number of tuples in { ⟨a₁, a₂⟩ | ∃b (R(a₁, b) ∧ R(a₂, b) ∧ a₁ ≠ a₂) } is

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The shared bound variable b links two occurrences of the same relation — a self-join expressed in calculus. The condition a₁ ≠ a₂ removes the diagonal.

Step by step.

  1. Group the tuples by b.
  2. b = 2: a ∈ {1, 4} → the ordered pairs (1, 4) and (4, 1) → 2.
  3. b = 3: a ∈ {2, 5} → (2, 5) and (5, 2) → 2.
  4. b = 1: a ∈ {3} only → no pair with a₁ ≠ a₂ → 0.
  5. Total 2 + 2 + 0 = 4.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Forgetting that the answer is ordered. ⟨a₁, a₂⟩ and ⟨a₂, a₁⟩ are different tuples, so {1, 4} contributes two, not one. Dropping the a₁ ≠ a₂ condition would add the three diagonal tuples.

DB-U1-Q49MCQmedium

For union-compatible R(A, B) and S(A, B), which tuple calculus expression is equivalent to R − S?

  1. { t | t ∈ R ∧ ¬(t ∈ S) }
  2. { t | ¬(t ∈ R) ∧ t ∈ S }
  3. { t | t ∈ R ∨ t ∈ S }
  4. { t | ¬(t ∈ R ∧ t ∈ S) }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Translation is compositional: ∧ for 'and', ∨ for 'or', ¬ for 'not', and a membership clause for each relation.

Step by step.

  1. 'In R and not in S' translates to t ∈ R ∧ ¬(t ∈ S) — (A).
  2. (B) is S − R, the other difference.
  3. (C) is R ∪ S.
  4. (D) is the complement of the intersection — everything except the shared tuples.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Swapping the operands: t ∈ R ∧ ¬(t ∈ S) and ¬(t ∈ R) ∧ t ∈ S look symmetric but are different sets. Order matters in difference.

DB-U1-Q50MCQhard

Which one of the following tuple calculus expressions is safe?

  1. { t | ¬(t ∈ R) }
  2. { t | t ∈ R ∨ t[A] > 5 }
  3. { t | t ∈ S ∧ ¬(t ∈ R) }
  4. { t | t[A] = 3 }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Safety is a syntactic test: every free variable must be confined by a positive membership clause in a relation, so the result is drawn from the finite active domain.

Step by step.

  1. (A) has a free t under a negation with no relation bounding it — it admits every tuple not in R, an infinite set. Unsafe.
  2. (B) is a disjunction whose second branch, t[A] > 5, is unbounded — a ∨ is safe only if both branches are. Unsafe.
  3. (C) bounds t by the positive clause t ∈ S; the negation then only filters within that finite set. Safe.
  4. (D) constrains one attribute and leaves the rest unconstrained — infinitely many tuples satisfy it. Unsafe.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming any formula with a negation is unsafe, or that a ∨ is safe as long as one branch is bounded. A negated or disjunctive subformula is safe only when something positive in the formula still confines the variable.

Takeaway. The practical rule: a variable is confined if some positive membership t ∈ R appears in the same conjunction. Filtering inside a confined range is always safe.

aggregate-functions-group-by-having · 10
DB-U2-Q11MCQeasy

Using the data below, what does the query return?SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(marks) FROM enroll;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. 8 and 2
  2. 8 and 8
  3. 6 and 6
  4. 8 and 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. COUNT(*) counts rows and never looks at column values, so NULLs are included. COUNT(expr) counts only the rows where expr is not NULL. Every other aggregate (SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX) also ignores NULLs; only COUNT(*) counts them.

Step by step.

  1. enroll holds 8 rows, so COUNT(*) = 8.
  2. Two rows have marks NULL — (2,CS101) and (5,EC201) — so COUNT(marks) = 8 − 2 = 6.
  3. The pair is therefore (8, 6).

Answer: Option (D) — 8 and 6.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would need every row to be graded.
  • (C) would be the answer if COUNT(*) also skipped NULL rows.
  • (A) reports the number of NULL marks instead of the number of non-NULL ones.

Common misconception. Assuming COUNT(marks) and COUNT(*) are the same. The gap between them is precisely the number of NULLs, which is why COUNT(*) - COUNT(col) is a standard idiom for counting missing values.

Takeaway. COUNT(*) counts rows; COUNT(col) counts non-NULL values of col.

DB-U2-Q12MCQeasy

Which statement about WHERE and HAVING is TRUE?

  1. WHERE filters individual rows before grouping; HAVING filters whole groups after aggregation and may contain aggregate functions.
  2. HAVING filters individual rows before grouping; WHERE filters groups after aggregation.
  3. Both clauses may contain aggregate functions and are interchangeable.
  4. WHERE may contain aggregate functions but HAVING may not.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SQL evaluates a grouped query in a fixed order: FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → ORDER BY. Because WHERE runs before the groups exist, it cannot refer to an aggregate; because HAVING runs after grouping, it can.

Step by step.

  1. WHERE sees one row at a time, so its predicates use plain columns.
  2. GROUP BY then collapses rows into groups.
  3. HAVING tests the group, so COUNT(*), SUM(marks) and friends are legal there.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the order of the two clauses.
  • (C) is false: an aggregate in WHERE is a syntax error in every major system.
  • (D) inverts the rule.

Common misconception. Trying to filter on a group with WHERE COUNT(*) > 2. The engine rejects it; the same condition belongs in HAVING. A useful memory hook: HAVING is to groups what WHERE is to rows.

Takeaway. Row filter → WHERE; group filter → HAVING.

DB-U2-Q13MCQmedium

Using the data below, what does the query return (averages shown to one decimal place)?SELECT dept, AVG(cgpa) FROM student GROUP BY dept HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. Three rows: CSE, ECE and MECH
  2. Two rows: CSE 8.8 and ECE 7.5
  3. One row: CSE 8.8
  4. Two rows: CSE 9.1 and ECE 7.8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The query groups by department, computes each group's average, and then keeps only the groups whose COUNT(*) exceeds 1. The HAVING test is applied to the group, so a department with a single student disappears even though its average is perfectly well defined.

Step by step.

  1. Group and count: CSE has 3 students, ECE has 2, MECH has 1.
  2. HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 keeps CSE and ECE and drops MECH.
  3. CSE average = (9.1 + 8.4 + 8.9)/3 = 26.4/3 = 8.8.
  4. ECE average = (7.8 + 7.2)/2 = 15.0/2 = 7.5.
  5. Two rows are returned.

Answer: Option (B) — CSE 8.8 and ECE 7.5.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) keeps MECH, whose group has only one row and therefore fails the HAVING test.
  • (C) drops ECE, whose count is 2 and so satisfies > 1.
  • (D) reports the first value of each group instead of the group average.

Common misconception. Thinking HAVING filters rows. It filters groups: a department with one student is removed as a group, not the student removed from the average.

Takeaway. GROUP BY partitions, aggregates summarise, HAVING keeps or drops whole partitions.

DB-U2-Q14NATmedium

Using the data below, what single value does the following query return?SELECT AVG(marks) FROM enroll WHERE cid = 'CS101';student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)Give the exact value (one decimal place).

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. AVG(expr) is defined as SUM(expr) / COUNT(expr). Both aggregates skip NULL, so the denominator is the number of non-NULL values, not the number of rows. That is the whole point of the question: the ungraded row is left out of both the numerator and the denominator.

Step by step.

  1. cid = 'CS101' selects three rows: (1,CS101,88), (2,CS101,NULL) and (4,CS101,95).
  2. The NULL row is excluded, so SUM(marks) = 88 + 95 = 183 and COUNT(marks) = 2.
  3. AVG = 183 / 2 = 91.5.

Answer: 91.5

Common misconception. Dividing by the row count, 183/3 = 61. That is the value you get if NULL is treated as 0 — SQL never does that for AVG. If the intent really is "treat ungraded as zero", the question must say AVG(COALESCE(marks,0)).

Takeaway. AVG ignores NULLs in both the sum and the count; the denominator is COUNT(col).

DB-U2-Q15MSQhard

Which of the following statements about grouping and aggregate functions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. SELECT dept, name FROM student GROUP BY dept; is invalid in standard SQL, because name is neither grouped nor aggregated.
  2. A WHERE clause may not contain an aggregate function, but a HAVING clause may.
  3. COUNT(*) ignores rows in which some column is NULL.
  4. An aggregate query with no GROUP BY returns exactly one row even when the table is empty.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. After GROUP BY, the SELECT list may contain only (i) grouped columns, (ii) aggregates and (iii) expressions built from those two. Anything else has no single value per group. Separately, an aggregate query with no GROUP BY defines one implicit group covering the whole table — and a group over zero rows still exists, so one row is produced.

Step by step.

  1. (A) name varies inside a dept group, so the standard rejects the query. True. (MySQL with ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY disabled returns an arbitrary name, which is a vendor extension, not the standard.)
  2. (B) WHERE is evaluated before grouping, so an aggregate there is meaningless; HAVING is evaluated after grouping. True.
  3. (C) COUNT(*) counts rows regardless of NULLs anywhere in them. False.
  4. (D) the implicit single group exists even for an empty table: COUNT(*) = 0 and SUM/AVG return NULL, but one row is returned. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true; (C) is false.

Common misconception. Expecting an empty aggregate query to return no rows. It returns one row — the empty-group row — which is why SELECT MAX(marks) FROM enroll WHERE 1=0 yields a single NULL rather than nothing.

Takeaway. Grouped SELECT may only use grouped columns and aggregates; the no-GROUP-BY aggregate always emits one row.

DB-U2-Q36MCQeasy

With the schema and data below, what does SELECT COUNT(cid), COUNT(DISTINCT cid), COUNT(*) FROM enroll; return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)

  1. 10, 3, 10
  2. 3, 3, 10
  3. 10, 10, 10
  4. 10, 3, 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. COUNT(col) counts the rows where col is not NULL, COUNT(DISTINCT col) counts the distinct non-NULL values, and COUNT(*) counts rows regardless of any value.

Step by step.

  1. All ten enroll rows have a non-NULL cid, so COUNT(cid) = 10.
  2. The distinct cid values are C1, C2 and C3, so COUNT(DISTINCT cid) = 3.
  3. COUNT(*) counts rows, so it is 10.
  4. The answer is (10, 3, 10).

Answer: Option (A) — 10, 3, 10.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) applies DISTINCT to the first count as well.
  • (C) drops DISTINCT from the second count.
  • (D) reports 7, which is the number of rows for the courses with at least two enrollments — irrelevant here.

Common misconception. Assuming COUNT(DISTINCT cid) equals COUNT(cid) because there are only three courses. Distinctness and row count are different questions.

Takeaway. COUNT(col) counts values, COUNT(DISTINCT col) counts distinct values, COUNT(*) counts rows.

DB-U2-Q37MCQeasy

With the data below, what happens to SELECT COUNT(*) FROM enroll HAVING COUNT(*) > 5;?student: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)

  1. It is rejected, because HAVING requires a GROUP BY clause.
  2. It is legal: the whole table is one implicit group, so it returns the single value 10.
  3. It is legal, and it returns all ten rows.
  4. It is legal, and it returns one row per course that has more than five enrollments.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A query with aggregates and no GROUP BY has exactly one group — the whole table. HAVING is a filter on groups, so it is legal without GROUP BY and is applied to that single group. The result is one row when the condition holds and zero rows when it does not.

Step by step.

  1. No GROUP BY → one group containing all ten rows.
  2. COUNT(*) for that group is 10.
  3. 10 > 5 is true, so the group survives and one row is produced.
  4. The result is the single value 10 — not ten rows, because the row count is fixed by the number of groups.

Answer: Option (B) — one row containing 10.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is a common belief but not the standard: HAVING without GROUP BY is well defined.
  • (C) confuses filtering groups with filtering rows; WHERE filters rows.
  • (D) would need a GROUP BY cid.

Common misconception. Thinking HAVING is only meaningful with GROUP BY. It filters whatever groups exist, and without GROUP BY exactly one group exists.

Takeaway. No GROUP BY + aggregate = one implicit group; HAVING tests that one group.

DB-U2-Q38MCQmedium

Table t(g, v) holds (A,1), (A,2), (NULL,3), (NULL,4), (B,5). What does SELECT g, COUNT(*) FROM t GROUP BY g; return?

  1. 4 rows, because each NULL is its own group.
  2. 2 rows, because groups whose key is NULL are discarded.
  3. 3 rows: A with 2, B with 1, and one NULL group with 2.
  4. 3 rows: A with 2, B with 1, and one NULL group with 1.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. GROUP BY treats NULLs as equal to each other for the purpose of forming groups. All rows with a NULL grouping key fall into a single group, which is then reported with a NULL key. This is a special case of grouping, not of =, under which NULL = NULL is UNKNOWN.

Step by step.

  1. Partition by g: {A,A}, {NULL,NULL}, {B}.
  2. Counts: A → 2, NULL → 2, B → 1.
  3. Three groups, hence three rows; the NULL group is reported, not discarded.

Answer: Option (C) — 3 rows, the NULL group holding 2.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) applies the = semantics of NULL to grouping, which does not use it.
  • (B) confuses GROUP BY with an equality predicate that could fail.
  • (D) counts only one of the two NULL rows.

Common misconception. Believing NULL groups vanish, or that each NULL forms its own group. Grouping collapses all NULL keys into one group.

Takeaway. All NULL keys form one group; COUNT(*) then reports every row in it.

DB-U2-Q39NATmedium

With the schema and data below, what single value does the following query return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT COUNT(*) FROM ( SELECT dept FROM student GROUP BY dept HAVING AVG(cgpa) > 8.0 ) x;

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The derived table groups students by department and keeps only the departments whose average cgpa exceeds 8.0. The outer COUNT(*) then counts the surviving groups — one row per department.

Step by step.

  1. CSE: (8.6 + 9.2 + 7.9)/3 = 25.7/3 ≈ 8.567, which is above 8.0 → kept.
  2. ECE: (7.4 + 8.0)/2 = 7.7, which is not above 8.0 → dropped.
  3. MECH: 6.8/1 = 6.8, not above 8.0 → dropped.
  4. One group survives, so COUNT(*) = 1.

Answer: 1

Common misconception. Counting students rather than departments, or rounding CSE's 8.567 down to 8.0 and then applying a strict >. The comparison is on the exact average, and 25.7/3 is strictly greater than 8.

Takeaway. HAVING filters groups, so the outer count is a count of groups.

DB-U2-Q40MSQhard

Which of the following statements about grouping and aggregate functions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. SELECT dept, COUNT(*) FROM student; is rejected in standard SQL, because dept is neither grouped nor aggregated.
  2. SELECT cid, COUNT(*) FROM enroll GROUP BY cid HAVING marks > 80; is legal, because HAVING may reference any column of the table.
  3. AVG(marks) and SUM(marks) / COUNT(*) agree when no mark is NULL, and differ when some mark is NULL.
  4. MIN and MAX ignore NULLs, so a group in which every mark is NULL yields NULL for MAX(marks).
Show answer & solution
