Detailed solution

Concept. After GROUP BY, each output row stands for a whole group, so the select list may contain only grouped columns, aggregates and expressions built from those two. HAVING is itself a condition on the group and follows the same rule. Every aggregate except COUNT(*) ignores NULL, so an all-NULL group aggregates to NULL.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: dept varies inside the single implicit group, so it has no single value per group.
  2. (B) False: marks is neither grouped nor aggregated, so it has no value per group either — the engine rejects it.
  3. (C) True: without NULLs the two expressions are identical; with k NULLs out of n rows, AVG divides by n − k while SUM/COUNT(*) divides by n, so they differ.
  4. (D) True: MAX over an empty set of non-NULL values is NULL.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) are true; (B) is false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the trap: HAVING may use aggregates and grouped columns, not arbitrary columns.

Common misconception. Thinking HAVING relaxes the grouping rule because it runs after grouping. It filters groups; it does not let an ungrouped column through.

Takeaway. Grouped SELECT and HAVING may only use grouped columns and aggregates.

ddl-dml-dcl · 10
DB-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Which of the following sets contains only Data Definition Language (DDL) statements?

  1. INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE
  2. CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE
  3. GRANT, REVOKE, DENY
  4. SELECT, COMMIT, ROLLBACK
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SQL statements are grouped by what they act on. DDL defines and changes the schema (CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE, RENAME). DML changes the data inside the objects (SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE). DCL controls privileges (GRANT, REVOKE). TCL controls transactions (COMMIT, ROLLBACK, SAVEPOINT).

Step by step.

  1. Check each word against the four groups: definition, manipulation, control, transaction.
  2. Option (B) lists four schema-changing statements only — all DDL.
  3. Option (A) is the DML family; (C) is DCL; (D) mixes a DML query with two transaction-control statements.

Answer: Option (B) — CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE are all DDL.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and MERGE act on rows, so they are DML.
  • (C) GRANT and REVOKE hand out or take back privileges, so they are DCL (DENY is the same family).
  • (D) SELECT reads rows (DML/DQL), while COMMIT and ROLLBACK end or undo a transaction (TCL).

Common misconception. TRUNCATE looks like a row operation, so it is often filed under DML. In the SQL standard it is a DDL statement: it drops and re-creates the table's storage rather than deleting rows one at a time, which is why it is far faster than an unqualified DELETE.

Takeaway. DDL = definition, DML = data, DCL = privileges, TCL = transactions.

DB-U2-Q02MCQeasy

Table enroll holds 8 rows. Compare DELETE FROM enroll; with DROP TABLE enroll;. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. DELETE removes the table definition but leaves the rows; DROP removes the rows.
  2. Both statements remove the rows and the table definition.
  3. DELETE removes every row but keeps the table definition; DROP removes the rows together with the table definition.
  4. DROP removes only the rows and leaves the definition; DELETE removes the definition.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The row set and the schema object are separate things. DELETE is DML: it removes rows from an existing table, and the table keeps its columns, constraints, indexes and privileges. DROP is DDL: it removes the table object itself from the catalogue, so the rows go with it and any later reference to the name fails.

Step by step.

  1. DELETE FROM enroll; with no WHERE deletes all 8 rows; the table still exists and accepts new inserts.
  2. DROP TABLE enroll; deletes the definition; the 8 rows disappear because the object that held them is gone.
  3. A re-created table has no rows and no old constraints — nothing is restored.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is wrong because DELETE never touches the definition.
  • (A) and (D) invert the two statements.

Common misconception. Treating DROP as "delete the rows". The distinction is testable: after DELETE, SELECT * FROM enroll returns an empty result set; after DROP, the same query raises a "no such table" error.

Takeaway. DELETE empties, DROP erases.

DB-U2-Q03MCQmedium

In the schema below, enroll.roll is declared REFERENCES student(roll) ON DELETE SET NULL. Student 4 has two rows in enroll. After DELETE FROM student WHERE roll = 4; what happens to those two rows?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungraded

  1. Both enroll rows are deleted with the student.
  2. One row is deleted and the other is set to NULL.
  3. The delete is rejected, because two rows still reference student 4.
  4. Both enroll rows remain, with their roll value set to NULL.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A foreign key with a referential action decides what happens to referencing rows when the referenced row is deleted. The four standard actions are CASCADE (delete the children), SET NULL (keep the children, null out the key), SET DEFAULT, and NO ACTION/RESTRICT (reject the delete).

Step by step.

  1. ON DELETE SET NULL keeps every referencing row and overwrites only the foreign-key column.
  2. Both of student 4's enrollment rows are referencing rows, so both are kept.
  3. Their roll becomes NULL; the cid and marks values are untouched.
  4. The student row itself is removed, so the two orphaned enrollments now have no owner.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes ON DELETE CASCADE.
  • (C) describes NO ACTION or RESTRICT, the default when no action is written.
  • (B) is not an action any standard system performs.

Common misconception. Confusing "the delete is refused" with "the children are removed". A foreign key by itself does not prevent a delete; it only prevents leaving a dangling reference. SET NULL avoids the dangling reference by erasing the value, so the delete is allowed and the child rows survive. Note the column must be nullable for this action to be legal.

Takeaway. Foreign key actions: CASCADE deletes children, SET NULL orphans them, RESTRICT refuses the parent delete.

DB-U2-Q04NATmedium

student holds 6 rows and enroll holds 8 rows, as below. Two DML statements are then executed, in this order:DELETE FROM enroll WHERE marks IS NULL; DELETE FROM student WHERE roll NOT IN (SELECT roll FROM enroll);How many rows remain in student?student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is a two-step DML trace. The first statement is a filtered delete on the child table; the second deletes parent rows that no longer have any child. Because roll is the primary key of student it can never be NULL, so the NOT IN subquery contains no NULL and behaves like an ordinary anti-join.

Step by step.

  1. Step 1 removes the two ungraded enrollments: (2,CS101,NULL) and (5,EC201,NULL). Six enrollment rows remain.
  2. The rolls still present in enroll are 1, 2, 3 and 4 — note that roll 2 survives through its CS102 row, and roll 5 has now lost its only row.
  3. Step 2 deletes student rows whose roll is not in {1, 2, 3, 4}: that removes rolls 5 and 6.
  4. 6 − 2 = 4 students remain: rolls 1, 2, 3 and 4.

Answer: 4 students remain (rolls 1, 2, 3, 4).

Common misconception. Deleting roll 5 in step 1 by mistake, because its enrollment was ungraded. The first statement deletes enrollment rows, not students; roll 5 is removed only in step 2, and only because it has no enrollment left. Also note that a NULL in the NOT IN subquery would have made step 2 delete nothing at all — it is safe here only because roll is a key.

Takeaway. Trace each statement on the table it names, then re-evaluate the next statement against the new state.

DB-U2-Q05MSQhard

Which of the following statements about SQL constraints and privileges are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A CHECK constraint rejects a row whose checked expression evaluates to UNKNOWN.
  2. GRANT SELECT ON student TO analyst WITH GRANT OPTION; lets analyst pass that same privilege on to other users.
  3. In standard SQL a UNIQUE constraint permits several rows that have NULL in the unique column.
  4. A column declared PRIMARY KEY may contain NULL provided the table also has a UNIQUE constraint on the same column.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three separate rules are being tested: how CHECK treats UNKNOWN, what WITH GRANT OPTION adds, and how the standard treats NULL inside UNIQUE and PRIMARY KEY. A constraint is violated only when its predicate is false; UNKNOWN (from a NULL operand) satisfies it. NULL is never equal to NULL, so two NULLs cannot duplicate each other in a unique column. A primary key, by definition, may not be NULL.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A CHECK (marks >= 0) on a NULL marks evaluates to UNKNOWN, which is accepted — so the row is inserted. The statement is false.
  2. (B) WITH GRANT OPTION is exactly the right to re-grant. True.
  3. (C) The unique constraint is violated only when two rows agree on every constrained column with non-NULL values; NULLs are distinct, so many NULLs may coexist. True (SQL Server is the well-known deviation, allowing only one).
  4. (D) Entity integrity forbids NULL in any primary-key column, whatever other constraints exist. False.

Answer: Options (B) and (C) are true.

Common misconception. Reading a CHECK as "must be true". SQL uses three-valued logic, so a constraint fires only on FALSE; UNKNOWN passes. This is why CHECK (marks IN (0,1)) does not stop a NULL from being inserted, and why a "not equal" check such as CHECK (a <> b) still admits rows where either side is NULL.

Takeaway. Constraints reject FALSE, not UNKNOWN; NULLs are distinct in UNIQUE; a primary key is never NULL.

DB-U2-Q26MCQeasy

Table orders is declared as orders(oid INT PRIMARY KEY, qty INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 5, note VARCHAR(20)). The statement INSERT INTO orders (oid, note) VALUES (7, NULL); is then executed. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. It is rejected, because qty is NOT NULL and no value was supplied for it.
  2. It is accepted: qty receives the declared default 5, and note is stored as NULL.
  3. It is accepted, and qty is stored as NULL because the column was left out of the column list.
  4. It is rejected, because note may not be given a NULL.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A column that is omitted from an INSERT column list takes its declared DEFAULT, and a declared default satisfies NOT NULL. NOT NULL forbids only an explicit NULL or an omitted column that has no default; it never forbids a default value.

Step by step.

  1. The statement names two columns, oid and note; qty is not named.
  2. An unnamed column is filled from its DEFAULT clause, here 5, so NOT NULL is satisfied.
  3. NULL is written explicitly into note, which is nullable because no constraint forbids it.
  4. The row (7, 5, NULL) is inserted.

Answer: Option (B) — the row is accepted with qty = 5 and note = NULL.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) confuses an omitted column with a missing value; the default supplies one.
  • (C) would hold only if qty had no DEFAULT, in which case the insert would be rejected rather than storing NULL.
  • (D) invents a constraint on note; the declaration makes it nullable.

Common misconception. Reading NOT NULL as "every insert must name the column". It only says the stored value may not be NULL, and the default guarantees that.

Takeaway. Omitted column with a DEFAULT → default value, never NULL and never an error.

DB-U2-Q27MCQeasy

With the schema and data below, the statement UPDATE enroll SET marks = marks + 5 WHERE cid = 'C3'; is executed. Which statement is TRUE?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)

  1. Both rows of course C3 change: 68 becomes 73 and the NULL becomes 5.
  2. The statement is rejected, because one row of C3 has a NULL mark.
  3. The graded row becomes 73, and the row whose mark is NULL stays NULL.
  4. The row whose mark is NULL is deleted, because NULL + 5 cannot be computed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Any arithmetic with NULL yields NULL, so NULL + 5 is NULL. An UPDATE writes that NULL back, which is a legal value in a nullable column. The WHERE clause tests only cid, so both C3 rows are still selected.

Step by step.

  1. Rows selected by WHERE cid = 'C3': (12,C3,68) and (16,C3,NULL).
  2. (12,C3,68) → 68 + 5 = 73.
  3. (16,C3,NULL) → NULL + 5 = NULL, so the column is set to NULL again.
  4. Two rows are updated; one value changes, the other is unchanged.

Answer: Option (C) — 73 and NULL.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) treats NULL as 0, which SQL never does for arithmetic.
  • (B) is wrong: a NULL operand is not an error, only an UNKNOWN value.
  • (D) is wrong: no statement deletes rows unless it is a DELETE.

Common misconception. Treating NULL as zero. marks + 5 on a NULL mark is NULL, not 5 — a value is only turned into 0 by an explicit COALESCE(marks,0).

Takeaway. NULL propagates through arithmetic; the row is still updated, to NULL.

DB-U2-Q28MCQmedium

u1 holds SELECT on student WITH GRANT OPTION and has granted SELECT on it to u2. The DBA now runs REVOKE SELECT ON student FROM u1 RESTRICT;. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. The statement succeeds, and u2 keeps the privilege.
  2. The statement succeeds, and u2 loses the privilege as well.
  3. The statement fails, but u1 still loses the privilege.
  4. The statement fails, because u1 has passed the privilege on; REVOKE ... CASCADE would succeed and take it away from both u1 and u2.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A privilege that has been granted onward creates a dependency. RESTRICT refuses to revoke a privilege that others still hold through it; CASCADE revokes the privilege from the grantee and, transitively, from everyone who received it from that grantee.

Step by step.

  1. u1 was granted SELECT WITH GRANT OPTION, so its grant to u2 is a dependent grant.
  2. RESTRICT is the default: it fails rather than silently changing u2's rights.
  3. CASCADE removes the privilege from u1 and follows the dependency to u2.
  4. Nothing is revoked when the RESTRICT form fails, so u1 keeps the privilege.

Answer: Option (D) — RESTRICT fails; CASCADE removes it from both users.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) both assume RESTRICT succeeds, which it does not when a dependent grant exists.
  • (C) invents a partial effect; a failed REVOKE changes nothing at all.

Common misconception. Expecting RESTRICT to revoke u1 and leave u2 dangling. The statement is atomic: it either succeeds completely or not at all.

Takeaway. RESTRICT protects dependent grants by failing; CASCADE is the deliberate recursive revoke.

DB-U2-Q29NATmedium

A schema declares both foreign keys with ON DELETE CASCADE:dept(dno) -- primary key emp(eno, dno) -- dno REFERENCES dept(dno) ON DELETE CASCADE dependent(did, eno) -- eno REFERENCES emp(eno) ON DELETE CASCADEand holds the datadept: 1, 2, 3 emp: (101,1) (102,1) (103,2) (104,3) dependent: (1,101) (2,101) (3,103)The statement DELETE FROM dept WHERE dno = 1; is executed. How many rows are removed from emp and dependent together? (Count only those two tables.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cascade chain is followed transitively. Deleting a dept row cascades to its emp rows, and deleting an emp row in turn cascades to its dependent rows.

Step by step.

  1. Delete dept 1 → cascade to emp rows with dno = 1: 101 and 102. That is 2 rows of emp.
  2. Each deleted emp row cascades to its dependents: emp 101 has dependents 1 and 2; emp 102 has none. That is 2 rows of dependent.
  3. Dept 2 and dept 3 are untouched, so emp 103, 104 and dependent 3 survive.
  4. 2 + 2 = 4 rows removed.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Counting only the direct children (2 emp rows) and forgetting that the cascade continues to the grandchildren.

Takeaway. CASCADE is transitive; follow the chain until no referencing row remains.

DB-U2-Q30MSQhard

Which of the following statements about DML, DDL and privileges are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If an UPDATE would violate a CHECK constraint for even one of the rows it touches, none of those rows is updated — the statement is atomic.
  2. An INSERT INTO t SELECT ... that fails on the last of five rows leaves the first four rows inserted.
  3. DELETE FROM t; with no WHERE removes every row but keeps the table definition and the privileges granted on it.
  4. DROP TABLE t; also removes the privileges that were granted on t.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two separate principles are at work. A single SQL statement is atomic: it either has its full effect or none. And privileges are attached to the object, so destroying the object destroys the privileges that depended on it.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Constraint checking is part of the statement, so a violation anywhere rolls the whole statement back.
  2. (B) False. Statement atomicity means the four earlier rows are undone with the fifth.
  3. (C) True. DELETE is DML: the rows go, the object and its grants stay.
  4. (D) True. A grant is a privilege on a named object; once the object no longer exists the grant has nothing to attach to.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) are true; (B) is false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the classic partial-effect trap: only an explicit loop of separate statements can leave earlier rows behind.

Common misconception. Believing that a multi-row DML statement commits row by row. It does not; the whole statement succeeds or the whole statement is undone.

Takeaway. One statement = all or nothing; dropping the object drops the grants on it.

joins-outer-joins · 10
DB-U2-Q16MCQeasy

Using the data below, how many rows does the following query return?SELECT * FROM student s LEFT JOIN enroll e ON s.roll = e.roll;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. 6
  2. 8
  3. 9
  4. 14
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A left outer join emits one row for every matching pair, plus one NULL-extended row for each left row that found no match. So the count is (number of matching pairs) + (number of unmatched left rows).

Step by step.

  1. Matching pairs: roll 1 contributes 2, roll 2 contributes 2, roll 3 contributes 1, roll 4 contributes 2, roll 5 contributes 1 — 8 pairs in total, the whole of enroll.
  2. Unmatched left rows: roll 6 (Farhan) has no enrollment, so it contributes 1 row with cid and marks NULL.
  3. 8 + 1 = 9 rows.

Answer: Option (C) — 9 rows.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the inner-join count: an inner join returns only the 8 matched pairs and silently drops roll 6.
  • (A) is the number of students, not the number of joined rows.
  • (D) would be the count if the join were many-to-many across a larger table; here the total is bounded by 8 + 6 = 14 only in the degenerate case where every enrollment failed to match.

Common misconception. Assuming an outer join adds one row per left row. It adds one row only for unmatched left rows; matched rows are duplicated exactly as an inner join would duplicate them.

Takeaway. LEFT JOIN row count = matched pairs + unmatched left rows.

DB-U2-Q17MCQeasy

Using the data below, how many rows does the following query return?SELECT * FROM student, course;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. 18
  2. 9
  3. 6
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A comma-separated FROM list with no join condition is a Cartesian product: every row of the first table is paired with every row of the second. The size is the product of the two row counts.

Step by step.

  1. student has 6 rows and course has 3 rows.
  2. No WHERE clause removes any pairing.
  3. 6 × 3 = 18 rows.

Answer: Option (A) — 18 rows.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is 6 + 3, the sum of the row counts rather than the product.
  • (C) and (D) are the individual table sizes.

Common misconception. Confusing the Cartesian product with a natural join. The product has |R| × |S| rows; a natural join on a common attribute collapses the pairs that agree, and here the two tables share no attribute name at all, so even NATURAL JOIN would degenerate to the product.

Takeaway. No join condition = cross product = product of the cardinalities.

DB-U2-Q18MCQmedium

Using the data below, what does the following query return?SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student s LEFT JOIN enroll e ON s.roll = e.roll WHERE e.roll IS NULL;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. 8
  2. 0
  3. 6
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the standard anti-join idiom: left-join, then keep only the NULL-extended rows. After the outer join, e.roll is NULL exactly for the left rows that found no match, so the WHERE predicate isolates the students with no enrollment.

Step by step.

  1. The left join produces 9 rows, of which 8 are matched and 1 is the NULL-extended row for roll 6.
  2. e.roll IS NULL is true only on that single row.
  3. COUNT(*) = 1.

Answer: Option (D) — 1 (only roll 6).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would be the answer if every student had at least one enrollment.
  • (C) counts all students, ignoring the join.
  • (A) is the size of enroll.

Common misconception. Writing WHERE e.roll IS NULL inside an inner join and expecting the same answer. With an inner join the NULL-extended rows never exist, so the predicate can never be true and the result is always empty — the outer join is what makes the test meaningful.

Takeaway. Anti-join = LEFT JOIN … WHERE right.key IS NULL.

DB-U2-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about outer joins are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The ON condition of a left outer join also filters out left rows that fail to match.
  2. A predicate on a right-table column placed in the WHERE clause of a left outer join can remove the NULL-extended rows and so reduce the result to that of an inner join.
  3. A FULL OUTER JOIN returns unmatched rows from both sides, padding the other side with NULLs.
  4. A LEFT JOIN B and B RIGHT JOIN A produce the same set of result rows (with the same column pairs) when written with the same join condition.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An outer join preserves one or both sides. LEFT preserves the left input, RIGHT preserves the right, FULL preserves both. Swapping the operands therefore swaps which side is preserved, and the two results describe the same pairs. The ON condition decides matching; the WHERE clause is applied after the NULL extension, so a WHERE predicate can undo the preservation.

Step by step.

  1. (D) swapping the operands of a left join turns it into the mirror-image right join with identical pairs. True.
  2. (B) if the WHERE clause requires, say, e.marks > 70, the NULL-extended rows evaluate to UNKNOWN and are discarded, leaving exactly the inner-join rows. True — this is the classic "outer join that is really an inner join" bug.
  3. (C) is the definition of a full outer join. True.
  4. (A) false: a left row that fails the ON condition is precisely the row that is kept, padded with NULLs.

Answer: Options (D), (B) and (C) are true; (A) is false.

Common misconception. Putting a right-table predicate in WHERE and expecting NULL-padded rows to survive. They cannot: a NULL never satisfies a comparison. To filter the matching without losing the unmatched rows, the predicate must move into the ON clause.

Takeaway. ON decides matches, WHERE prunes the final rows — and that is how a left join silently becomes an inner join.

DB-U2-Q20NAThard

Using the schema and data below, what does the following query return?SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student s JOIN enroll e ON e.roll = s.roll JOIN course c ON c.cid = e.cid WHERE s.dept = 'CSE' AND c.credits = 4;student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A three-table equi-join is a chain of matches: the student must match an enrollment, and that enrollment must match a course. COUNT(*) then counts rows of the joined result, so a student with two qualifying enrollments contributes two, not one.

Step by step.

  1. CSE students are rolls 1, 2 and 4.
  2. Their enrollment rows: roll 1 has CS101 and CS102; roll 2 has CS101 and CS102; roll 4 has CS101 and CS102 — six rows in total.
  3. All six reference CS101 or CS102, and both of those courses have credits = 4, so the third join and the credits predicate keep all six.
  4. No EC201 enrollment belongs to a CSE student, so nothing is filtered further. The answer is 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Answering 3 (the number of CSE students) or 2 (the number of courses). The query counts joined rows, so the grain is (student, enrollment, course) — one row per enrollment.

Takeaway. COUNT(*) after a join counts the joined rows; check the grain before you answer.

DB-U2-Q41MCQeasy

With the schema and data below, what does SELECT * FROM student NATURAL JOIN enroll; return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)

  1. Six columns and 60 rows, because the two roll columns are both kept.
  2. One roll column (not two) and 10 rows, because roll is the only column name the two tables share.
  3. One roll column and 6 rows, one per student.
  4. It is rejected, because a natural join needs a declared foreign key.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A natural join is an equi-join on every column name the two tables share, with the duplicate columns merged into one. The row count is the number of matching pairs, exactly as for any inner join.

Step by step.

  1. Shared column name: roll only. So the join condition is s.roll = e.roll.
  2. The merged result has 4 + 3 − 1 = 6 columns.
  3. Every one of the 10 enroll rows has a matching student, so there are 10 matching pairs.
  4. Roll 14 has no enrollment, so it does not appear — this is an inner join.

Answer: Option (B) — one roll column and 10 rows.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a cross join that keeps both copies of roll.
  • (C) counts students rather than matching pairs, and would silently drop the multi-enrollment students.
  • (D) is false: a natural join is driven by column names, not by declared keys.

Common misconception. Forgetting that NATURAL JOIN merges the shared column, and that it is an inner join, so the unenrolled student disappears.

Takeaway. NATURAL JOIN = equi-join on shared names + one merged copy of each shared column.

DB-U2-Q42MCQeasy

Which statement about ON and WHERE in an inner join is TRUE?

  1. ON carries only equality conditions; an inequality belongs in WHERE.
  2. ON is evaluated after WHERE.
  3. They give different results whenever a joined column contains a NULL.
  4. For an inner join, moving a predicate from ON to WHERE gives the same result.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For an inner join the two clauses are interchangeable, because both simply discard rows that fail the predicate. The distinction only becomes visible with an outer join, where ON decides which pairs match but WHERE runs after the NULL-extension and can remove the preserved rows.

Step by step.

  1. An inner join keeps exactly the pairs for which the ON predicate is TRUE.
  2. A WHERE predicate is also applied to those same pairs, before the result is produced.
  3. Conjunction is commutative, so applying either predicate first leaves the same set of pairs.
  4. Hence the two placements are equivalent for an inner join — and only for an inner join.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: ON accepts any boolean expression, including inequalities and conjunctions.
  • (B) reverses the order; ON determines the pairs, WHERE filters afterwards.
  • (C) is false for inner joins; NULL behaviour is the same under either placement.

Common misconception. Generalising the outer-join rule to inner joins. The placement matters only when there are NULL-extended rows to lose.

Takeaway. Inner join: ON and WHERE are equivalent. Outer join: ON matches, WHERE prunes.

DB-U2-Q43MCQmedium

With the data below, what does the following query return?student: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student a JOIN student b ON a.dept = b.dept AND a.roll < b.roll;

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 9
  4. 15
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is a self-join that pairs students within a department. The strict inequality a.roll < b.roll keeps each unordered pair once, so the count is the sum over departments of C(k,2) for a department of k students.

Step by step.

  1. CSE: rolls 11, 13, 16 → C(3,2) = 3 pairs.
  2. ECE: rolls 12, 15 → C(2,2) = 1 pair.
  3. MECH: roll 14 alone → 0 pairs.
  4. 3 + 1 + 0 = 4.

Answer: Option (A) — 4.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 6 counts the CSE pairs twice, i.e. it uses a.roll <> b.roll instead of a strict order.
  • (C) 9 is 3 × 3, the CSE rows of the unrestricted product.
  • (D) 15 is C(6,2), the number of pairs ignoring the department.

Common misconception. Using <> instead of <, which returns each unordered pair twice.

Takeaway. Self-join for unordered pairs: use a strict inequality so each pair appears once.

DB-U2-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about joins are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. NATURAL JOIN joins on every column name the two tables share and keeps one copy of each shared column.
  2. CROSS JOIN and INNER JOIN ... ON TRUE produce the same row multiset.
  3. A LEFT JOIN whose ON condition matches nothing still returns every row of the left table, NULL-extended.
  4. A FULL OUTER JOIN never returns more rows than the sum of the two tables' row counts.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A join with no effective condition is a Cartesian product, whether it is written CROSS JOIN or INNER JOIN ... ON TRUE. An outer join guarantees the preserved side is represented: an unmatched preserved row still appears, padded with NULLs. The number of rows can therefore far exceed the sum of the inputs.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True — that is the definition of a natural join.
  2. (B) True: a condition that is always TRUE restricts nothing, so the inner join degenerates to the product.
  3. (C) True — that is exactly what the preserved side means.
  4. (D) False: if every left row matches every right row the result has m × n rows, which exceeds m + n as soon as both sides have at least two rows.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) fails on a complete many-to-many match: 3 × 3 = 9 rows from tables of 3 and 3.

Common misconception. Assuming an outer join bounds the output by the input sizes. Only the unmatched rows are added; matched pairs are duplicated exactly as an inner join would duplicate them.

Takeaway. No condition = product; outer join guarantees the preserved side, and nothing else.

DB-U2-Q45NAThard

With the schema and data below, how many rows does the following query return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student s LEFT JOIN enroll e ON s.roll = e.roll AND e.marks > 80;

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a LEFT JOIN the ON condition decides which pairs match; the left table is preserved regardless. The row count is (matching pairs) + (left rows with no match). Placing the right-table predicate in ON is what keeps the unmatched students in the result.

Step by step.

  1. Enrollments above 80: (11,C1,82), (11,C2,91), (13,C1,95), (13,C2,88), (16,C1,88) — 5 matching pairs.
  2. Students with at least one such enrollment: 11, 13, 16. They contribute their 5 pairs and nothing extra.
  3. Students 12, 14 and 15 have no enrollment above 80, so each contributes one NULL-extended row: 3 rows.
  4. 5 + 3 = 8 rows.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Two traps. Putting e.marks > 80 in WHERE instead of ON, which deletes the three NULL-extended rows and leaves 5; or counting students (6) instead of joined rows.

Takeaway. LEFT JOIN row count = matching pairs + unmatched left rows, with the right-side filter kept in ON.

nested-and-correlated-subqueries · 10
DB-U2-Q06MCQeasy

Using the schema below, which query returns exactly the students who have not enrolled in any course?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

  1. SELECT roll FROM student s WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll);
  2. SELECT roll FROM student s WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll);
  3. SELECT roll FROM student WHERE roll NOT IN (SELECT roll FROM enroll WHERE marks IS NOT NULL);
  4. SELECT roll FROM student WHERE roll <> ALL (SELECT roll FROM enroll WHERE marks IS NULL);
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. NOT EXISTS is the standard "for no matching row" test. The inner query is correlated on s.roll, so for each student the database asks whether any enrollment row exists; NOT EXISTS keeps the student only when the answer is "no row". Unlike NOT IN it is safe in the presence of NULLs, because it never compares values — it only tests whether a row was produced.

Step by step.

  1. For roll 1 the inner query returns two rows, so NOT EXISTS is false and roll 1 is dropped.
  2. For roll 6 the inner query returns no row at all, so NOT EXISTS is true and roll 6 is kept.
  3. Applying this to every student leaves exactly roll 6.

Answer: Option (A) — only roll 6 (Farhan) has no enrollment.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the complement: it returns the five students who are enrolled.
  • (C) filters the subquery to graded rows, so the enrolled rolls it sees are {1, 2, 3, 4}. Roll 5 is absent from that set even though it is enrolled, so the query wrongly returns rolls 5 and 6.
  • (D) asks for rolls that differ from every ungraded enrollment roll ({2, 5}), which returns 1, 3, 4 and 6 — the opposite of the intended set.

Common misconception. Assuming NOT IN and NOT EXISTS are interchangeable. They agree only when the inner column is declared NOT NULL. When a NULL can appear, NOT IN silently returns the empty set while NOT EXISTS still answers the intended question.

Takeaway. For "has no related row", prefer NOT EXISTS: it is NULL-safe and reads as a universal test.

DB-U2-Q07MCQeasy

A subquery refers to a column of the table named in the enclosing query. Which statement about such a subquery is TRUE?

  1. It must appear in the FROM clause; a correlated reference is illegal in WHERE.
  2. It is an independent subquery, evaluated exactly once and then reused for every outer row.
  3. It is a correlated subquery, and conceptually it is evaluated again for each candidate row of the outer query.
  4. It cannot contain an aggregate function.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A subquery that mentions an outer column is correlated. Its result depends on the current outer row, so the conceptual model is: for each candidate outer row, bind the outer columns, run the inner query, then test the predicate. An uncorrelated subquery mentions only its own tables, so it can be computed once and its result reused — which is why optimisers try to decorrelate.

Step by step.

  1. Spot the outer reference: here s.roll inside the inner query.
  2. Because the inner result changes with each outer row, it is re-evaluated per row in the conceptual model.
  3. That is the definition of a correlated subquery.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes an uncorrelated (independent) subquery.
  • (A) is false: correlation is legal in WHERE, in the SELECT list and in HAVING.
  • (D) is false: a correlated aggregate such as (SELECT AVG(marks) FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll) is perfectly legal.

Common misconception. Believing a correlated subquery always costs one full scan per row. That is the naive plan; most engines rewrite it into a semi-join or an index lookup, so the practical cost can be close to a single join.

Takeaway. Outer reference present = correlated = per-row semantics; absent = uncorrelated = compute once.

DB-U2-Q08MCQmedium

Suppose the roll column of enroll is nullable and the subquery below returns at least one NULL.SELECT roll FROM student WHERE roll NOT IN (SELECT roll FROM enroll);What does the outer query return?

  1. Every student roll that is absent from the non-NULL values of the subquery.
  2. No rows at all.
  3. Every student roll, including those present in the subquery.
  4. A syntax error, because NULL cannot appear in an IN list.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. x NOT IN (a, b, NULL) expands to x <> a AND x <> b AND x <> NULL. The last conjunct is UNKNOWN for every x, and TRUE AND UNKNOWN = UNKNOWN. A WHERE clause keeps a row only when the predicate is TRUE, so no row qualifies — even a roll that matches nothing in the list.

Step by step.

  1. Rewrite NOT IN as a chain of <> comparisons joined by AND.
  2. One comparison is against NULL, which yields UNKNOWN.
  3. UNKNOWN AND anything is never TRUE, so the whole predicate can never be TRUE.
  4. The result set is therefore empty.

Answer: Option (B) — the query returns no rows.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is what the query would return if the subquery contained no NULL; the NULL destroys it.
  • (C) is the opposite of what happens: UNKNOWN fails, it does not pass.
  • (D) is false: NULL is a legal value in a subquery result; the failure is semantic, not syntactic.

Common misconception. Reading NOT IN as set difference. Set difference ignores NULLs; NOT IN propagates them. The fix is either to add WHERE roll IS NOT NULL to the subquery, or to rewrite the query as NOT EXISTS, which never compares values.

Takeaway. NOT IN + a NULL in the subquery = empty result. Use NOT EXISTS or filter the NULL.

DB-U2-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about SQL subqueries and quantified comparisons are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. x = ANY (S) returns the same truth value as x IN (S) for every subquery S whose result is not NULL-valued.
  2. x > ALL (S) is TRUE when the subquery S returns no rows at all.
  3. A correlated subquery written in the SELECT list is conceptually evaluated once per row of the result.
  4. NOT IN and NOT EXISTS always return the same rows.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Quantified comparisons are shorthand for a fold: = ANY is an OR over equality (that is IN), and > ALL is an AND over the comparisons. An OR over an empty set is FALSE, so = ANY on an empty subquery is false; an AND over an empty set is TRUE, so > ALL on an empty subquery is vacuously true.

Step by step.

  1. (A) = ANY ≡ IN, by definition of the quantifier. True.
  2. (B) x > ALL (empty) has no counterexample, so it holds vacuously. True — this is the "every student scored more than the best student of an empty class" case.
  3. (C) a SELECT-list subquery correlated on the outer row must be recomputed for each output row. True.
  4. (D) false: they diverge as soon as the inner column can be NULL, where NOT IN collapses to the empty set and NOT EXISTS does not.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Common misconception. Assuming > ALL needs a non-empty subquery. The empty case is exactly where GATE-style questions aim: universal quantification over an empty domain is TRUE, existential quantification over it is FALSE.

Takeaway. = ANY is IN, <> ALL is NOT IN, > ALL over an empty set is vacuously true.

DB-U2-Q10NAThard

Using the schema and data below, how many students does the following query return?SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student s WHERE (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll AND e.marks > (SELECT AVG(marks) FROM enroll e2 WHERE e2.cid = e.cid)) >= 2;student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The query is a doubly nested correlated count. For a given student, the middle query walks that student's enrollments; for each of them the innermost query recomputes the average marks of that course. A NULL marks never satisfies >, so ungraded courses simply do not contribute.

Step by step.

  1. Course averages over the non-NULL marks: CS101 = (88 + 95)/2 = 91.5; CS102 = (74 + 65 + 80)/3 = 73; EC201 = 91/1 = 91.
  2. Roll 1: 88 > 91.5 is false, 74 > 73 is true → count 1.
  3. Roll 2: CS101 is NULL (not counted), 65 > 73 is false → count 0.
  4. Roll 3: 91 > 91 is false → count 0.
  5. Roll 4: 95 > 91.5 is true, 80 > 73 is true → count 2.
  6. Roll 5: its only mark is NULL → count 0. Roll 6 has no enrollment rows, so the inner count is 0.
  7. Only roll 4 reaches the threshold of 2, so the outer COUNT(*) is 1.

Answer: 1 student (roll 4, Divya).

Common misconception. Two traps sit here. First, AVG ignores NULLs rather than treating them as 0, so CS101 averages 91.5 and not 61. Second, the threshold is "at least 2 courses", so roll 1 with a single qualifying course must be excluded — a count of 1 is not a pass.

Takeaway. Resolve innermost first: per-course average, then per-student comparison, then the outer count.

DB-U2-Q31MCQeasy

With the schema and data below, what does the following query produce for roll 14, the student with no row in enroll?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT s.roll, (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll) AS n FROM student s;

  1. Roll 14 is left out of the result entirely.
  2. Roll 14 appears with n set to NULL.
  3. Roll 14 appears with n = 0, because COUNT(*) over no rows is 0.
  4. The query is rejected, because a correlated subquery may not appear in the SELECT list.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An aggregate without GROUP BY always returns exactly one row, even when it sees no input rows. COUNT(*) over an empty set is 0, so the correlated subquery yields 0 rather than NULL or no row. A scalar subquery in the SELECT list is legal as long as it returns at most one row.

Step by step.

  1. For roll 14 the inner query is SELECT COUNT(*) FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = 14, which matches nothing.
  2. An aggregate query over zero rows still produces one row: the count of an empty set is 0.
  3. The outer row for roll 14 therefore survives, with n = 0.
  4. Rolls 11, 13 and 16 get 2, 2 and 2; rolls 12 and 15 get 2 each as well — every enrolled student has exactly two rows.

Answer: Option (C) — roll 14 appears with n = 0.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a join or an EXISTS filter, not a scalar subquery in the select list.
  • (B) would be right for SUM or MAX, which return NULL over no rows; COUNT does not.
  • (D) is false: correlation in the SELECT list is standard and is how per-row aggregates are written.

Common misconception. Assuming every aggregate over an empty input returns NULL. Only COUNT returns 0; SUM, AVG, MIN and MAX return NULL.

Takeaway. COUNT over nothing is 0; every other aggregate over nothing is NULL.

DB-U2-Q32MCQeasy

On the data below, the subquery inSELECT s.roll FROM student s WHERE s.cgpa = (SELECT e.marks FROM enroll e WHERE e.cid = 'C1');returns five rows. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. The statement fails at run time, because a scalar subquery used with = must return at most one row.
  2. The statement returns every student whose cgpa equals any of those five marks.
  3. The statement returns no rows, because a comparison against a multi-row subquery is always UNKNOWN.
  4. The statement is a syntax error, because a subquery may not stand on the right of = in a WHERE clause.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A subquery in a position that expects one value — the right operand of =, or a SELECT list item — is a scalar subquery and must produce at most one row. Producing more is a run-time cardinality error, not a silent set comparison.

Step by step.

  1. The right side of = expects a single value, so the subquery is scalar by position.
  2. WHERE e.cid = 'C1' matches five rows, so the subquery returns five values.
  3. A scalar subquery that returns more than one row raises an error when it is evaluated.
  4. To compare against a set, the query must say so: IN or = ANY.

Answer: Option (A) — the statement fails at run time with a cardinality error.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes = ANY (equivalently IN), which is a different operator.
  • (C) confuses a cardinality error with three-valued logic; no comparison is even attempted.
  • (D) is wrong: the position is legal, only the number of rows is not.

Common misconception. Expecting = followed by a subquery to behave like IN. It does not; = demands exactly one value, and an empty subquery would give NULL rather than an error.

Takeaway. Use IN or = ANY for a set; = is for one value and fails loudly on many.

DB-U2-Q33MCQmedium

With the schema and data below, which students does the following query return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT s.roll FROM student s WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll AND e.marks > 80);

  1. Only roll 14, the student with no row in enroll at all.
  2. Rolls 12, 14 and 15 — every student with no enrollment above 80, including the student who has no enrollment at all.
  3. Rolls 12 and 15 only, because roll 14 has no enroll row for the inner query to test.
  4. Rolls 12, 15 and 16.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The extra condition belongs inside the EXISTS subquery, so the inner query asks "does this student have an enrollment above 80?" and NOT EXISTS keeps the students for whom the answer is no. A student with no enrollment trivially has none above 80, so that student is kept too.

Step by step.

  1. Enrollments above 80: 82 and 91 (roll 11), 95 and 88 (roll 13), 88 (roll 16).
  2. Rolls with such an enrollment: 11, 13, 16 — these are dropped.
  3. Roll 12 has 74 and 68; roll 15 has 74 and 60; roll 14 has no row at all. None of them has an enrollment above 80.
  4. The result is rolls 12, 14 and 15.

Answer: Option (B) — rolls 12, 14 and 15.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reads the query as "has no enrollment", which is what you get if the marks > 80 condition is dropped.
  • (C) wrongly assumes an empty inner result is not a valid answer; it is exactly what NOT EXISTS is built to detect.
  • (D) keeps roll 16, whose 88 is above 80, so EXISTS is true and the row is removed.

Common misconception. Moving marks > 80 out of the subquery into the outer WHERE. That changes the question from "no enrollment above 80" to "has no enrollment, and separately scored above 80", and it also silently drops every student with no enrollment.

Takeaway. Conditions that define the thing being searched for go inside the EXISTS subquery.

DB-U2-Q34MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about subqueries are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In the conceptual model, a correlated subquery in the SELECT list is evaluated once for each row the outer query produces.
  2. NOT EXISTS is TRUE for an outer row whenever the inner query produces no rows, even when the inner column is nullable.
  3. IN and EXISTS always return the same rows, so one may be substituted for the other without care.
  4. A subquery in the FROM clause may refer to a column of another table in the same FROM clause.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Correlation means the inner query depends on the current outer row, so conceptually it is re-evaluated per row. EXISTS tests only whether a row was produced, which makes it immune to NULLs — unlike IN, whose comparison against a NULL yields UNKNOWN. Tables in one FROM list are combined as a set, so one cannot see the other's columns without a lateral construct, which standard SQL does not have.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True by the definition of correlation.
  2. (B) True: EXISTS never compares values, only counts rows, so a NULL in the inner column cannot break it.
  3. (C) False: they diverge as soon as the inner column can be NULL (NOT IN collapses to the empty set) and when duplicate rows matter.
  4. (D) False: standard SQL has no LATERAL; a derived table sees only its own tables and the enclosing query.

Answer: Options (A) and (B) are true; (C) and (D) are false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) fails on NULLs: x NOT IN (…, NULL) is never TRUE, while NOT EXISTS is unaffected.
  • (D) describes a lateral join, a vendor extension rather than standard SQL.

Common misconception. Assuming IN and EXISTS are interchangeable. They agree only when the inner column is declared NOT NULL and the outer query needs no duplicate elimination.

Takeaway. EXISTS counts rows, IN compares values; only one of the two survives NULLs.

DB-U2-Q35NAThard

With the schema and data below, how many students does the following query return?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)SELECT COUNT(*) FROM student s WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM enroll e WHERE e.roll = s.roll AND e.marks > (SELECT AVG(e2.marks) FROM enroll e2 WHERE e2.cid = e.cid));

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is a doubly nested correlated query. For a candidate student the middle query walks that student's enrollments; for each of them the innermost query recomputes the average marks of that course over its non-NULL marks. A NULL mark never satisfies >, so ungraded rows contribute nothing.

Step by step.

  1. Course averages over non-NULL marks: C1 = (82+74+95+74+88)/5 = 413/5 = 82.6; C2 = (91+88+60)/3 = 239/3 ≈ 79.67; C3 = 68/1 = 68.
  2. Roll 11: 82 > 82.6 is false, 91 > 79.67 is true → the student qualifies.
  3. Roll 12: 74 > 82.6 false, 68 > 68 false → does not qualify.
  4. Roll 13: 95 > 82.6 true → qualifies.
  5. Roll 14: no enrollment rows, so the middle query produces nothing → does not qualify.
  6. Roll 15: 74 > 82.6 false, 60 > 79.67 false → does not qualify.
  7. Roll 16: 88 > 82.6 true, and the C3 NULL is never above 68 → qualifies.
  8. Qualifying students: rolls 11, 13 and 16, so the count is 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Two traps. Computing one global average instead of a per-course average, and treating the NULL mark as 0 — which would make the C3 average 34 and let roll 12's 68 clear it.

Takeaway. Resolve the innermost aggregate per group first, then compare each row against its own group's value.

views-triggers-constraints · 10
DB-U2-Q21MCQeasy

Which statement about an SQL view is TRUE?

  1. A view always stores a private copy of the rows it returns, refreshed on every insert into the base table.
  2. A view is a named query: it stores no rows of its own (unless it is a materialised view) and its rows are recomputed from the base tables when the view is queried.
  3. A view must be defined over exactly one base table.
  4. Dropping a view deletes the rows of the underlying table.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In standard SQL a view is a derived table: the catalogue stores only the defining query. Every reference to the view expands to that query, so the view always reflects the current base tables. A materialised view is the exception — it does keep a stored copy and must be refreshed — but that is an optimisation feature, not what a plain CREATE VIEW gives you.

Step by step.

  1. The definition is stored as text; no tuples are copied.
  2. Querying the view runs (or rewrites) the defining query against the base tables.
  3. Therefore the view is always consistent with the base data, with no refresh step.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a materialised view, and even then the refresh is explicit or scheduled, not automatic on every insert.
  • (C) is false: a view may join any number of tables, as the aggregate view in this unit does.
  • (D) is false: DROP VIEW removes only the definition; the base tables and their rows are untouched.

Common misconception. Believing a view is a snapshot. Because it is recomputed, a view over student immediately shows a newly inserted student — there is nothing to refresh.

Takeaway. A view is stored logic, not stored data.

DB-U2-Q22MCQeasy

Which statement about an SQL trigger is TRUE?

  1. A trigger fires only for SELECT statements.
  2. A trigger must be called explicitly, for example with EXECUTE TRIGGER, after the statement it is meant to guard.
  3. A trigger fires automatically when its specified event (such as an INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE) occurs; it cannot be invoked directly by a statement.
  4. A table that has triggers may not also have constraints.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A trigger is an event–condition–action rule attached to a table or view. The database fires it as a side effect of the triggering statement; there is no SQL statement that calls a trigger by name. Triggers are declared BEFORE or AFTER the event, and FOR EACH ROW or FOR EACH STATEMENT.

Step by step.

  1. The event is a DML change (in standard SQL), optionally restricted with WHEN to specific columns.
  2. The action is a block of SQL executed by the engine when the event occurs.
  3. No user statement names the trigger, so it cannot be called directly.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) there is no such statement in standard SQL.
  • (A) triggers are defined on data-changing events; some systems add INSTEAD OF triggers on views, but a plain SELECT does not fire one.
  • (D) triggers and constraints are independent mechanisms and routinely coexist; constraints are usually preferred for declarative rules because they are cheaper.

Common misconception. Thinking a trigger replaces a foreign key. Triggers can enforce rules that constraints cannot express, but they are procedural, harder to reason about, and a well-modelled schema uses constraints first.

Takeaway. Triggers are fired by the engine, never called by the user.

DB-U2-Q23MCQmedium

Using the schema below, which of the following views is updatable in the standard sense — that is, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE through the view can be mapped onto the single base table it reads?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungraded

  1. CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT roll, name, dept FROM student WHERE dept = 'CSE';
  2. CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT dept, COUNT(*) AS n FROM student GROUP BY dept;
  3. CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM student;
  4. CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT s.roll, AVG(e.marks) AS avgmarks FROM student s JOIN enroll e ON e.roll = s.roll GROUP BY s.roll;
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A view is updatable when each of its rows corresponds to exactly one row of exactly one base table. Selection and projection preserve that correspondence; aggregation, DISTINCT, GROUP BY and joins do not, because one view row can then be derived from many base rows (or from none).

Step by step.

  1. (A) reads one table, keeps its key column roll and only filters rows, so every view row maps back to one student row. Updatable.
  2. (B) produces one row per department; no single student row corresponds to it. Not updatable.
  3. (C) collapses duplicates, so a view row has no unique source row. Not updatable.
  4. (D) aggregates over a join; again one row summarises many. Not updatable.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B), (C) and (D) are all read-only views under the standard updatability rules. A system may still let you modify them through an INSTEAD OF trigger, but that is a workaround, not updatability.

Common misconception. Assuming a view is read-only whenever it has a WHERE clause. A selection predicate is harmless — in fact, with WITH CHECK OPTION it becomes an enforced constraint on what may be written through the view.

Takeaway. One view row ↔ one base row is what makes a view updatable; aggregation and DISTINCT break it.

DB-U2-Q24NATmedium

The view below is created over the data shown. How many rows does SELECT * FROM v WHERE n > 1; return?CREATE VIEW v AS SELECT dept, COUNT(*) AS n FROM student GROUP BY dept;student: (1,Aarav,CSE,9.1) (2,Bhavna,CSE,8.4) (3,Chirag,ECE,7.8) (4,Divya,CSE,8.9) (5,Esha,MECH,6.9) (6,Farhan,ECE,7.2) course: (CS101,DBMS,4) (CS102,OS,4) (EC201,Signals,3) enroll: (1,CS101,88) (1,CS102,74) (2,CS101,NULL) (2,CS102,65) (3,EC201,91) (4,CS101,95) (4,CS102,80) (5,EC201,NULL)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The view materialises the grouping as an ordinary table of two columns, which can then be filtered like any other table. The WHERE on the view is applied after the view's own GROUP BY, so it filters groups, not students.

Step by step.

  1. The view has three rows: CSE with n = 3, ECE with n = 2 and MECH with n = 1.
  2. n > 1 keeps CSE and ECE.
  3. The result has 2 rows.

Answer: 2 rows (CSE with 3, ECE with 2).

Common misconception. Reading the query as "count the students in departments with more than one student", which would give 5. The outer query counts nothing — it returns the view's rows, so the answer is the number of qualifying groups.

Takeaway. A view over an aggregate is a table of groups; filtering it filters groups.

DB-U2-Q25MSQhard

Which of the following statements about views, triggers and constraints are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. With WITH CHECK OPTION on a view, an insert or update performed through the view is rejected if the new row would not satisfy the view's defining condition.
  2. Dropping a base table that a view references is always rejected by the DBMS.
  3. A trigger declared FOR EACH ROW fires once for every row that the triggering statement affects.
  4. A CHECK constraint may be declared directly on a view in standard SQL.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three different mechanisms are being separated. WITH CHECK OPTION makes the view's predicate a write-time constraint. Triggers are declared at row granularity or statement granularity. CHECK is a table constraint; the standard mechanism for a cross-table rule is an assertion, which no mainstream product implements.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of WITH CHECK OPTION: a row that would vanish from the view cannot be written through it. True.
  2. (B) false: the standard permits the drop and leaves the view invalid ("inoperative") until it is re-created or the table returns; some systems require CASCADE, others simply fail at the next use of the view. It is not universally rejected.
  3. (C) FOR EACH ROW means one firing per affected row; FOR EACH STATEMENT fires once for the whole statement. True.
  4. (D) false: CHECK is declared on a table or column, not on a view.

Answer: Options (A) and (C) are true; (B) and (D) are false.

Common misconception. Two traps. First, assuming WITH CHECK OPTION only affects SELECT — it affects writes. Second, assuming a trigger fires once per statement by default; the row granularity has to be read from the declaration, and FOR EACH ROW is the more common form.

Takeaway. CHECK OPTION guards writes through a view; FOR EACH ROW sets trigger granularity; CHECK belongs to tables.

DB-U2-Q46MCQeasy

CREATE VIEW high AS SELECT roll, cid, marks FROM enroll WHERE marks > 80 WITH CHECK OPTION; is created, and then INSERT INTO high VALUES (17, 'C1', 50); is executed. What happens?

  1. The row is inserted into enroll and is simply invisible through high.
  2. The row is inserted into enroll with marks raised to 81 by the check option.
  3. The insert is rejected, because the new row would not satisfy the view's WHERE predicate.
  4. The insert is rejected, because high is not updatable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. WITH CHECK OPTION turns the view's predicate into a condition on writes made through the view: a row that would fall outside the view is refused rather than inserted invisibly. Without the clause the insert would be accepted and the row would simply not appear through the view.

Step by step.

  1. The view is updatable: it is a selection and projection of a single base table.
  2. The check option requires every inserted or updated row to satisfy marks > 80.
  3. 50 does not satisfy it, so the insert fails with an error.
  4. Nothing is written to enroll.

Answer: Option (C) — the insert is rejected.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is what happens without the check option.
  • (B) invents a coercion; SQL never adjusts data to make a constraint pass.
  • (D) is false: this view is updatable, which is why the check option is meaningful.

Common misconception. Confusing WITH CHECK OPTION with WITH READ ONLY. The first permits the write if the predicate holds and rejects it otherwise; the second forbids writes altogether.

Takeaway. WITH CHECK OPTION makes the view's predicate a write-time constraint.

DB-U2-Q47MCQeasy

Which statement about an INSTEAD OF trigger on a view is TRUE?

  1. It fires after the row has been written to the base table.
  2. It runs in place of the triggering statement, which is how a non-updatable view is made writable.
  3. It may be declared only for DELETE on a view.
  4. It can be attached to a base table as well as to a view.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An INSTEAD OF trigger replaces the triggering DML statement on the view. Because the view has no storage of its own, the trigger body performs the real writes on the base tables — which is exactly what makes an otherwise non-updatable view writable. It is the one trigger form that is defined on views rather than on tables.

Step by step.

  1. The statement targets the view, and the view cannot store rows.
  2. The trigger intercepts the statement and supplies its own actions on the base tables.
  3. No automatic write happens, which is why the trigger body must perform it.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a normal AFTER trigger, which assumes the write already happened.
  • (C) is false: INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE are all allowed.
  • (D) is false: INSTEAD OF is defined on views; base tables use BEFORE and AFTER.

Common misconception. Assuming an INSTEAD OF trigger also performs the original write. It replaces it, so the base-table write must be written by hand.

Takeaway. INSTEAD OF = on views, replaces the statement, body does the real work.

DB-U2-Q48MCQmedium

In standard SQL, a CHECK constraint whose condition contains a subquery over another table …

  1. is allowed, and is re-evaluated whenever the other table changes.
  2. is allowed, but evaluated only on INSERT, never on UPDATE.
  3. is not allowed: a CHECK condition may reference only columns of the row being tested.
  4. is allowed, but silently ignored.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A CHECK constraint is a row-level predicate: it is tested against each candidate row and may mention only columns of that row. A subquery would make the constraint depend on other rows, which the standard excludes; cross-row conditions belong in a foreign key, an assertion or a trigger.

Step by step.

  1. The constraint is evaluated per row, at insert and update time, with no wider context.
  2. A subquery would need the rest of the database, including rows being changed concurrently.
  3. The standard therefore forbids subqueries in CHECK; most engines reject the DDL outright.

Answer: Option (C) — not permitted.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) would require re-checking every row of the table whenever the other table changes — not part of the model.
  • (B) invents a partial enforcement.
  • (D) is false: a rejected statement is not a silent ignore.

Common misconception. Believing a CHECK can compare against another table because the syntax accepts a general condition. The permitted scope is the current row only.

Takeaway. CHECK sees one row; anything cross-row needs a trigger, a key or an assertion.

DB-U2-Q49NATmedium

CREATE VIEW cs AS SELECT roll, cgpa FROM student WHERE dept = 'CSE'; is created on the schema and data below, and then UPDATE cs SET cgpa = cgpa + 0.5; is executed. How many rows of student change?student(roll, name, dept, cgpa) -- roll is the primary key course(cid, title, credits) -- cid is the primary key enroll(roll, cid, marks) -- marks is NULL while a course is ungradedstudent: (11,Ira,CSE,8.6) (12,Kabir,ECE,7.4) (13,Lata,CSE,9.2) (14,Manoj,MECH,6.8) (15,Nisha,ECE,8.0) (16,Omar,CSE,7.9) course: (C1,DBMS,4) (C2,OS,3) (C3,Networks,4) enroll: (11,C1,82) (11,C2,91) (12,C1,74) (12,C3,68) (13,C1,95) (13,C2,88) (15,C1,74) (15,C2,60) (16,C1,88) (16,C3,NULL)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This view is updatable: it selects a subset of rows and a subset of columns of one base table, so each view row maps to exactly one base row. An UPDATE through the view therefore reaches the underlying rows, and the WHERE in the view definition limits it to the CSE students.

Step by step.

  1. The view exposes only rows with dept = 'CSE': rolls 11, 13 and 16.
  2. Selection and projection of a single table preserve updatability, so the update is propagated.
  3. The statement names no rows, so every row in the view is updated.
  4. 3 rows of student change.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Adding up all six students, or assuming a view is never updatable. Only the CSE rows are visible through the view, so only they can be reached.

Takeaway. Updatable view = one view row ↔ one base row; an update through it hits exactly the rows the view exposes.

DB-U2-Q50MSQhard

Which of the following statements about views, triggers and constraints are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A view whose definition uses GROUP BY is not updatable in standard SQL.
  2. WITH CHECK OPTION turns the view's predicate into a condition on every write made through the view.
  3. An INSTEAD OF trigger fires only after the row has been written to the base table.
  4. A view may rename base columns, so the same base column can appear under different names in different views.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Updatability requires a one-to-one map from view rows to base rows. Grouping destroys that map because one view row summarises many base rows, so such a view is read-only. WITH CHECK OPTION adds a write-time predicate. A view is a named query, so it may rename and reorder columns freely — the name in the view is what users see.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: a grouped row is an aggregate over many base rows, so an update has no single target row.
  2. (B) True — that is its definition.
  3. (C) False: INSTEAD OF replaces the statement; nothing has been written when it runs.
  4. (D) True: SELECT roll AS id, cgpa AS score FROM student is a legal view definition.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true; (C) is false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) inverts the firing semantics: an INSTEAD OF trigger runs in place of the write, so no base-table row exists yet.

Common misconception. Assuming any view can be written through. Grouping, DISTINCT and unions all break the one-to-one mapping and make the view read-only.

Takeaway. Grouped view = read-only; WITH CHECK OPTION = write-time predicate; views may rename columns.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 3 — Database Normalization
1nf-2nf-3nf-bcnf · 10
DB-U3-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following is the exact condition for a relation to be in first normal form (1NF)?

  1. Every attribute of every tuple holds a single atomic value from the attribute's domain.
  2. No non-prime attribute is partially dependent on a candidate key.
  3. No non-prime attribute is transitively dependent on a candidate key.
  4. Every determinant of a non-trivial functional dependency is a superkey.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 1NF is the weakest of the normal forms and the only one that talks about the shape of a value rather than about dependencies. It requires the relational model's basic assumption: every cell holds one atomic value from the column's domain, so there are no repeating groups, no sets, and no nested relations.

Step by step.

  1. 1NF = atomic values only. A cell such as {98111, 98122} in a phone column breaks it; the fix is one row per phone number.
  2. Option (B) is the definition of 2NF, which presupposes 1NF and adds a constraint on partial dependencies.
  3. Option (C) is the definition of 3NF, one step further up.
  4. Option (D) is the definition of BCNF.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is 2NF, which is a statement about non-prime attributes and keys, not about atomic values.
  • (C) is 3NF.
  • (D) is BCNF.

Common misconception. Treating 1NF as a trivial form that "every table satisfies anyway". A table with a comma-separated list, a repeating group, or an array column is not in 1NF, and every higher form is then undefined for it.

Takeaway. Learn the ladder in one line each: 1NF atomic values, 2NF no partial dependency, 3NF no transitive dependency, BCNF every determinant is a superkey.

DB-U3-Q07MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) has the single candidate key {A, B} and satisfies A -> C. Which one of the following correctly explains why R is not in 2NF?

  1. C is a prime attribute, and prime attributes may not be functionally determined by anything.
  2. The dependency A → C is transitive, and 2NF forbids transitive dependencies.
  3. C is not determined by {A, B} at all, so the key cannot identify a tuple.
  4. C is a non-prime attribute that is determined by A alone, a proper subset of the candidate key {A, B}.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 2NF forbids partial dependencies: a non-prime attribute must not depend on a proper subset of a candidate key. Here the key is {A, B}, so both A and B are prime and both C and D are non-prime.

Step by step.

  1. Prime attributes: A and B, because they belong to the candidate key {A, B}.
  2. Non-prime attributes: C and D.
  3. A → C has a left-hand side {A} that is a proper subset of the key, and its right-hand side C is non-prime.
  4. That is precisely a partial dependency, so the relation violates 2NF. The fix is to split off R₁(A, C) and keep R₂(A, B, D).

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: a prime attribute may perfectly well be determined by something, including by a proper subset of another key.
  • (B) confuses 2NF with 3NF; transitivity is the 3NF concern, and it is measured against non-prime attributes determining other non-prime attributes.
  • (C) is false: C is determined by {A, B}, since A → C implies AB → C by augmentation.

Common misconception. Checking whether "a part of the key determines something" without checking that the something is non-prime. If the right-hand side is prime, the dependency is not a 2NF violation at all.

Takeaway. Two conditions must both hold for a 2NF violation: the left-hand side is a proper subset of a candidate key, and the right-hand side is non-prime.

DB-U3-Q11MCQhard

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {AB -> C, C -> D}. Which one of the following is the highest normal form that R satisfies?

  1. 1NF only
  2. 2NF
  3. 3NF
  4. BCNF
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Test the forms bottom-up. Each one adds a condition, so the highest form satisfied is the last test that passes before the first that fails.

Step by step.

  1. Find the keys. A⁺ = {A}, B⁺ = {B}, C⁺ = {C, D}, D⁺ = {D}; (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C, D}. So AB is the only candidate key, A and B are prime, C and D are non-prime.
  2. 1NF: every value is atomic — satisfied.
  3. 2NF: is any non-prime attribute determined by a proper subset of AB? The only dependency with a smaller left-hand side is C → D, and C is not a subset of the key. So there is no partial dependency — 2NF holds.
  4. 3NF: for C → D, C is not a superkey and D is not prime, so the 3NF condition fails. The relation is not in 3NF.
  5. BCNF fails for the same dependency, since C is not a superkey. The highest form is therefore 2NF.

Answer: Option (B) — 2NF, and not 3NF.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is too low: 2NF is satisfied, so the relation is better than 1NF only.
  • (C) fails because C → D is a transitive dependency on a non-prime attribute.
  • (D) fails for the same reason, and BCNF is stricter than 3NF anyway.

Common misconception. Declaring the relation to be in 3NF because "the key determines everything". A relation can be 2NF and still have a non-prime attribute determining another non-prime attribute, which is exactly the 3NF violation here.

Takeaway. Compute the keys first, then label every attribute prime or non-prime, then walk the ladder one form at a time. Skip no rung.

DB-U3-Q16MCQeasy

A relation R is in Boyce-Codd normal form (BCNF) exactly when

  1. every attribute is atomic and no attribute holds a set of values.
  2. no non-prime attribute is transitively dependent on a candidate key.
  3. every candidate key is a superkey.
  4. for every non-trivial functional dependency X → Y that holds in R, X is a superkey of R.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. BCNF strengthens 3NF by dropping 3NF's escape clause. 3NF allows a dependency whose left-hand side is not a superkey provided the right-hand side is a prime attribute; BCNF allows no such exception. Every non-trivial dependency must have a superkey on its left.

Step by step.

  1. Take every non-trivial FD X → Y that holds in the relation.
  2. Ask whether X is a superkey — that is, whether X⁺ is the whole relation.
  3. If the answer is yes for all of them, the relation is in BCNF; a single no means it is not.
  4. Option (D) states exactly this. (A) is 1NF, (B) is 3NF stated for non-prime attributes, and (C) is a consequence of the definition rather than the definition itself.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes 1NF.
  • (B) describes 3NF, which is weaker than BCNF.
  • (C) is true of every relation by definition — a candidate key is a minimal superkey — so it cannot be the BCNF test.

Common misconception. Quoting BCNF as "no transitive dependency". That is 3NF. The difference shows up precisely when a non-superkey determinant has a prime attribute on its right-hand side: 3NF permits it, BCNF does not.

Takeaway. BCNF = every determinant of a non-trivial FD is a superkey. No exceptions, no prime-attribute escape.

DB-U3-Q21MSQhard

Which of the following statements about 3NF and BCNF decompositions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)
