  1. Every relation has a decomposition into 3NF that is both lossless and dependency preserving.
  2. Every relation has a decomposition into BCNF that is lossless.
  3. Every relation has a decomposition into BCNF that is both lossless and dependency preserving.
  4. Every relation that is in BCNF is also in 3NF.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two guarantees are not symmetric. 3NF can always deliver both properties at once, by the synthesis algorithm. BCNF can always deliver losslessness, but may be forced to give up dependency preservation.

Step by step.

  1. (A) 3NF synthesis from a minimal cover produces relations that are lossless and preserve every dependency, adding a relation over a candidate key if none of the synthesised relations contains one — true.
  2. (B) The BCNF decomposition algorithm splits on any violating dependency and always yields a lossless join — true.
  3. (C) is false. The classic counterexample is R(A, B, C) with {AB → C, C → B}: the BCNF decomposition into (A, C) and (B, C) loses AB → C.
  4. (D) BCNF is strictly stronger than 3NF: its condition implies the 3NF condition, so every BCNF relation is in 3NF — true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) overstates BCNF. When the two goals conflict, BCNF wins on redundancy and loses on dependency preservation, which is why 3NF is often the practical stopping point.

Common misconception. Believing BCNF is always preferable because it is "higher". A BCNF decomposition that drops a dependency forces every update to be checked by an expensive join, which is often worse than the redundancy 3NF leaves behind.

Takeaway. 3NF = lossless + dependency preserving, always. BCNF = lossless, always; dependency preserving, only sometimes.

DB-U3-Q36MCQeasy

A relation R is in third normal form (3NF) exactly when, for every non-trivial functional dependency X → A that holds in R,

  1. X is a superkey of R.
  2. A is a prime attribute of R.
  3. X is a superkey of R, or A is a prime attribute of R.
  4. X contains no prime attribute of R.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 3NF relaxes BCNF with one escape clause. BCNF demands that every non-trivial determinant be a superkey; 3NF allows a determinant that is not a superkey provided the determined attribute is prime.

Step by step.

  1. The strict condition alone is the BCNF definition, so option (A) describes BCNF, not 3NF.
  2. The prime-attribute condition alone is too weak on its own and does not state 3NF either.
  3. The correct statement is the disjunction: for each non-trivial X → A, either X is a superkey or A is prime.
  4. Because a superkey condition implies the prime condition is irrelevant once satisfied, 3NF is a strict weakening of BCNF.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is exactly BCNF and is therefore too strong.
  • (B) ignores the superkey route entirely and would wrongly reject relations whose determinants are keys.
  • (D) is not part of any normal-form definition.

Common misconception. Reciting 3NF as "no transitive dependency" and then applying it literally. The usable test is the disjunction above; the transitive-dependency phrasing is only an informal description.

Takeaway. 3NF = BCNF + the prime-attribute escape. Remember the escape, because every 3NF-but-not-BCNF question turns on it.

DB-U3-Q37MCQeasy

A relation R is in 2NF but not in 3NF. Which one of the following must be TRUE?

  1. Some non-prime attribute is transitively dependent on a candidate key.
  2. Some non-prime attribute is partially dependent on a candidate key.
  3. Some determinant of a non-trivial dependency is not a superkey, and its right-hand side is a prime attribute.
  4. R has at least two candidate keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 2NF removes partial dependencies of non-prime attributes on part of a key. 3NF additionally removes transitive dependencies of non-prime attributes on a key. Being in 2NF but not 3NF therefore means the remaining defect is transitive, not partial.

Step by step.

  1. Since R is in 2NF, no non-prime attribute depends on a proper subset of a candidate key.
  2. Since R is not in 3NF, some non-trivial X → A has X not a superkey and A non-prime.
  3. Because A is non-prime and determined by a non-superkey, it reaches A only through another attribute set — that is precisely a transitive dependency on a candidate key.
  4. Hence option (A) must hold.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes a violation of 2NF, which contradicts the premise.
  • (C) describes 3NF-but-not-BCNF: there the right-hand side is prime, so 3NF still holds.
  • (D) is not forced; a relation with a single candidate key can be in 2NF and not 3NF.

Common misconception. Reading "not in 3NF" as "not in 2NF". The forms are nested — every 3NF relation is in 2NF — so a 3NF failure leaves the 2NF guarantee intact.

Takeaway. Read a normal-form gap as a diagnosis: 2NF fixes partial dependencies, 3NF fixes transitive ones, BCNF fixes everything else.

DB-U3-Q38NATmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies F = {A → BC, CD → E, B → D, E → A}. How many of the five attributes of R are non-prime?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An attribute is prime when it belongs to at least one candidate key. So the only way to count non-prime attributes is to enumerate candidate keys and take the complement of their union.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E}: A → BC, B → D, CD → E. So {A} is a candidate key.
  2. E⁺ = {E, A, B, C, D}: E → A, then A → BC, B → D. So {E} is a candidate key.
  3. (BC)⁺ = {B, C, D, E, A}: B → D, CD → E, E → A. So {B, C} is a candidate key.
  4. (CD)⁺ = {C, D, E, A, B}, so {C, D} is a candidate key.
  5. (CE)⁺ = {C, E, A, B, D}, so {C, E} is a candidate key.
  6. (BE)⁺ = {B, E, A, C, D}, so {B, E} is a candidate key.
  7. Union of all candidate keys = {A, B, C, D, E}. Every attribute is prime, so 0 attributes are non-prime.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Assuming every relation has some non-prime attribute, so answering 1 or more without checking. Here the dependency structure is so tightly connected that each attribute sits inside some key.

Takeaway. When every attribute is prime, the 3NF prime-attribute escape applies to every dependency, so the relation is automatically in 3NF — a useful shortcut worth checking before a long normal-form analysis.

DB-U3-Q39MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about second normal form are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 2NF forbids a non-prime attribute from depending on a proper subset of any candidate key.
  2. A relation in 1NF whose only candidate key is a single attribute is automatically in 2NF.
  3. Every relation in BCNF is also in 2NF.
  4. Every relation in 2NF is also in 3NF.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 2NF is about partial dependencies: a non-prime attribute must depend on a whole candidate key, never on part of one. Partial dependency can only arise when a candidate key has at least two attributes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of 2NF, stated for every candidate key rather than just the primary key.
  2. (B) follows because a single-attribute key has no non-empty proper subset, so no partial dependency is possible.
  3. (C) follows because BCNF forbids every non-superkey determinant, which includes the partial ones; BCNF is the strongest of the three forms and implies the weaker two.
  4. (D) is false: a relation can be in 2NF and still carry a transitive dependency, which is exactly what 3NF removes.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (D) reverses the containment: 3NF ⊂ 2NF ⊂ 1NF, so 3NF implies 2NF and never the other way round.

Common misconception. Checking partial dependency against the primary key only. 2NF is defined against every candidate key, and a relation can be clean on one key and dirty on another.

Takeaway. Nested order 1NF ⊂ 2NF ⊂ 3NF ⊂ BCNF; each step removes one specific defect.

DB-U3-Q40MCQhard

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies F = {A → B, BC → D, D → E, E → C}. Which one of the following is the highest normal form that R satisfies?

  1. 1NF only
  2. 2NF
  3. 3NF
  4. BCNF
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Test the forms from the strongest down, but you need the candidate keys first: whether a dependency is partial depends on which attributes form a key.

Step by step.

  1. A appears on no right-hand side, so A is in every key. A⁺ = {A, B}, so {A} is not a key.
  2. (AC)⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E}: A → B, BC → D, D → E. So {A, C} is a key.
  3. (AD)⁺ = {A, B, D, E, C}: D → E, E → C, then BC → D. So {A, D} is a key. Likewise {A, E} is a key.
  4. Candidate keys are {A, C}, {A, D} and {A, E}; the non-prime attribute is B alone.
  5. 2NF: A → B determines the non-prime B from A, a proper subset of the key {A, C}. That is a partial dependency, so 2NF fails.
  6. Since 2NF fails, 3NF and BCNF fail too, and the highest form satisfied is 1NF.

Answer: Option (A) — 1NF only.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would require B to depend on the whole of every key, but A → B gives it from A alone.
  • (C) and (D) cannot hold once 2NF has failed, because the forms are nested.

Common misconception. Spotting that all three keys contain A and concluding the relation is fine. A shared attribute in every key makes partial dependencies more likely, not less.

Takeaway. Find the candidate keys first; without them "partial dependency" is not even a well-defined test.

closure-of-attribute-sets-keys · 10
DB-U3-Q03MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A -> B, B -> C, C -> D}. Which one of the following sets is the only candidate key of R?

  1. {A, B}
  2. {A, C}
  3. {A}
  4. {A, B, C}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A candidate key is a minimal attribute set whose closure is the whole relation. Compute the closure by repeatedly applying the dependencies, then check minimality: no proper subset may close to R.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺ = {A}. Apply A → B to get B; then B → C gives C; then C → D gives D.
  2. So A⁺ = {A, B, C, D}: the whole relation is determined by A alone.
  3. {A} is therefore a superkey, and it has no non-empty proper subset, so it is minimal.
  4. Every set containing A, such as {A, B} or {A, B, C}, is a superkey but not minimal. No set without A closes: B, C and D each determine only attributes further down the chain.

Answer: Option (C) — {A}.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (D) contain A, so they are superkeys; they are not minimal, and a candidate key must be minimal.
  • (B) contains A as well, and C adds nothing: A alone already reaches every attribute.

Common misconception. Answering {A, B} because the schema "looks like" it needs two attributes, or missing the chain because only one dependency is applied per pass. Keep applying the rules until nothing new appears.

Takeaway. In a chain A → B → C → D the head of the chain is the only key. Chains always collapse to their first attribute.

DB-U3-Q08NATmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {AB -> C, C -> D, D -> A}. How many candidate keys does R have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An attribute that never appears on the right-hand side of any dependency can never be derived, so it must be present in every candidate key. Here only B has that property, which narrows the search to sets containing B.

Step by step.

  1. Right-hand sides are C, D and A, so B never appears there: B is in every key.
  2. B⁺ = {B}, so B alone is not a key; test the pairs that contain B.
  3. (AB)⁺: AB → C, then C → D, then D → A gives {A, B, C, D}. AB is a candidate key.
  4. (BC)⁺: C → D, D → A, and then AB → C is already satisfied — the closure is {A, B, C, D}. BC is a candidate key.
  5. (BD)⁺: D → A gives A, and now AB → C gives C, so the closure is {A, B, C, D}. BD is a candidate key.
  6. No larger set is minimal, and the three keys found are not supersets of one another. The count is 3.

Answer: 3 — the candidate keys are AB, BC and BD.

Common misconception. Stopping at the first key found, or missing BD because it needs a two-step derivation: D → A first, and only then AB → C. Closure computation must be run to exhaustion.

Takeaway. Find the attributes that appear on no right-hand side — they are in every key — then grow each of them with one more attribute at a time until the closure is the full relation.

DB-U3-Q13NATeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A -> B, B -> C, C -> D}. How many attributes are there in the closure A+?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. X⁺ is the set of all attributes determined by X. It is computed by starting with X and repeatedly adding the right-hand side of every dependency whose left-hand side is already inside the set.

Step by step.

  1. Start with A⁺ = {A}.
  2. A → B applies, so add B: {A, B}.
  3. B → C applies, so add C: {A, B, C}.
  4. C → D applies, so add D: {A, B, C, D}.
  5. No further dependency applies, so the closure holds all 4 attributes.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Counting only the attributes reached in one pass, or forgetting that the starting attribute itself belongs to its closure. A⁺ always contains A.

Takeaway. A chain A → B → C → D sweeps up every attribute downstream, so the closure of the head is the whole relation.

DB-U3-Q18NAThard

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies F = {AB -> C, C -> D, D -> E, E -> A}. How many candidate keys does R have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. B appears on no right-hand side, so B is in every candidate key. Every key is therefore B together with the smallest extra attribute or attributes needed to close the cycle A → C → D → E → A.

Step by step.

  1. B⁺ = {B}, so B alone is not a key; B needs a partner.
  2. (AB)⁺: AB → C, C → D, D → E, E → A — the closure is all five. AB is a key.
  3. (BC)⁺: C → D, D → E, E → A, then AB → C is already satisfied — all five. BC is a key.
  4. (BD)⁺: D → E, E → A, then AB → C — all five. BD is a key.
  5. (BE)⁺: E → A, then AB → C, C → D — all five. BE is a key.
  6. None of the four contains another, so all four are minimal. The count is 4.

Answer: 4 — the candidate keys are AB, BC, BD and BE.

Common misconception. Assuming the cycle A → C → D → E → A produces one key. A cycle lets you enter it at any point, and each entry point gives a different minimal key: that is exactly why there are four here and not one.

Takeaway. Locate the attributes that appear on no right-hand side, pair them with each possible entry into the cycle, and keep only the pairs whose closure is the whole relation.

DB-U3-Q23NATeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A -> B, B -> C, C -> D}. How many superkeys does R have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A superkey is any attribute set that contains a candidate key. Once the candidate keys are known, counting superkeys is a counting problem on the remaining attributes.

Step by step.

  1. Keys: A⁺ = {A, B, C, D}, and no attribute alone or in a pair without A closes, so the only candidate key is {A}.
  2. Every superkey must contain A; the other three attributes B, C, D are free to be in or out.
  3. The number of superkeys is therefore 2³ = 8.
  4. Check by listing: {A}, {AB}, {AC}, {AD}, {ABC}, {ABD}, {ACD}, {ABCD} — eight.

Answer: 8.

Common misconception. Counting 2⁴ = 16 by letting A vary as well, or counting only the four subsets that are not supersets. A superkey must contain the whole candidate key, so A is not free.

Takeaway. With a single candidate key of size k in an n-attribute relation the superkey count is 2^(n − k); here 2³ = 8.

DB-U3-Q31MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) has exactly two candidate keys, {A} and {B, C}. Which attributes of R are prime?

  1. A only
  2. A and B
  3. A and C
  4. A, B and C
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An attribute is prime when it belongs to at least one candidate key. It need not belong to every key, and it need not be a key on its own.

Step by step.

  1. A is in the candidate key {A}, so A is prime.
  2. B is in the candidate key {B, C}, so B is prime.
  3. C is in the same candidate key, so C is prime.
  4. All three attributes are prime.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) counts only the single-attribute key and forgets the composite one.
  • (B) and (C) each drop one member of {B, C}, but membership of a key is enough on its own.

Common misconception. Assuming a prime attribute must be able to identify a tuple by itself. Prime means "part of some key", nothing more; B alone determines nothing.

Takeaway. Collect every candidate key first, then take their union — that union is exactly the set of prime attributes.

DB-U3-Q32MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {AB → C, C → D, D → B}. Which one of the following is a candidate key of R?

  1. {A, B, C}
  2. {A, C}
  3. {B, D}
  4. {C, D}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A candidate key is a minimal superkey. First find the attributes that can never be produced by any dependency — they must appear in every key — then close each smallest candidate.

Step by step.

  1. A appears on no right-hand side, so every key must contain A. A⁺ = {A}, so A alone is not a key.
  2. (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C, D}: AB → C, then C → D. So {A, B} is a key.
  3. (AC)⁺ = {A, C, D, B}: C → D, then D → B. So {A, C} is a key.
  4. (AD)⁺ = {A, D, B, C}: D → B, then AB → C. So {A, D} is a key.
  5. The candidate keys are therefore {A, B}, {A, C} and {A, D}; {A, C} is the only option among them.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) {A, B, C} is a superkey but not minimal — C is unnecessary because {A, B} already determines everything.
  • (C) (BD)⁺ = {B, D}: nothing determines A or C from B and D.
  • (D) (CD)⁺ = {C, D, B}: A is still missing.

Common misconception. Stopping at the first superkey found. {A, B, C} closes to everything but fails minimality, which is the whole difference between a superkey and a key.

Takeaway. Start from the attributes that appear in no right-hand side; they are forced into every key and cut the search down immediately.

DB-U3-Q33NATeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A → B, B → C, C → D, D → A}. How many superkeys does R have?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A superkey is any attribute set whose closure is the whole relation. Count keys by finding the minimal ones first, then count every set that contains at least one of them.

Step by step.

  1. The dependencies form a single cycle A → B → C → D → A, so from any one attribute the closure grows to all four.
  2. A⁺ = {A, B, C, D}, B⁺ = {B, C, D, A}, C⁺ = {C, D, A, B}, D⁺ = {D, A, B, C}.
  3. Every single attribute is therefore a candidate key, and no proper subset of a singleton exists.
  4. A superkey is any non-empty set containing at least one attribute — which is every non-empty subset of a four-attribute relation.
  5. Count: 2⁴ − 1 = 15.

Answer: 15

Common misconception. Answering 4 by counting only the candidate keys. The question asks for superkeys, and every set that contains a key is one.

Takeaway. When every attribute is a key, every non-empty subset is a superkey — the count is 2ⁿ − 1.

DB-U3-Q34MSQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {AB → C, C → D, D → A}. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. {A, B} is a candidate key of R.
  2. {B, C} is a candidate key of R.
  3. {A} is a superkey of R.
  4. D is a prime attribute of R.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Candidate keys here are all pairs containing B, because B appears on no right-hand side and so must be in every key. Prime attributes are the union of all candidate keys.

Step by step.

  1. B is never determined, so every key contains B. B⁺ = {B}, so {B} is not a key.
  2. (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C, D} — a candidate key.
  3. (BC)⁺ = {B, C, D, A} — a candidate key.
  4. (BD)⁺ = {B, D, A, C} — a candidate key.
  5. A⁺ = {A}, so {A} is not even a superkey.
  6. Prime attributes = {A, B} ∪ {B, C} ∪ {B, D} = {A, B, C, D}; in particular D is prime, through the key {B, D}.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true; (C) is false.

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (C) A determines nothing at all: the only dependency with A on the left is AB → C, which needs B.

Common misconception. Concluding that D is non-prime because it sits on the right-hand side of C → D. Being determined by something says nothing about prime status; only key membership does.

Takeaway. Enumerate candidate keys, take their union for the prime attributes, and answer superkey questions from the key list rather than from the arrows.

DB-U3-Q35MCQhard

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies F = {AB → C, BC → D, CD → E, DE → A}. How many superkeys does R have?

  1. 10
  2. 12
  3. 13
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count candidate keys first, then count the attribute sets that contain at least one of them. Where keys overlap, inclusion–exclusion removes the double counts.

Step by step.

  1. No single attribute determines all five: A⁺ = {A}, B⁺ = {B}, C⁺ = {C}, D⁺ = {D}, E⁺ = {E}.
  2. (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E}: AB → C, BC → D, CD → E. So {A, B} is a key.
  3. (BC)⁺ = {B, C, D, E, A}: BC → D, CD → E, DE → A. So {B, C} is a key.
  4. (BDE)⁺ = {B, D, E, A, C}: DE → A, then AB → C. So {B, D, E} is a third key — easy to miss.
  5. No other set closes to all five: (CD)⁺ = {C, D, E, A} is missing B, and (AC)⁺ = {A, C}.
  6. Count the supersets of each key: {A, B} gives 2³ = 8, {B, C} gives 8, {B, D, E} gives 2² = 4.
  7. Remove the double counts: sets containing both {A, B} and {B, C} number 4, both {A, B} and {B, D, E} number 2, both {B, C} and {B, D, E} number 2, and the set containing all three is counted back once.
  8. 8 + 8 + 4 − 4 − 2 − 2 + 1 = 13.

Answer: Option (C) — 13 superkeys.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 10 ignores the third key {B, D, E} altogether.
  • (B) 12 is what you get if the two overlapping keys are treated as disjoint and the triple overlap is dropped.
  • (D) 16 is every non-empty subset of the five attributes, which would need every attribute to be a key.

Common misconception. Stopping after the first two keys found. {B, D, E} only closes to everything through the chain DE → A → C, and missing it changes the answer.

Takeaway. Superkey counts need the complete key list plus inclusion–exclusion; never count from the number of dependencies.

functional-dependencies-armstrong-axioms · 10
DB-U3-Q01MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) is subject to no constraint other than its schema. Which one of the following functional dependencies is trivial?

  1. A → B
  2. AB → C
  3. A → AB
  4. ABC → A
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A functional dependency X → Y is trivial when Y ⊆ X: the right-hand side is already part of the left-hand side, so the dependency holds in every relation with that schema, whatever the data. Triviality is a syntactic test on the two attribute sets, not a claim about what the data happens to satisfy.

Step by step.

  1. A → B: B ⊆ A is false (B is a different attribute), so this dependency constrains the data and is non-trivial.
  2. AB → C: C ⊆ AB is false, so it is non-trivial.
  3. A → AB: the right-hand side contains B, which is not in A, so it is non-trivial. It happens to be true whenever A → B holds, but being implied is not the same as being trivial.
  4. ABC → A: A ⊆ ABC is true, so every tuple satisfies it automatically. This one is trivial.

Answer: Option (D) — ABC → A.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A → B forbids two tuples that agree on A and differ on B — a real constraint.
  • (B) AB → C is the ordinary kind of dependency a schema is built on.
  • (C) A → AB is valid only when A → B is, so it is not automatic.

Common misconception. Calling A → AB trivial because it looks obviously true. It is true only under an extra assumption; a trivial dependency needs no assumption at all. The other frequent slip is testing the left-hand side against the right-hand side instead of the other way round.

Takeaway. Ask one question only: is every attribute on the right already on the left? If yes, the dependency is trivial and tells you nothing about the data.

DB-U3-Q06MCQmedium

Let F = {A -> B, B -> C, C -> D} be the set of dependencies of R(A, B, C, D). Which one of the following dependencies is NOT in F+?

  1. D → A
  2. A → C
  3. B → D
  4. A → D
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. F⁺ is the closure of F: every dependency implied by F. A dependency X → Y is implied exactly when Y ⊆ X⁺, so the whole question reduces to computing attribute closures.

Step by step.

  1. D⁺ = {D}: no dependency has D on its left-hand side, so nothing can be derived. D → A is therefore not implied.
  2. A⁺ = {A, B, C, D}: A → B, then B → C, then C → D. So A → C and A → D are both implied.
  3. B⁺ = {B, C, D}: B → C then C → D. So B → D is implied.
  4. Only the first candidate fails, so (A) is the answer.

Answer: Option (A) — D → A is not implied.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) follows by transitivity through B.
  • (C) follows by transitivity through C.
  • (D) is the transitive chain of all three dependencies.

Common misconception. Assuming dependencies run backwards as well as forwards. Transitivity is one-directional: A → D says nothing about D → A. Reversing an arrow is the single most common error in closure questions.

Takeaway. To test any dependency X → Y, compute X⁺ and look for Y inside it. Never reason from the right-hand side.

DB-U3-Q10MSQeasy

Armstrong's axiom system for functional dependencies consists of three inference rules. Which of the following are among those three axioms? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Reflexivity: if Y ⊆ X then X → Y.
  2. Augmentation: if X → Y then XZ → YZ for every attribute set Z.
  3. Pseudotransitivity: if X → Y and YW → Z then XW → Z.
  4. Transitivity: if X → Y and Y → Z then X → Z.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Armstrong's three axioms are reflexivity, augmentation and transitivity. They are sound (they never derive a dependency that fails in some legal instance) and complete (every implied dependency can be derived from them). Everything else in the literature is a derived rule.

Step by step.

  1. Reflexivity: a set determines its own subsets — an axiom.
  2. Augmentation: adding the same attributes to both sides preserves the dependency — an axiom.
  3. Transitivity: chaining two dependencies — the third axiom.
  4. Pseudotransitivity is sound, and it can be proved from augmentation and transitivity, but it is not one of the three axioms. So (C) is not an axiom, while (A), (B) and (D) are.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is a derived rule: it follows from (A), (B) and (D) rather than being assumed.

Common misconception. Listing pseudotransitivity, union, decomposition or additivity as axioms. They are all valid rules, but a valid rule is not necessarily an axiom — the system needs only three assumptions to generate all the rest.

Takeaway. Three axioms, three words: reflexivity, augmentation, transitivity. Any other named rule is derived.

DB-U3-Q14NATmedium

Let F = {A -> B, B -> C, A -> C} be a set of dependencies on R(A, B, C). How many functional dependencies are there in a minimal cover of F?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A minimal (canonical) cover is an equivalent set of dependencies in which every right-hand side is a single attribute, no dependency is redundant, and no left-hand side can be shrunk. Here every right-hand side is already single, and every left-hand side is a single attribute that cannot be shrunk further.

Step by step.

  1. Right-hand sides are already singletons, so there is nothing to split.
  2. Is A → B redundant? Remove it and compute A⁺ from {B → C, A → C}: the closure is {A, C}, which does not contain B. So it is needed.
  3. Is B → C redundant? Remove it and the remaining set gives B⁺ = {B}, so it is needed.
  4. Is A → C redundant? Remove it: from {A → B, B → C} we get A⁺ = {A, B, C}, which contains C, so it is redundant and can be dropped.
  5. The minimal cover is {A → B, B → C}: two dependencies.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Reporting three because the original set had three. A minimal cover is not a subset of the original in general — it is an equivalent set with no redundancy — and here the transitive dependency must go.

Takeaway. To minimise: make right-hand sides single, then remove each dependency in turn and test whether the rest still derives it.

DB-U3-Q19MSQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies F = {A -> B, B -> C}. Which of the following functional dependencies are in F+? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A → C
  2. AB → C
  3. A → BC
  4. C → A
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. X → Y belongs to F⁺ exactly when Y ⊆ X⁺. Compute the closure of each left-hand side and test containment.

Step by step.

  1. A⁺ = {A, B, C}: A → B then B → C. So A → C holds, and A → BC holds too.
  2. (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C}, so AB → C holds. It also follows directly by augmentation from B → C.
  3. C⁺ = {C}: C is on the right-hand side only, so nothing can be derived from it and C → A fails.
  4. Hence (A), (B) and (C) are in F⁺; (D) is not.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) reverses the direction of the chain; a dependency never implies its own converse.

Common misconception. Missing AB → C because "AB is bigger than the left-hand side of the original dependency". Augmentation makes any left-hand side bigger and keeps the dependency valid.

Takeaway. Closure test, every time: grow the left-hand side until it stops growing, then check that the right-hand side is inside.

DB-U3-Q26MCQeasy

Which one of the following is NOT a valid inference rule for functional dependencies?

  1. Union: if X → Y and X → Z then X → YZ.
  2. Decomposition: if X → YZ then X → Y.
  3. Converse: if X → Y then Y → X.
  4. Pseudotransitivity: if X → Y and YW → Z then XW → Z.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A functional dependency is a one-way statement about values: equal values on the left force equal values on the right. It never licenses the reverse. Reversing the arrow is the single most common invalid inference in this unit.

Step by step.

  1. Union and decomposition are derived rules that follow from Armstrong's augmentation and transitivity; both are sound.
  2. Pseudotransitivity follows from augmentation and transitivity; it is also sound.
  3. The converse claims that X → Y implies Y → X. Take R(A, B) with the single tuple (1, 1) against (2, 1): A → B holds but B → A fails, because B = 1 maps to two different A values.
  4. So the converse is the invalid rule.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Union is valid: from X → Y and X → Z, augment and combine to get X → YZ.
  • (B) Decomposition is valid because a set of attributes determines each of its members.
  • (D) Pseudotransitivity is valid: augment X → Y to XW → YW, then transitively compose with YW → Z.

Common misconception. Treating → as if it were an equation. Augmentation adds attributes on both sides, which looks like reversibility, but it is a completely different operation from swapping the two sides.

Takeaway. You may add attributes to both sides, or chain arrows, but you may never reverse an arrow.

DB-U3-Q27MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about Armstrong's three axioms is TRUE?

  1. The axioms are sound but not complete, so some dependencies in F⁺ cannot be derived from them.
  2. The axioms are both sound and complete: every dependency they derive holds in every relation satisfying F, and every dependency that holds can be derived.
  3. The axioms generate exactly the dependencies listed in F and never anything new.
  4. Completeness means the axioms never derive a dependency that does not actually hold.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two separate properties are at stake. Soundness means every derived dependency is genuinely implied by F. Completeness means every dependency implied by F can be derived. Armstrong's reflexivity, augmentation and transitivity are sound and complete.

Step by step.

  1. Soundness: each of the three axioms is easily checked against the definition of a functional dependency.
  2. Completeness is the harder half; it is what guarantees that closure computation by repeated application of the axioms reaches all of F⁺.
  3. Together they mean the closure F⁺ is exactly the set of derivable dependencies.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Denies completeness, which Armstrong's system has.
  • (C) Denies that anything new is derived, which would make transitivity useless.
  • (D) Describes soundness, not completeness — the two words are swapped.

Common misconception. Using the two words interchangeably. Soundness is "nothing wrong gets derived"; completeness is "nothing right is left underived".

Takeaway. Sound + complete is what lets a closure computation stand in for reasoning about F⁺ directly.

DB-U3-Q28MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies F = {AB → C, C → A}. Which one of the following functional dependencies is in F⁺?

  1. B → C
  2. A → C
  3. C → B
  4. BC → A
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dependency X → Y is in F⁺ exactly when Y ⊆ X⁺. So the whole question is four small closure computations.

Step by step.

  1. B⁺ = {B} — no dependency has B alone on its left, so B → C is out.
  2. A⁺ = {A} — AB → C needs B as well, so A → C is out.
  3. C⁺ = {C, A} — C → A applies, then AB → C needs B. So C → B is out.
  4. (BC)⁺ = {B, C, A} — C → A gives A, and now AB → C is already satisfied. Hence BC → A holds.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) have left-hand sides that determine nothing beyond themselves.
  • (C) would need B in C⁺; the only dependency producing B is AB → C, which produces C, not B.

Common misconception. Reading AB → C backwards as "C determines A and B". The given C → A supplies only A.

Takeaway. To test membership, compute the closure of the left-hand side only — never reason about the arrow by analogy.

DB-U3-Q29MSQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies F = {A → B, B → C}. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. F is a minimal cover of itself.
  2. A → C is in F⁺.
  3. C → A is in F⁺.
  4. The dependency A → B is redundant in F.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A minimal cover is a set of dependencies with singleton right-hand sides, no extraneous attribute on any left-hand side, and no dependency that is implied by the rest. A dependency is redundant when it survives in the closure of the others.

Step by step.

  1. Both right-hand sides are already singletons, and both left-hand sides are single attributes, so no attribute can be extraneous.
  2. Drop A → B: with only B → C left, A⁺ = {A}, so A → B is not recoverable — it is needed.
  3. Drop B → C: with only A → B left, B⁺ = {B}, so B → C is needed too.
  4. Hence F is already minimal, and by transitivity A⁺ = {A, B, C}, so A → C holds.
  5. C⁺ = {C}, so C → A does not hold.

Answer: Options (A) and (B) are true; (C) and (D) are false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) inverts the chain: the arrows run A → B → C, never back.
  • (D) removing A → B leaves nothing that produces B, so the dependency is essential.

Common misconception. Believing a minimal cover must be unique. Several minimal covers can exist for the same F; here there happens to be only one.

Takeaway. Minimal means no redundancy, not minimum cardinality over all possible equivalent sets in every case.

DB-U3-Q30NAThard

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A → BC, B → D, C → D}. How many functional dependencies are there in a minimal cover of F?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Building a minimal cover has three passes: split every right-hand side into singletons, remove extraneous left-hand attributes, then remove redundant dependencies.

Step by step.

  1. Split: A → BC becomes A → B and A → C. The set is now {A → B, A → C, B → D, C → D}.
  2. No left-hand side has more than one attribute, so there is no extraneous attribute to remove.
  3. Test A → B: without it, A⁺ = {A, C, D} (via A → C and C → D), which does not contain B. Needed.
  4. Test A → C: without it, A⁺ = {A, B, D} (via A → B and B → D), which does not contain C. Needed.
  5. Test B → D: without it, B⁺ = {B}. Needed.
  6. Test C → D: without it, C⁺ = {C}. Needed.
  7. Nothing is redundant, so the minimal cover has 4 dependencies.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Answering 3 by counting the three original dependencies. Splitting A → BC increases the count before any redundancy removal can reduce it.

Takeaway. Split first, then prune — the two passes change the count in opposite directions, so do them in order and never skip one.

lossless-join-dependency-preservation · 10
DB-U3-Q05MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) is decomposed into R1(A, B) and R2(B, C). The decomposition is lossless if and only if

  1. A → B or C → B holds in R.
  2. A → C or C → A holds in R.
  3. B → A or B → C holds in R.
  4. no dependency need hold: a binary decomposition is always lossless.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a decomposition into two relations, the lossless-join test is: the common attributes must functionally determine all of at least one part, i.e. the common set must be a superkey of R₁ or of R₂. Here the common attributes are {B}, so the condition is B → A (B is a key of R₁) or B → C (B is a key of R₂).

Step by step.

  1. Compute the intersection: R₁ ∩ R₂ = {B}.
  2. Ask whether the intersection determines the whole of R₁, that is B → A.
  3. Ask whether the intersection determines the whole of R₂, that is B → C.
  4. If either holds, every tuple of the join is a tuple of the original relation, so the join loses nothing. If neither holds, the join can manufacture spurious tuples.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) puts the dependencies on the wrong side: A and C are not the common attributes, so they cannot reconstruct a tuple across the join.
  • (B) names attributes that do not even appear together in one part.
  • (D) is false: R₁(A, B) and R₂(B, C) with no dependencies at all can join into extra tuples.

Common misconception. Testing the common attribute in the wrong direction, for example accepting A → B. That dependency is about A, but A is in only one part; the join is stitched together on B.

Takeaway. Two-way rule: intersect the two schemas, then ask whether that intersection is a key of either side. One side is enough.

DB-U3-Q12MCQmedium

The tableau (chase) test is applied to a decomposition of R into R1, ..., Rn. The test declares the decomposition lossless when

  1. some row of the final tableau consists entirely of distinguished (a) symbols.
  2. every row of the final tableau contains at least one distinguished symbol.
  3. no row of the tableau can be changed by any further application of the dependencies.
  4. the union of the parts is R and the intersection of all the parts is empty.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The chase builds a tableau with one row per decomposed relation. A cell holds the distinguished symbol aᵢ when the attribute Aᵢ belongs to that relation, and a subscripted b symbol otherwise. Dependencies are then applied until nothing changes. The decomposition is lossless exactly when one row becomes all-distinguished, because that row represents a tuple of the original relation that the join reproduces.

Step by step.

  1. Write one row per Rᵢ, marking aᵢ in the columns that Rᵢ contains.
  2. Apply each dependency X → Y: if two rows agree on all of X, make them agree on all of Y, preferring an a symbol over a b symbol.
  3. Repeat to a fixed point.
  4. If some row is all a symbols, the join is lossless; otherwise it is lossy.

Answer: Option (A) — one row must become entirely distinguished.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is too weak: scattered a symbols across different rows prove nothing.
  • (C) merely says the chase has finished, which is the stopping rule, not the verdict.
  • (D) describes a union and an empty intersection, which is a property of the decomposition, not a lossless guarantee; it is in fact the classic lossy shape.

Common misconception. Assuming that a decomposition into relations whose union is R is automatically lossless. That condition is necessary for the decomposition to make sense at all; it says nothing about whether the join reconstructs the original tuples.

Takeaway. Chase verdict in one line: one all-a row means lossless, no such row means lossy.

DB-U3-Q17MSQhard

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A -> B, B -> C, C -> D}. Which of the following decompositions of R are lossless? (Select all that apply.)

  1. R₁(A, C) and R₂(B, D)
  2. R₁(A, B) and R₂(B, C, D)
  3. R₁(A, B) and R₂(A, C, D)
  4. R₁(A, B, C) and R₂(C, D)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a two-way decomposition the test is short: intersect the two schemas and check whether the intersection determines one of the parts. The chase test gives the same verdict.

Step by step.

  1. (A) R₁(A, C) ∩ R₂(B, D) = ∅. With no common attribute the join is a cartesian product, so the decomposition is lossy. (A) is out.
  2. (B) common attribute B. B⁺ = {B, C, D}, so B is a key of R₂(B, C, D). Lossless.
  3. (C) common attribute A. A⁺ = {A, B, C, D}, so A is a key of R₁(A, B). Lossless.
  4. (D) common attribute C. C⁺ = {C, D}, so C is a key of R₂(C, D). Lossless.
  5. Three of the four pass: (B), (C) and (D).

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) shares no attribute between the two parts, which is the textbook lossy case.

Common misconception. Judging losslessness by "does the decomposition look natural" or by counting attributes. Only the intersection test decides, and a decomposition can look perfectly reasonable and still be lossy.

Takeaway. Intersect, then ask "is the intersection a key of either part?" For a chain A → B → C → D, any split whose two parts share a point of the chain is lossless.

DB-U3-Q22MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A -> B, BC -> D} and is decomposed into R1(A, B) and R2(A, C, D). Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The decomposition is lossless and dependency preserving.
  2. The decomposition is dependency preserving but lossy.
  3. The decomposition is lossless but not dependency preserving.
  4. The decomposition is both lossy and not dependency preserving.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Losslessness and dependency preservation are independent properties and must be tested separately. Losslessness is about the join reconstructing the original tuples; preservation is about being able to check every original dependency inside a single decomposed relation.

Step by step.

  1. Losslessness: R₁ ∩ R₂ = {A}, and A → B holds, so A is a key of R₁(A, B). The join is lossless.
  2. Dependency preservation: A → B is checkable inside R₁.
  3. BC → D needs B and C together, but B appears only in R₁ and C only in R₂. Neither part contains both, and no part implies it: the projection of F on R₂ is empty, and on R₁ it is just A → B.
  4. So BC → D is lost. The decomposition is lossless but not dependency preserving.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails on preservation, as shown above.
  • (B) fails on losslessness: the common attribute A is a key of R₁, so no spurious tuples can appear.
  • (D) is wrong on both counts.

Common misconception. Assuming a lossless decomposition automatically preserves dependencies. It does not, and this example is the smallest one that shows it: the two properties pull in different directions.

Takeaway. Run two tests, not one. Lossless: does the intersection determine a part? Preserving: does every dependency live inside some single part?

DB-U3-Q25MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) is decomposed into R1(A, B, C) and R2(C, D). The decomposition is lossless if

  1. C → D holds in R.
  2. A → C holds in R.
  3. D → C holds in R.
  4. B → C holds in R.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The common attribute here is C. The decomposition is lossless when C is a superkey of at least one of the two parts: of R₂(C, D), which needs C → D, or of R₁(A, B, C), which would need C to determine A and B.

Step by step.

  1. R₁ ∩ R₂ = {C}.
  2. If C → D holds, C is a key of R₂, so every tuple of the join is a tuple of the original relation — lossless.
  3. A → C constrains A and C, both of which live inside R₁; it never relates the two parts, so it cannot make the join lossless.
  4. D → C makes D a key of R₂, not C. The common attribute must itself be the key, so this does not help.
  5. B → C likewise lives inside R₁ and does not involve D at all.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (D) are dependencies entirely inside one part; they say nothing about how the parts join.
  • (C) points the arrow the wrong way: it makes D determine C, so C is not the key of R₂.

Common misconception. Accepting any dependency that mentions the common attribute. The dependency must have the common attribute on the left and must reach across the split.

Takeaway. Common attribute C, split into R₁ and R₂: lossless if C → (R₁ − C) or C → (R₂ − C). Here C → D is the second form.

DB-U3-Q41MCQeasy

A decomposition of R into R₁, …, Rₙ is dependency preserving when

  1. the union of the projections of F onto the parts is equivalent to F.
  2. every pair of parts shares at least one attribute.
  3. the union of the parts is R, so no attribute is lost.
  4. every part is in BCNF.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Dependency preservation is about whether the constraints can still be enforced without joining the parts back together. Each part Rᵢ can only enforce the dependencies whose attributes all lie inside it — its projection Fᵢ of F⁺.

Step by step.

  1. Project F onto each part: Fᵢ contains every dependency of F⁺ whose attributes are all in Rᵢ.
  2. Take the union G = F₁ ∪ … ∪ Fₙ.
  3. The decomposition preserves dependencies exactly when G⁺ = F⁺, i.e. when G is equivalent to F.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes a condition sometimes used for losslessness, not for dependency preservation; two parts can share an attribute and still lose a dependency.
  • (C) is the basic sanity condition for any decomposition at all, and is satisfied by decompositions that lose dependencies.
  • (D) is a normal-form property and is unrelated to whether dependencies survive.

Common misconception. Confusing dependency preservation with losslessness. They are independent: a decomposition can be lossless and not preserve dependencies, or preserve them and be lossy.

Takeaway. Lossless = no spurious tuples on join; dependency preserving = constraints still enforceable part by part.

DB-U3-Q42MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A → B, C → D} and is decomposed into R₁(A, B) and R₂(C, D). Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The decomposition is lossless and dependency preserving.
  2. The decomposition is lossless but not dependency preserving.
  3. The decomposition is lossy but dependency preserving.
  4. The decomposition is both lossy and not dependency preserving.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two independent tests. Losslessness for a binary decomposition: the common attributes must be a superkey of at least one part. Dependency preservation: the union of the projected dependencies must cover F.

Step by step.

  1. R₁ ∩ R₂ = ∅. An empty intersection is never a superkey of either part, so the join test fails and the decomposition is lossy.
  2. Concretely, the join of the two parts produces every combination of the A, B pairs with the C, D pairs, including tuples that were never in R.
  3. Projection onto R₁(A, B) gives A → B; projection onto R₂(C, D) gives C → D.
  4. Their union is exactly F, so both dependencies survive: the decomposition is dependency preserving.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) both claim losslessness, which an empty intersection rules out.
  • (D) claims the dependencies are lost, but each dependency lies entirely inside one part.

Common misconception. Assuming a decomposition that keeps every attribute and every dependency must be lossless. Losing no attribute is not the same as losing no information.

Takeaway. Check the intersection first — if R₁ ∩ R₂ is empty, the binary decomposition is lossy no matter how the dependencies fall.

DB-U3-Q43MSQhard

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A → B, B → C, C → D}. Which of the following decompositions of R are dependency preserving? (Select all that apply.)

  1. R₁(A, B), R₂(B, C), R₃(C, D)
  2. R₁(A, B), R₂(B, D)
  3. R₁(A, B), R₂(A, C, D)
  4. R₁(A, B, C), R₂(C, D)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For each dependency of F, compute the closure of its left-hand side under the union of the projected dependencies. The decomposition preserves dependencies only if every dependency passes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Projections give A → B, B → C and C → D — exactly F. Preserved.
  2. (B) Projections give A → B and B → D (valid since B⁺ = {B, C, D}). Under these, B⁺ = {B, D}, so B → C is lost.
  3. (C) Projections give A → B and, on A, C, D, A → C, A → D, C → D. Under these, B⁺ = {B}, so B → C is lost.
  4. (D) Projections give A → B, B → C, A → C and C → D. All three dependencies of F are recovered. Preserved.

Answer: Options (A) and (D) are dependency preserving; (B) and (C) are not.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (C) both split B away from C, so nothing inside either part can express B → C.

Common misconception. Judging preservation by whether the original dependencies "appear" in the parts. A dependency can be implied by the projections even when it is not written down, as A → C in part (D) shows.

Takeaway. Preservation is a closure test on the union of projections, not a visual check for identical arrows.

DB-U3-Q44NATmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D, E) satisfies F = {AB → C, C → D, D → E} and is decomposed into R₁(A, B, C), R₂(A, B, D) and R₃(D, E). How many of the three dependencies of F are preserved by this decomposition?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Project F⁺ onto each part, union the projections into G, then test each dependency of F by computing the left-hand closure under G.

Step by step.

  1. Projection onto R₁(A, B, C): AB → C (attributes A, B, C all present).
  2. Projection onto R₂(A, B, D): AB → D, which is valid because (AB)⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E} in F.
  3. Projection onto R₃(D, E): D → E.
  4. So G = {AB → C, AB → D, D → E}.
  5. Test AB → C: (AB)⁺ under G = {A, B, C, D, E}. Preserved.
  6. Test C → D: C⁺ under G = {C}, because no dependency in G has C on its left. Lost.
  7. Test D → E: D⁺ under G = {D, E}. Preserved.
  8. Two of the three are preserved.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting C → D as preserved because C and D both survive somewhere in the schema. They never appear together in a single part, so no part can enforce that dependency.

Takeaway. A dependency survives only if some single part contains all its attributes and the union of projections still implies it.

DB-U3-Q45MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies F = {A → B, C → D}. Which one of the following decompositions of R is lossless?

  1. R₁(A, B), R₂(C, D)
  2. R₁(A, C), R₂(B, D)
  3. R₁(A, B), R₂(A, C, D)
  4. R₁(A, C), R₂(B, C)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a binary decomposition into R₁ and R₂, the decomposition is lossless exactly when R₁ ∩ R₂ determines every attribute of at least one of the two parts — that is, the intersection is a superkey of R₁ or of R₂.

Step by step.

  1. (A) R₁ ∩ R₂ = ∅, which is never a superkey. Lossy.
  2. (B) R₁ ∩ R₂ = ∅ again. Lossy.
  3. (C) R₁ ∩ R₂ = {A}, and A → B holds, so {A} determines all of R₁(A, B). Lossless.
  4. (D) R₁ ∩ R₂ = {C}. Neither C → A (for R₁(A, C)) nor C → B (for R₂(B, C)) holds. Lossy.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) have empty intersections, so the join reconstructs the full cross product of the parts.
  • (D) shares C but C determines only D, which is in neither part, so nothing is pinned down.

Common misconception. Assuming any two parts that share an attribute join losslessly. The shared attributes must actually be a key of one of the parts.

Takeaway. Binary losslessness = "the intersection is a superkey of one side". Test it with the closure of the intersection.

multivalued-dependencies-4nf · 10
DB-U3-Q04MCQeasy

Relation R(X, Y, Z) satisfies the multivalued dependency X ->> Y. Which one of the following best states what this means?

  1. Each value of X is associated with exactly one value of Y.
  2. For each value of X, the set of Y values that occurs with it does not depend on which Z values occur with it.
  3. Within each group of equal X values, Y functionally determines Z.
  4. X, Y and Z must together form the primary key.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multivalued dependency X →→ Y in R(X, Y, Z) says that Y and Z are independent given X. Formally, whenever (x, y₁, z₁) and (x, y₂, z₂) are both in the relation, so are (x, y₁, z₂) and (x, y₂, z₁). The Y values repeat for every Z value and vice versa.

Step by step.

  1. Take one X value, say a student. The set of courses that student takes and the set of hobbies that student has are two independent facts about that student.
  2. If the two facts are stored in one relation, every course must be paired with every hobby, or the table would wrongly suggest that a particular course goes with a particular hobby.
  3. That forced cross-product is exactly the independence statement: given X, the Y set is the same whichever Z you look at.
  4. So the meaning is (B); nothing here says a single Y per X, which would be the functional dependency X → Y.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a functional dependency, a strictly stronger statement.
  • (C) invents a dependency inside the group that the MVD does not assert.
  • (D) confuses the MVD with a key constraint; keys are about uniqueness, not about independence.

Common misconception. Reading →→ as "many values, therefore many-to-many". An MVD is about two independent multi-valued facts sharing one determinant, which is why it needs a decomposition into two relations and not a junction table of pairs.

Takeaway. FD = one value per determinant. MVD = a set of values per determinant, independent of the remaining attributes.

DB-U3-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about multivalued dependencies are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every functional dependency X → Y is also a multivalued dependency X →→ Y.
  2. Every multivalued dependency X →→ Y is also a functional dependency X → Y.
  3. X →→ Y is trivial exactly when Y ⊆ X or X ∪ Y = R.
  4. A relation is in 4NF exactly when, for every non-trivial multivalued dependency X →→ Y that holds in it, X is a superkey.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Functional dependencies are a special case of multivalued dependencies, so the MVD class is strictly larger. Triviality and the 4NF condition are the two definitions the form is built on.

Step by step.

  1. (A) If every X value pairs with exactly one Y value, then certainly the Y values do not vary with Z. So X → Y implies X →→ Y — true.
  2. (B) The converse fails: a student may take several courses and have several hobbies, which is an MVD but certainly not an FD — false.
  3. (C) X →→ Y is trivial when Y is inside X (then it says nothing) or when X and Y together exhaust the relation (then the leftover part is empty and again nothing is constrained) — true.
  4. (D) That is exactly the definition of 4NF: every non-trivial MVD must have a superkey on its left — true.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the containment: MVDs are the weaker, more general form.

Common misconception. Treating →→ as a notational variant of →. The arrow direction of the implication matters: FD implies MVD, never the other way.

Takeaway. 4NF is to MVDs what BCNF is to FDs: every non-trivial dependency of the relevant kind must have a superkey on the left.

DB-U3-Q15MCQhard

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies the two multivalued dependencies A ->> B and A ->> C, and no functional dependency. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. R is in BCNF, and therefore also in 4NF.
  2. R is in 4NF because A →→ B and A →→ C are its only dependencies.
  3. R is not in 4NF, because A is not a superkey of R; the lossless 4NF decomposition is R₁(A, B) and R₂(A, C).
  4. R cannot be decomposed into 4NF without loss of information.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 4NF asks that every non-trivial MVD have a superkey on its left. With no functional dependency at all, the only candidate key of R(A, B, C) is the full set ABC, so A is not a superkey and both MVDs violate 4NF.

Step by step.

  1. Keys: no FD holds, so no proper subset determines the relation; the candidate key is ABC.
  2. A →→ B is non-trivial (B is not inside A, and A ∪ B = AB ≠ ABC) and A is not a superkey, so 4NF fails.
  3. The standard 4NF decomposition of X →→ Y in R(X, Y, Z) is (X, Y) and (X, Z). Applying it gives R₁(A, B) and R₂(A, C), and the second MVD is then trivially satisfied inside those parts.
  4. The decomposition is lossless: the common attribute A is a key of both parts (each part is a two-attribute relation in which A determines the other attribute by the MVD), so the join restores exactly the original tuples.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: BCNF speaks only about FDs, so a relation with no FDs is trivially in BCNF — and that is exactly why BCNF alone cannot detect this redundancy. It is 4NF that fails.
  • (B) inverts the 4NF condition: having MVDs is precisely what must be checked, not a reason to pass.
  • (D) is false: the MVD-based decomposition above is lossless.

Common misconception. Concluding "in BCNF, therefore in 4NF". BCNF is defined on functional dependencies only; a relation can be in BCNF and still be in 3NF-violating shape for MVDs. Every 4NF relation is in BCNF, never the reverse.

Takeaway. An MVD with no FD on the same determinant is invisible to BCNF and fatal to 4NF. Split X →→ Y into (X, Y) and (X, Z).

DB-U3-Q20MCQmedium

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies A ->> B and A ->> C, with candidate key ABC. Which one of the following is a correct 4NF decomposition of R?

  1. R₁(A, B, C) — R is already in 4NF, so no decomposition is needed.
  2. R₁(A, B) and R₂(A, C)
  3. R₁(A, B) and R₂(B, C)
  4. R₁(A, C) and R₂(B, C)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When X →→ Y holds in R(X, Y, Z), the 4NF decomposition is (X, Y) and (X, Z). The determinant X is kept in both parts, which is what makes the join lossless.

Step by step.

  1. A is not a superkey of ABC, so the relation is not in 4NF and must be decomposed.
  2. Applying the rule to A →→ B with the leftover attribute C gives (A, B) and (A, C).
  3. Check the parts: in (A, B) the MVD A →→ B is trivial because A ∪ B is the whole relation; the same holds for A →→ C in (A, C). Both parts are in 4NF.
  4. Check losslessness: the common attribute is A, and each part is determined by A, so the join restores exactly the original tuples.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) leaves a non-trivial MVD with a non-superkey determinant in place, so 4NF is still violated.
  • (C) and (D) both drop A from one part. Joining on B or on C cannot reconstruct the pairing of Y values with Z values, so the decomposition loses information.

Common misconception. Decomposing like a binary many-to-many relationship, into (A, B) and (B, C). An MVD split is not a relationship split: the determinant must appear in both parts.

Takeaway. MVD split: keep X on both sides, put Y in one part and the remaining attributes in the other.

DB-U3-Q24MCQeasy

In R(A, B, C), which one of the following multivalued dependencies is trivial?

  1. A →→ B
  2. A →→ BC
  3. B →→ C
  4. C →→ A
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. X →→ Y is trivial when Y ⊆ X or when X ∪ Y is the whole relation. In either case nothing is left for Y to be independent of, so the dependency imposes no condition on the data.

Step by step.

  1. A →→ B: B ⊆ A is false and A ∪ B = AB ≠ ABC, so it is non-trivial.
  2. A →→ BC: BC ⊆ A is false, but the second clause holds, because A ∪ BC = ABC = R. There is no leftover attribute, so the dependency is trivial.
  3. B →→ C: C ⊆ B is false and B ∪ C = BC ≠ ABC, so it is non-trivial.
  4. C →→ A: A ⊆ C is false and C ∪ A = AC ≠ ABC, so it is non-trivial.

Answer: Option (B) — A →→ BC.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (C) and (D) all leave a genuinely undetermined remainder, so each one constrains the data.

Common misconception. Using only the functional-dependency rule Y ⊆ X and forgetting the second clause X ∪ Y = R. That second clause is what makes A →→ BC trivial, and it is also why a dependency can be trivial without looking obviously so.

Takeaway. Two triviality clauses for MVDs, one for FDs. If nothing remains outside X ∪ Y, the dependency cannot bite.

DB-U3-Q46MCQeasy

Relation R(A, B, C) satisfies the multivalued dependency A →→ B. Which one of the following multivalued dependencies is guaranteed to hold in R as well?

  1. A →→ C
  2. A → B
  3. B →→ A
  4. C →→ B
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Multivalued dependencies come with a complementation rule: if X →→ Y holds in R, then X →→ (R − X − Y) holds too. The two right-hand sides partition what is left of R once X is removed.

Step by step.

  1. Here X = A and Y = B in R(A, B, C).
  2. R − X − Y = {A, B, C} − {A} − {B} = {C}.
  3. So A →→ C follows immediately.
  4. Intuitively, "the set of B values under an A is independent of the C values" is exactly the same statement as "the set of C values is independent of the B values".

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the converse of the implication X → Y ⇒ X →→ Y; an MVD does not give a functional dependency.
  • (C) reverses the left and right sides, which no rule permits.
  • (D) moves the determinant to C, which nothing licenses.

Common misconception. Treating an MVD as a weaker functional dependency. It is a different statement — about independent sets of values — and the only guaranteed consequences are complementation, augmentation, transitivity and the trivial cases.

Takeaway. For every X →→ Y there is a matching X →→ (R − X − Y); always check the complement before concluding anything.

DB-U3-Q47MCQeasy

Relation R(X, Y, Z) is decomposed into R₁(X, Y) and R₂(X, Z). This decomposition is lossless whenever

  1. X →→ Y holds in R.
  2. X → Y holds in R.
  3. Y →→ Z holds in R.
  4. no condition is needed: every binary decomposition is lossless.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multivalued dependency X →→ Y in R(X, Y, Z) says the Y values and the Z values under an X vary independently. Storing them in separate relations (X, Y) and (X, Z) therefore loses nothing: the cross product on join regenerates exactly the original tuples.

Step by step.

  1. If X →→ Y holds, then for each X value the relation contains every combination of its Y values with its Z values.
  2. Joining R₁(X, Y) with R₂(X, Z) on X regenerates exactly those combinations.
  3. No tuple that was absent can appear, so the join is exactly R — lossless.
  4. This is the standard MVD decomposition used by 4NF.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) X → Y is stronger than needed and would also work, but it is not the condition; the MVD is what justifies the split.
  • (C) Y →→ Z would justify a different split, on Y, not this one.
  • (D) is false: R₁(X, Y), R₂(Z) is a binary decomposition and is lossy.

Common misconception. Believing losslessness requires a functional dependency. An MVD is enough, and the 4NF decomposition depends on exactly that fact.

Takeaway. Lossless binary rule: the intersection is a superkey of one part or an MVD holds across the split.

DB-U3-Q48MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about fourth normal form are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every relation in 4NF is also in BCNF.
  2. Every relation in BCNF is also in 4NF.
  3. Decomposing R on a multivalued dependency X →→ Y into (X ∪ Y) and (X ∪ (R − X − Y)) is lossless.
  4. 4NF is defined using functional dependencies alone.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. 4NF strengthens BCNF: for every non-trivial multivalued dependency X →→ Y, X must be a superkey. Since every functional dependency is also a multivalued dependency, the 4NF condition subsumes the BCNF condition.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: if every non-trivial MVD has a superkey determinant, then in particular every non-trivial FD does, because X → Y ⇒ X →→ Y. So 4NF implies BCNF.
  2. (B) False: a relation can have a non-trivial MVD with a non-superkey determinant while having no non-trivial FD at all.
  3. (C) True: that split is exactly the MVD decomposition, and joining the two parts on X regenerates R.
  4. (D) False: 4NF is defined in terms of multivalued dependencies; using only FDs would just restate BCNF.

Answer: Options (A) and (C) are true; (B) and (D) are false.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the containment: 4NF ⊂ BCNF, not the other way round.
  • (D) removes the very concept that distinguishes 4NF from BCNF.

Common misconception. Thinking 4NF and BCNF are incomparable. They are nested, and the nesting direction matters: a relation can be in BCNF and fail 4NF, never the reverse.

Takeaway. Order: 1NF ⊂ 2NF ⊂ 3NF ⊂ BCNF ⊂ 4NF, each defined by a strictly stronger condition.

DB-U3-Q49NATmedium

Relation R(A, B, C, D) satisfies the multivalued dependencies A →→ B and A →→ C. Applying the 4NF decomposition repeatedly until no non-trivial multivalued dependency with a non-superkey left-hand side remains, how many relations result?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The 4NF decomposition on X →→ Y replaces R with (X ∪ Y) and (X ∪ (R − X − Y)), and the process repeats on each part until every remaining MVD is trivial or has a superkey left-hand side.

Step by step.

  1. A is not a superkey of R(A, B, C, D) and A →→ B is non-trivial, so split on it: (A, B) and (A, C, D).
  2. In (A, B), the restriction of A →→ B is trivial because A ∪ B is the whole part. Done.
  3. In (A, C, D), the restriction of A →→ C is non-trivial and A is not a superkey. Split: (A, C) and (A, D).
  4. Both remaining parts have two attributes, so every MVD in them is trivial. The process stops.
  5. Resulting relations: (A, B), (A, C), (A, D) — three relations.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Answering 2 by splitting only once on A →→ B and stopping. The second MVD survives inside (A, C, D) and must be applied again.

Takeaway. Keep decomposing until every part is either two attributes wide or has only trivial MVDs — the count comes from the recursion, not from the number of given dependencies.

DB-U3-Q50MCQhard

Which one of the following relations is in BCNF but not in 4NF?

  1. R(A, B, C) with A →→ B and no non-trivial functional dependency.
  2. R(A, B, C) with A → B and B → C.
  3. R(A, B, C) with AB → C and C → B.
  4. R(A, B, C, D) with A → B and C → D.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. BCNF constrains functional dependencies; 4NF constrains multivalued dependencies. A relation escapes BCNF only through a non-trivial FD with a non-superkey determinant, and escapes 4NF through a non-trivial MVD with a non-superkey determinant. To be in BCNF but not 4NF, the relation must have no offending FD but at least one offending MVD.

Step by step.

  1. (A) With no non-trivial FD, BCNF is satisfied vacuously. But A →→ B is non-trivial (B ¬⊆ A and A ∪ B ≠ R) and A is not a superkey. So 4NF fails. This is the answer.
  2. (B) B → C has B as a non-superkey determinant, so BCNF already fails.
  3. (C) C → B has C as a non-superkey determinant (the keys are AB and AC), so BCNF fails.
  4. (D) A → B has A as a non-superkey determinant, so BCNF fails.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B), (C) and (D) each contain a non-trivial FD whose determinant is not a superkey, so none of them is even in BCNF.

Common misconception. Assuming that a relation with no functional dependencies must be fully normalized. With no FDs it is in BCNF automatically, but an MVD alone can still leave it outside 4NF — which is precisely why 4NF exists.

Takeaway. BCNF looks only at FDs; 4NF looks at MVDs. A relation with MVDs and no FDs is the classic BCNF-but-not-4NF case.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 4 — Transactions & Concurrency Control
acid-properties · 10
DB-U4-Q01MCQeasy

A transaction debits ₹5,000 from account A and credits ₹5,000 to account B. The debit is written to disk, then the system crashes before the credit is written. On restart the debit is undone. Which ACID property is being enforced?

  1. Atomicity: either all of the transaction's updates are reflected in the database or none of them is.
  2. Consistency: the sum of the two balances is unchanged.
  3. Isolation: no other transaction observed the intermediate state.
  4. Durability: the committed debit survives the crash.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Atomicity requires that a transaction is an indivisible unit of work: its effects are either all applied or all removed. A partially applied transaction that is then rolled back is exactly what atomicity forbids, and undoing the debit restores the all-or-nothing guarantee.

Step by step.

  1. The transaction consists of two updates that must travel together: debit A and credit B.
  2. The debit reached the database, the credit did not, so the database held a state in which money had disappeared.
  3. Recovery used the before-image of A to undo the debit, leaving the database as if the transaction had never run.
  4. Removing the partial effect is the definition of atomicity; consistency and durability are properties of the end states and of committed data respectively, and neither is what the undo enforces.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Consistency is about the integrity constraints holding before and after a complete transaction; the undo is not what preserves the sum, the completed pair of updates would be.
  • (C) Isolation concerns interference between concurrent transactions; no second transaction appears here.
  • (D) Durability concerns committed data surviving a crash. This transaction never committed, so durability has nothing to protect.

Common misconception. Reading 'the system crashed and recovery fixed it' and answering durability. Durability protects work that has committed; atomicity removes work that has not.

Takeaway. Undo of a partial transaction is the atomicity mechanism; redo of a committed transaction is the durability mechanism.

DB-U4-Q02MCQeasy

Which ACID property guarantees that, once a transaction has committed, its updates survive a subsequent system crash?

  1. Isolation
  2. Consistency
  3. Atomicity
  4. Durability
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Durability is the guarantee that the effects of a committed transaction are permanent: once the commit is acknowledged, a later failure cannot erase them.

Step by step.

  1. The moment of interest is the commit point: before it, the transaction may be undone; after it, it must never be undone.
  2. Durability is implemented by forcing the log records of the transaction to stable (non-volatile) storage before the commit is acknowledged, and by redoing committed work during recovery.
  3. Hence the property that speaks about survival after a crash is durability.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Isolation is about the visibility of intermediate states to concurrent transactions.
  • (B) Consistency is about integrity constraints being preserved by a complete transaction.
  • (C) Atomicity is about all-or-nothing execution; it undoes aborted work but says nothing about committed work surviving a crash.

Common misconception. Confusing atomicity with durability because both involve recovery. Recovery performs undo for atomicity and redo for durability; they are opposite halves of the same log.

Takeaway. Undo = atomicity, redo = durability, locks/timestamps = isolation, constraints = consistency.

DB-U4-Q03MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about the consistency property (the C of ACID) is TRUE?

  1. The DBMS can enforce consistency entirely on its own, without being told the application's integrity constraints.
  2. Consistency and isolation are the same requirement, because both concern concurrent execution.
  3. A transaction that starts from a state satisfying the declared integrity constraints must leave the database in a state that again satisfies them.
  4. A schedule is consistent only when no two transactions execute concurrently.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Consistency is a statement about states, not about execution: if a transaction runs to completion on a consistent database, the database is consistent again afterwards. The constraints themselves come from the application; the DBMS can only enforce the ones it is told about (keys, foreign keys, CHECK assertions).

Step by step.

  1. Consistency is preserved by the transaction's own logic together with the declared constraints: every intermediate state may be inconsistent, but the final state must satisfy all constraints.
  2. The other three ACID properties are mechanisms the DBMS supplies; consistency is the property the application is responsible for stating.
  3. Therefore (C) is the accurate statement.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The DBMS cannot enforce constraints it does not know; a transfer of funds between accounts keeps the total constant only because the application says so.
  • (B) Isolation is about concurrent execution being equivalent to a serial order; consistency is about the end state satisfying constraints. A serializable schedule can still break a constraint if the transaction's logic is wrong.
  • (D) Serial execution is a scheduling notion; consistency does not require it.

Common misconception. Treating consistency as something the concurrency-control protocol delivers. A perfectly serializable schedule can still violate a business rule.

Takeaway. Isolation, atomicity and durability are DBMS mechanisms; consistency is the application's contract, checked with the DBMS's constraint machinery.

DB-U4-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the ACID properties and the way a DBMS implements them are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Atomicity is implemented by undoing, from the log, the updates of a transaction that aborts.
  2. Durability is obtained by forcing the log records of a committed transaction to stable storage before the commit is acknowledged.
  3. Isolation is obtained by a concurrency-control protocol whose effect is that concurrent execution is equivalent to some serial order.
  4. Consistency is guaranteed by the log manager, because replaying the log restores every integrity constraint.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each of the first three letters of ACID has a standard mechanism: undo logging for atomicity, force-log-at-commit plus redo for durability, and a serializability-enforcing protocol (locking, timestamps, validation) for isolation. Consistency has no such mechanism of its own.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. An aborted transaction is rolled back with the before-images in the undo part of the log.
  2. (B) True. A commit is acknowledged only after its log records are on stable storage; otherwise a crash immediately after commit could lose the transaction.
  3. (C) True. Isolation is defined as equivalence to a serial order, and that is precisely what 2PL or timestamp ordering enforces.
  4. (D) False. Replaying the log restores states, not constraints: if a transaction's logic broke a constraint, replaying it reproduces the violation. Consistency needs the constraints to be declared and checked.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the odd one out: the log manager has no knowledge of integrity constraints, and redo/undo are value-level operations.

Common misconception. Believing that recovery 'repairs' an inconsistent database. Recovery restores the last committed state; if that state was inconsistent, recovery faithfully restores the inconsistency.

Takeaway. Map each letter to its mechanism: A = undo, D = force + redo, I = serializability protocol, C = declared constraints plus correct transaction logic.

DB-U4-Q05NAThard

A DBMS uses the deferred-update technique, in which a transaction's updates reach the database only after its commit record has been written. Transaction T1 updates 6 data items and commits; its log records are on stable storage, but none of its pages has been written to the database yet. Transaction T2 updates 3 data items and aborts before commit, and none of its updates ever reached the database. After a crash and restart, how many data items must recovery process in total (redo plus undo)?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deferred update is a NO-UNDO/REDO scheme: updates are buffered and applied only after commit, so an aborted or uncommitted transaction leaves nothing in the database and needs no undo. A committed transaction whose pages were not yet written does need redo.

Step by step.

  1. T1 committed, so its effects are permanent, but its 6 pages are not yet in the database: recovery must redo all 6.
  2. T2 aborted before commit under deferred update, so none of its 3 updates ever reached the database: there is nothing to undo, so undo contributes 0.
  3. Total items processed = redo 6 + undo 0 = 6.

Answer: 6.

Common misconception. Adding 3 for T2 out of habit. Under deferred update an abort costs nothing at recovery because the update never touched the database; undo would only be needed under a steal policy or immediate update.

Takeaway. NO-UNDO/REDO (deferred update) means aborts are free and only committed work is redone; UNDO/NO-REDO (immediate update, no-force) means the reverse.

DB-U4-Q26MCQeasy

A transaction debits ₹2,000 from account A and credits ₹2,000 to account B. The debit reaches the database, the credit does not, and the system then crashes. Recovery undoes the debit. Which ACID property is the one that demands this undo?

  1. Consistency: the invariant that the sum of the two balances is preserved.
  2. Atomicity: every update of the transaction is applied, or none of them is.
  3. Isolation: no other transaction observes the intermediate state.
  4. Durability: an update of a committed transaction survives a crash.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution
