Concept. Atomicity makes a transaction an all-or-nothing unit. A transaction that has applied half of its updates must be rolled back to the state it started from, and the mechanism that does that rollback is what atomicity requires.

Step by step.

  1. The transaction has two updates that must travel together: debit A, credit B.
  2. The debit reached the database and the credit did not, so the transaction is only partly applied.
  3. Recovery finds no commit record for the transaction in the log and undoes the debit, restoring the before-image.
  4. The requirement that a partly applied transaction be removed completely, rather than left half done, is exactly atomicity.

Answer: Option (B) — atomicity demands the undo.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Consistency is about the transaction taking the database from one valid state to another; it is the property the application programmer preserves by writing a correct transaction, not the mechanism that undoes a half-finished one.
  • (C) Isolation is about what concurrent transactions may observe, and no second transaction appears in this example.
  • (D) Durability protects a committed transaction; this one never committed.

Common misconception. Answering "consistency" because the sum of the balances changed. The sum changed only because the transaction was left half applied, and the undo that fixes it is atomicity’s job.

Takeaway. Half-applied and then removed is atomicity; half-applied and left there would be the failure atomicity exists to prevent.

DB-U4-Q27MCQeasy

Which one of the following is the mechanism through which a DBMS guarantees the durability of a committed transaction?

  1. Forcing the transaction’s log records to stable storage before the commit is acknowledged, so that redo can reapply the updates after a crash.
  2. Keeping the transaction’s updated pages in the buffer pool until the next checkpoint.
  3. Holding every lock of the transaction until the transaction terminates.
  4. Taking a full backup of the database after every commit.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Durability says that once the DBMS acknowledges a commit, the transaction’s effects survive a crash. The acknowledgement is therefore tied to the log reaching stable storage, not to the data pages reaching it: a redo record on disk is enough, because recovery can replay it.

Step by step.

  1. At commit the DBMS appends a commit record to the log.
  2. It forces the log, including that commit record, to stable storage before it answers the client.
  3. If a crash follows, the data pages may be stale or missing entirely, but the log holds every after-image of the committed transaction.
  4. Recovery replays those after-images during redo, so the commit survives.

Answer: Option (A) — force the log at commit, redo the pages later.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Pages in the buffer pool vanish with the buffer pool; buffering is what makes redo necessary.
  • (C) Holding locks is isolation, not durability, and locks are meaningless after a crash.
  • (D) A backup taken after each commit is neither required nor how any real system works.

Common misconception. Believing the data page must be on disk at commit. It need not be; the log record must be.

Takeaway. Durability is a promise about the log at commit time, not about the data file.

DB-U4-Q28MCQhard

A buffer manager follows the steal and force policy: an updated page may be written to disk before its transaction commits, and all of a transaction’s pages are forced to disk before its commit is acknowledged. Which one of the following is TRUE about the recovery work this policy can require?

  1. Recovery may need both undo and redo.
  2. Recovery may need redo, but never undo.
  3. Recovery needs neither undo nor redo.
  4. Recovery may need undo, but never redo.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two buffer-policy questions decide recovery independently. Steal asks whether an uncommitted transaction’s page can reach disk, and if it can, that page must be undone when the transaction aborts. Force asks whether a committed transaction’s pages are on disk at commit, and if they are, nothing has to be redone.

Step by step.

  1. Steal is allowed, so a page holding an uncommitted update can be on disk when the system crashes.
  2. That page must be rolled back to its before-image, so undo is required.
  3. Force is required, so every page of a committed transaction was already written before the commit was acknowledged.
  4. A committed transaction therefore leaves no work for redo, so redo is never required.

Answer: Option (D) — undo yes, redo no.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Redo would be needed only if a committed transaction could leave stale pages on disk, which force rules out.
  • (B) This is the answer for the opposite policy, no-steal plus no-force.
  • (C) Steal alone is enough to demand undo.

Common misconception. Treating steal and force as one setting. They are two independent decisions: steal decides undo, force decides redo.

Takeaway. steal ⇒ undo needed; no-force ⇒ redo needed. Here steal and force together give undo without redo.

DB-U4-Q29MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the ACID properties and the way a DBMS implements them are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Write-ahead logging — the log record for an update reaches stable storage before the data page it describes — is what makes undo possible after a crash.
  2. A transaction whose commit record is on stable storage can still be lost after a crash, because its updated pages may have been only in the buffer pool.
  3. Isolation is normally enforced by a concurrency-control protocol such as two-phase locking or timestamp ordering, rather than by running transactions one at a time.
  4. Durability requires the effects of a committed transaction to survive a failure that wipes out the contents of main memory.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each ACID letter maps to a mechanism: atomicity and durability to the log, isolation to a concurrency-control protocol, consistency to the programmer’s invariants.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. Undo restores a before-image, and that before-image is only available if the log record reached stable storage first. That ordering is the write-ahead rule.
  2. (B) is false. If the commit record is durable, redo replays the after-images and the transaction survives; the missing data pages are exactly what redo is for.
  3. (C) is true. Serial execution is the definition of the ideal, not the implementation; 2PL, timestamp ordering and validation are how real systems approximate it.
  4. (D) is true. That is the definition of durability: the loss of volatile memory must not lose a committed transaction.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading (B) as true because the pages are missing. Missing pages are recoverable from the log; only a missing commit record is fatal.

Takeaway. Log first, then pages — and a durable commit record is enough on its own.

DB-U4-Q30NATmedium

A system uses the deferred-update technique, so no update reaches the database until its transaction has committed. The log on stable storage reads, in order: ⟨T1 start⟩, ⟨T1 write A⟩, ⟨T1 commit⟩, ⟨T2 start⟩, ⟨T2 write B⟩, ⟨T3 start⟩, ⟨T3 write C⟩, ⟨T3 commit⟩, and then the machine crashes. Under deferred-update (redo-only) recovery, how many transactions are redone?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under deferred update no uncommitted update ever reaches the database, so nothing needs undoing; recovery is redo-only, and only transactions with a commit record in the log are redone.

Step by step.

  1. Scan the log and list the transactions that carry a commit record: T1 and T3.
  2. T2 has a start record and a write record but no commit record, so it never committed and its write never reached the database. It is simply ignored.
  3. Redo replays the after-images of T1 and T3.
  4. Number of transactions redone = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting T2 because it appears in the log, or counting data items instead of transactions. The question asks for transactions redone, and an uncommitted transaction contributes none.

Takeaway. Redo-only recovery redoes exactly the transactions with a commit record.

recoverability-cascadeless · 10
DB-U4-Q11MCQeasy

A schedule is recoverable if, for every pair of transactions Ti and Tj, whenever Tj reads a data item previously written by Ti, the commit of Ti appears before the commit of Tj. Which one of the following is TRUE about a schedule in which T1 writes X, T2 then reads X, and T2 commits before T1 does?

  1. The schedule is recoverable, because T2 read a value that T1 had already written.
  2. The schedule is not recoverable, because if T1 aborts, T2 has already committed on the strength of a value that never existed and cannot be undone.
  3. The schedule is strict but not cascadeless.
  4. The schedule is cascadeless but not recoverable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recoverability exists so that a commit is never based on data that later turns out never to have existed. If a reader commits before its writer, the writer's abort forces an undo of committed work, which is impossible; such a schedule is therefore not recoverable.

Step by step.

  1. T2 read X written by the uncommitted T1: this is a dirty read.
  2. T2 then committed. T1 has not committed.
  3. If T1 now aborts, T2's committed read is invalidated, and no rollback can repair a committed transaction.
  4. The commit order required by recoverability (writer before reader) is violated, so the schedule is not recoverable.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) misreads the definition: reading a written value is not enough, the writer must commit first.
  • (C) A strict schedule forbids even reading or writing an uncommitted item, so this schedule is not strict.
  • (D) Cascadelessness is stronger than recoverability, so 'cascadeless but not recoverable' is impossible.

Common misconception. Checking only that the read happened after the write. Recoverability is about the order of the two commits, not the order of the operations.

Takeaway. Recoverable ⇔ no reader commits before the transaction it read from. Violating it means committed data may have to be undone, which no recovery manager can do.

DB-U4-Q12MCQmedium

Which one of the following best describes a cascadeless (avoids-cascading-rollback) schedule?

  1. No transaction reads a data item written by a transaction that has not yet committed.
  2. No transaction overwrites a data item written by a transaction that has not yet committed.
  3. Every transaction acquires all of its locks before releasing any lock.
  4. Transactions read uncommitted data only when the writer commits immediately afterwards.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cascadeless schedule forbids dirty reads: a read is allowed only on committed data. That single restriction means an abort can never force another transaction to roll back, because nobody depends on uncommitted values.

Step by step.

  1. Cascading rollback happens when a reader of uncommitted data must itself be undone because the writer aborted.
  2. Removing dirty reads removes the dependency, hence the cascade.
  3. So cascadeless means: reads only of committed values, which is statement (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes a strict schedule, which forbids both reading and writing uncommitted data. Strict implies cascadeless, not the reverse.
  • (C) describes two-phase locking, a protocol rather than the schedule property.
  • (D) is not a property of schedules at all: 'commits immediately afterwards' does not remove the window in which the reader depends on an uncommitted writer.

Common misconception. Swapping (A) and (B). Cascadeless restricts reads only; strict restricts reads and writes.

Takeaway. Recoverable ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ strict. Each step adds one restriction: commit order, then no dirty reads, then no dirty reads or writes.

DB-U4-Q13MCQeasy

Which one of the following implications is TRUE for all schedules?

  1. Every recoverable schedule is cascadeless.
  2. Every conflict-serializable schedule is recoverable.
  3. Every recoverable schedule is serializable.
  4. Every cascadeless schedule is recoverable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three recovery classes form a chain of strict inclusions: strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable. Serializability is an orthogonal notion: it constrains the order of conflicting operations, not the order of commits.

Step by step.

  1. Cascadeless forbids dirty reads. If nobody reads uncommitted data, then whenever Tj reads from Ti, Ti has already committed, which satisfies the recoverability condition. So cascadeless ⇒ recoverable.
  2. The converse fails: a schedule can allow a dirty read and still order the commits correctly, so recoverable does not imply cascadeless.
  3. Serializability says nothing about commit order, so neither implies the other; a conflict-serializable schedule with T2 committing before T1 can be non-recoverable.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses the implication; recoverable is the weaker class.
  • (B) is false: conflict serializability constrains conflicting operations, not commits. Take T1 writing X, T2 reading X, T2 committing, then T1 aborting: the conflict order is T1 before T2, serializable, yet not recoverable.
  • (C) is false: a serializable interleaving may still be non-recoverable, and a recoverable schedule need not be serializable.

Common misconception. Assuming serializable schedules are automatically recoverable. They are not; serializability and recoverability are independent axes.

Takeaway. Memorise the chain strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable, and keep serializability on a separate axis.

DB-U4-Q14MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every strict schedule is cascadeless.
  2. Every cascadeless schedule is strict.
  3. A schedule in which no transaction performs a dirty read cannot suffer cascading rollbacks.
  4. In a recoverable schedule, no committed transaction has read a value written by a transaction that later aborts.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Strict forbids both reading and writing uncommitted data; cascadeless forbids only reading it. The extra prohibition can only shrink the class, so strict is a subset of cascadeless. The purpose of both is to prevent one abort from forcing other transactions to roll back.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Strict forbids more, so it is contained in cascadeless.
  2. (B) False. A schedule that allows overwriting an uncommitted value but never reads one is cascadeless and not strict.
  3. (C) True. Cascading rollback is caused exactly by dirty reads; remove them and no abort can propagate.
  4. (D) True. That is the definition of recoverability read contrapositively: if Tj read from Ti and committed, then Ti committed first, so Ti never aborts after Tj's commit.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) asserts the reverse containment, which the overwrite-without-read example refutes.

Common misconception. Confusing the direction of the inclusion. Strict is the strongest condition, so it is the smallest class.

Takeaway. strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable; the extra power of strict comes from also banning writes over uncommitted values, which is what strict 2PL provides.

DB-U4-Q15NAThard

Consider the schedule: T1 writes X; T2 reads X and writes Y; T3 reads Y; T4 reads Z. No transaction has committed when T1 aborts. How many transactions in total must be rolled back, counting T1 itself?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Cascading rollback follows the reads-from relation transitively: every transaction that read a value produced by an aborted transaction must be undone, and so must every transaction that read a value produced by those, and so on.

Step by step.

  1. T1 wrote X and aborts: T1 must be rolled back.
  2. T2 read X from the uncommitted T1, so T2 must be rolled back too (first-level cascade).
  3. T3 read Y, which T2 wrote, and T2 is now being undone, so T3 must be rolled back as well (second-level cascade).
  4. T4 read only Z, which no undone transaction wrote, so T4 is unaffected.
  5. Total rolled back: T1, T2, T3 = 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Stopping at the first level and answering 2, or including T4 because it ran concurrently. Only the transitive closure of the reads-from relation from the aborted transaction is rolled back.

Takeaway. Cascading rollback = transitive closure over reads-from edges. A schedule with no dirty reads (cascadeless) has no such closure beyond the aborting transaction itself.

DB-U4-Q36MCQeasy

A schedule is NOT recoverable when there are transactions Ti and Tj such that Tj reads a data item written by Ti, and

  1. Ti commits before Tj commits.
  2. Tj aborts after Ti has committed.
  3. both transactions read the item before either of them writes it.
  4. Tj commits before Ti commits.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recoverability is about being able to undo a transaction that others have read from. If the reader commits first and the writer then aborts, the reader’s commit rests on a value that never existed, and there is no way to take it back.

Step by step.

  1. Tj reads a value written by Ti, so Tj depends on Ti.
  2. A recoverable schedule must delay Tj’s commit until Ti has committed.
  3. If Tj commits first and Ti then aborts, Tj has already published a value derived from a transaction that was rolled back.
  4. That is exactly the definition of a non-recoverable schedule.

Answer: Option (D) — the reader commits before the writer.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the recoverable case: the writer finishes first, so a rollback can still cascade to the reader.
  • (B) is harmless: an aborted reader leaves nothing behind.
  • (C) describes a read–read pair, which is not a dependency at all.

Common misconception. Confusing recoverability with "no dirty reads". A schedule can have dirty reads and still be recoverable, as long as the readers wait for the writer to finish.

Takeaway. Reader commits first, writer aborts later ⇒ non-recoverable.

DB-U4-Q37MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about a strict schedule is TRUE?

  1. Every strict schedule is cascadeless, and therefore recoverable as well.
  2. A strict schedule can still be non-recoverable when two transactions interleave their writes.
  3. Strictness is a property of the locking protocol alone; no schedule can be strict unless locks are used.
  4. A strict schedule can never contain a blind write.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A strict schedule forbids a transaction from reading or writing an item until the last transaction that wrote it has committed or aborted. Cascadelessness forbids only the read part. So strict is the stronger condition, and the implications run one way.

Step by step.

  1. Strict forbids both dirty reads and dirty writes.
  2. Cascadeless forbids only dirty reads, so every strict schedule is cascadeless.
  3. Cascadeless forbids every dependency on an uncommitted value, so the commit of a reader can never precede the commit of its writer; every cascadeless schedule is recoverable.
  4. Chaining the two gives: strict ⇒ cascadeless ⇒ recoverable.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Strictness is defined on the schedule, and it already rules out the dependency that non-recoverability needs.
  • (C) Locks are one way to enforce strictness, not the definition of it; the definition talks only about when reads and writes may occur.
  • (D) A blind write is a write without a read; it is perfectly compatible with strictness, which constrains the order of writes, not their presence.

Common misconception. Treating strict and cascadeless as synonyms, or believing the implication runs both ways.

Takeaway. strict ⊂ cascadeless ⊂ recoverable ⊂ all schedules.

DB-U4-Q38MCQhard

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w1(X); r2(X); w2(Y); c2; a1, in which T1 finally aborts. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. S is recoverable, because rolling back T1 leaves T2’s commit valid.
  2. S is cascadeless, because T2 wrote only Y, which T1 never touched.
  3. S is not recoverable, because T2 read X from T1 and committed before T1 terminated.
  4. S is strict, because T1’s abort has no effect on the value of Y.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Check the dependency first, then the commit order. T2 read a value written by T1, so T2 must not commit until T1 has committed. A schedule that breaks that rule cannot be repaired by any later action.

Step by step.

  1. r2(X) follows w1(X), so T2 reads from T1 — a dirty read, since T1 has not committed.
  2. c2 comes before a1, so T2 commits while T1 is still unresolved.
  3. T1 then aborts, so the value of X that T2 used is rolled back.
  4. T2’s committed effect, which was derived from that value, can no longer be undone. The schedule is not recoverable.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Recoverability is decided by the commit order of reader and writer, and here it is the wrong way round.
  • (B) Cascadelessness forbids exactly the dirty read in step 1, regardless of which item was written later.
  • (D) Strictness requires that no other transaction read or write X before T1 finished; r2(X) breaks that.

Common misconception. Thinking the writer’s abort is enough to fix things. Once the reader has committed, a cascading rollback is impossible.

Takeaway. A dirty read plus a reader that commits first is the textbook non-recoverable pattern.

DB-U4-Q39MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every strict schedule is cascadeless.
  2. Every cascadeless schedule is recoverable.
  3. Every recoverable schedule is cascadeless.
  4. A schedule in which no transaction reads uncommitted data is necessarily conflict serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three nested classes: strict schedules inside cascadeless schedules inside recoverable schedules. Serializability is a different axis altogether — it is about the order of conflicting operations, not about when commits happen.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. Strict forbids dirty reads and dirty writes; cascadeless forbids dirty reads. The stronger condition implies the weaker.
  2. (B) is true. With no dirty read there is no dependency on an uncommitted value, so every reader commits after its writer and the schedule is recoverable.
  3. (C) is false. A recoverable schedule may still contain a dirty read, provided the reader commits only after the writer. It then needs cascading rollback but stays recoverable.
  4. (D) is false. Cascadelessness says nothing about the order of conflicting operations between transactions that never share uncommitted data, so a cascadeless schedule can still be non-serializable.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Merging the recoverability family with the serializability family. A schedule can be serializable but non-recoverable, and recoverable but non-serializable.

Takeaway. Recoverability and serializability answer different questions and neither implies the other.

DB-U4-Q40NATeasy

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w1(X); r2(X); r1(Y); w2(Y), and suppose T1 aborts. Under cascading rollback, what is the total number of transactions that are rolled back, counting T1 itself?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Cascading rollback propagates along dirty reads: if Tj read an item from Ti and Ti rolls back, Tj must roll back too, and so on transitively.

Step by step.

  1. T1 aborts: rollback count = 1.
  2. r2(X) read X after w1(X), so T2 depends on T1.
  3. T2 must therefore roll back as well: rollback count = 2.
  4. T2 wrote only Y and no third transaction read it, so the cascade stops.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting only T1, or adding T1 twice because it both writes and aborts.

Takeaway. Follow the reads-from edges out of the aborted transaction; each reachable transaction rolls back once.

schedules-serializability-conflict-view · 10
DB-U4-Q06MCQeasy

Two operations conflict when they belong to different transactions, access the same data item, and at least one of them is a write. Which one of the following pairs of operations does NOT conflict?

  1. r1(X) and w2(X)
  2. w1(X) and r2(X)
  3. w1(X) and w2(X)
  4. r1(X) and r2(X)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Conflict is defined on three conditions together: different transactions, same data item, and at least one write. Two reads satisfy the first two conditions but fail the third, so their order can never be observed.

Step by step.

  1. (A) read-write on X from different transactions: conflicts.
  2. (B) write-read on X: conflicts.
  3. (C) write-write on X: conflicts.
  4. (D) read-read on X: both operations see the same value whatever their order, so swapping them changes nothing. No conflict.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) each contain a write on the same item from two transactions, so all three are conflicting pairs.

Common misconception. Assuming any two operations on the same item conflict. Read-read pairs are the single exception, and that exception is what lets the precedence graph be sparse.

Takeaway. Three conflict types: read-write, write-read, write-write. Read-read never conflicts.

DB-U4-Q07MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about conflict serializability and view serializability is TRUE?

  1. Every view-serializable schedule is conflict-serializable.
  2. Every conflict-serializable schedule is view-serializable.
  3. The two notions coincide for every schedule.
  4. A schedule whose precedence graph contains a cycle can still be conflict-serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Conflict equivalence implies view equivalence, because if two schedules agree on the order of every conflicting pair they necessarily agree on who reads which version and on the final writer. The reverse implication fails, and blind writes are the standard counterexample.

Step by step.

  1. Conflict equivalence compares every conflicting pair; view equivalence compares only the reads-from relation and the final write of each item.
  2. Agreeing on more (conflicts) forces agreement on less (views), so conflict-serializable implies view-serializable.
  3. A concrete counterexample is S = r1(X); w2(X); w1(X); w3(X). The conflict graph has T1 → T2 (from r1(X) and w2(X)) and T2 → T1 (from w2(X) and w1(X)), a cycle, so S is not conflict-serializable. Yet S is view-equivalent to the serial order T1, T2, T3: there too T1 reads the initial value of X and T3 performs the final write.
  4. Hence (B) is true and (A) is false.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses the implication; view serializability is the weaker, more permissive notion.
  • (C) fails because of blind-write schedules.
  • (D) is false by definition: an acyclic precedence graph is exactly the test for conflict serializability.

Common misconception. Treating the two definitions as interchangeable because both are 'serializable'. Conflict serializability is the practical notion (testable in polynomial time); view serializability is the theoretical superset (NP-complete to test).

Takeaway. Conflict-serializable ⊂ view-serializable, strictly. Precedence graph acyclic ⇔ conflict-serializable.

DB-U4-Q08MCQmedium

Consider the schedule S = r1(A); r2(A); w1(A); w2(A) of two transactions on a single data item A. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. S is conflict-serializable.
  2. S is view-serializable but not conflict-serializable.
  3. S is neither conflict-serializable nor view-serializable.
  4. S is both conflict-serializable and view-serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A schedule is conflict-serializable exactly when its precedence graph is acyclic. View serializability needs the same reads-from relation and the same final writer as some serial schedule; with no blind writes involved, a cycle in the conflict graph is fatal to both.

Step by step.

  1. Conflicts: r2(A) vs w1(A) gives the edge T2 → T1; r1(A) vs w2(A) gives T1 → T2; w1(A) vs w2(A) gives T1 → T2. The graph has a two-cycle, so S is not conflict-serializable.
  2. Try the serial order T1, T2: there T2 reads the value written by T1, whereas in S both transactions read the initial value. Different reads-from relation.
  3. Try the serial order T2, T1: there T1 reads the value written by T2, again different from S.
  4. The final writer in S is T2, which both serial orders also give, but the reads-from relation already rules out both, so S is not view-serializable either.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (D) fail on the two-cycle in the precedence graph.
  • (B) fails because no serial schedule reproduces the reads-from relation; view serializability is not rescued by the final write agreeing.

Common misconception. Checking only the final writer and declaring the schedule view-serializable. View equivalence requires the reads-from relation as well.

Takeaway. With no blind writes, view serializability coincides with conflict serializability, so a cycle settles both at once.

DB-U4-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about serializability are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A schedule is conflict-serializable if and only if its precedence graph is acyclic.
  2. Every conflict-serializable schedule is view-serializable, and every view-serializable schedule is conflict-serializable.
  3. Blind writes can make a schedule view-serializable even though it is not conflict-serializable.
  4. Every serial schedule is conflict-serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The precedence (serialization) graph is the decision procedure for conflict serializability: nodes are transactions, an edge Ti → Tj means Ti must precede Tj, and the schedule is conflict-serializable exactly when the graph is acyclic. View serializability is strictly weaker because blind writes may be reordered harmlessly.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. This is the serialization-graph theorem; a topological order of an acyclic graph is the equivalent serial schedule.
  2. (B) False. The forward implication holds, the reverse does not; view serializability is the strictly larger class.
  3. (C) True. Example: w1(X) w2(X) w3(X) where T1 and T2 both write blindly. The conflict graph has a cycle, yet the schedule is view-equivalent to the serial order T3, T1, T2 once the overwritten blind write of T2 is disregarded.
  4. (D) True. A serial schedule has no conflicting operations in an interleaved order at all, so its graph has no edges and is trivially acyclic.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the only false statement: it asserts the two notions coincide, which blind-write schedules disprove.

Common misconception. Assuming the two definitions are equivalent because every textbook example is conflict-serializable. View serializability is a strictly larger, NP-complete-to-test class.

Takeaway. Acyclic precedence graph is the examinable test; blind writes are the reason view serializability is larger.

DB-U4-Q10NAThard

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w1(X); r2(X); w2(X); r3(Y); w3(Y) of three transactions over data items X and Y. How many of the 3! = 6 serial schedules of T1, T2 and T3 are conflict-equivalent to S?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two schedules are conflict-equivalent when they order every conflicting pair of operations the same way. Equivalently, the set of serial schedules equivalent to S is exactly the set of topological orders of S's precedence graph.

Step by step.

  1. Conflicts on X: r1(X) vs w2(X), w1(X) vs r2(X), and w1(X) vs w2(X). All three give the edge T1 → T2.
  2. Conflicts on Y: none, because T3 touches only Y.
  3. The precedence graph is the single edge T1 → T2; T3 is isolated, so it may be placed anywhere relative to the pair.
  4. Topological orders: T1 T2 T3, T1 T3 T2 and T3 T1 T2. That is 3 of the 6 serial schedules.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting 2 by keeping T3 in its original position, or counting 6 by forgetting that T1 must precede T2. The count is the number of linear extensions of the partial order, which is 3!/(the constraints imposed) = 3 here.

Takeaway. Number of conflict-equivalent serial schedules = number of topological orders of the precedence graph. An isolated node multiplies the count by its number of insertion slots.

DB-U4-Q31MCQeasy

Two operations conflict when they belong to different transactions, access the same data item, and at least one of them is a write. Which one of the following pairs of operations does NOT conflict?

  1. r1(X) and w2(X)
  2. w1(X) and w2(X)
  3. r1(X) and r2(X)
  4. w1(X) and r2(X)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A conflict needs three things at once: different transactions, the same data item, and at least one write. Two reads on the same item fail the third condition, because neither operation can change what the other sees.

Step by step.

  1. (A) r1(X) and w2(X): same item, different transactions, one write. Conflict (read–write).
  2. (B) w1(X) and w2(X): two writes on the same item. Conflict (write–write).
  3. (C) r1(X) and r2(X): two reads on the same item. Neither changes the value, so their order cannot matter. No conflict.
  4. (D) w1(X) and r2(X): one write. Conflict (write–read).

Answer: Option (C) — two reads never conflict.

Why the other options are wrong. Each of (A), (B) and (D) contains a write on X by a different transaction, so swapping the two operations could change a value that someone reads.

Common misconception. Believing that any two operations on the same item conflict. Read–read is the one combination that is always harmless.

Takeaway. Conflicts come in three shapes: R–W, W–R and W–W. R–R is not one of them.

DB-U4-Q32MCQmedium

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w2(X); r3(X); w1(Y); w3(Y) of three transactions over data items X and Y. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. S is conflict serializable, and it is conflict equivalent to the serial order T1, T2, T3.
  2. S is conflict serializable, and it is conflict equivalent to the serial order T2, T1, T3.
  3. S is not conflict serializable, because the precedence graph contains the cycle T1 → T2 → T1.
  4. S is conflict serializable, and it is conflict equivalent to the serial order T1, T3, T2.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Draw the precedence graph: an edge Ti → Tj for every pair of conflicting operations in which Ti comes first. The schedule is conflict serializable exactly when that graph is acyclic, and any topological order of it is an equivalent serial order.

Step by step.

  1. r1(X) before w2(X): edge T1 → T2.
  2. w2(X) before r3(X): edge T2 → T3.
  3. w1(Y) before w3(Y): edge T1 → T3.
  4. r1(X) and r3(X) are both reads, so they add no edge; the two writes are on different items, so they add none either.
  5. The graph is T1 → T2 → T3 with the shortcut T1 → T3. It is acyclic, and the only topological order is T1, T2, T3.

Answer: Option (A) — acyclic, equivalent to T1, T2, T3.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) The edge T1 → T2 forbids putting T2 first.
  • (C) There is no edge T2 → T1 anywhere: the only operations T2 and T1 share are on X, and there r1(X) precedes w2(X).
  • (D) The edge T2 → T3 forbids putting T3 before T2.

Common misconception. Counting r1(X) and r3(X) as a conflict and inventing an edge T1 → T3 (or T3 → T1) that the schedule does not justify.

Takeaway. Read the graph off the conflicting pairs only, then topologically sort it.

DB-U4-Q33MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about view serializability are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every view-serializable schedule is conflict serializable.
  2. Every conflict-serializable schedule is view serializable.
  3. Deciding whether a given schedule is view serializable is NP-complete.
  4. A schedule that contains a blind write can be view serializable without being conflict serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. View equivalence is weaker than conflict equivalence. Two schedules are view equivalent if they have the same reads-from relation and the same final write on every item; they are conflict equivalent only if one can be turned into the other by swapping adjacent non-conflicting operations.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is false. The inclusion runs the other way: conflict serializability implies view serializability, not the reverse.
  2. (B) is true. If a schedule is conflict equivalent to a serial schedule, it reads the same values from the same transactions and has the same final writes, so it is also view equivalent to it.
  3. (C) is true. View serializability has no polynomial test; the decision problem is NP-complete, which is why practical schedulers settle for conflict serializability.
  4. (D) is true. Blind writes are the extra freedom view equivalence allows: the writer does not read, so its position relative to other writes can matter less than conflict equivalence assumes.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the two notions coincide because they coincide on schedules without blind writes. That is exactly the case where the difference disappears.

Takeaway. Conflict serializable ⊂ view serializable ⊂ serializable, and only the first has an efficient test.

DB-U4-Q34MCQhard

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w2(X); w1(X); w3(X) of three transactions over the single data item X, where every write stores a distinct value. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. S is conflict serializable but not view serializable.
  2. S is view serializable but not conflict serializable.
  3. S is both conflict serializable and view serializable.
  4. S is neither conflict serializable nor view serializable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Test the two notions separately. The precedence graph settles conflict serializability. For view serializability, compare the reads-from relation and the final write with those of a serial schedule.

Step by step.

  1. Conflicts on X: r1(X) before w2(X) gives T1 → T2.
  2. w2(X) before w1(X) gives T2 → T1. The graph already contains the cycle T1 → T2 → T1, so S is not conflict serializable.
  3. Now look at what S actually does: T1 reads the initial value of X (no write precedes r1(X)), and the last write is w3(X).
  4. The serial schedule T1, T2, T3 gives exactly the same two facts: T1 reads the initial value, and T3 writes last. T2 and T3 are blind writers, so nothing they read can differ.
  5. Hence S is view equivalent to the serial order T1, T2, T3, and is view serializable.

Answer: Option (B) — view serializable, not conflict serializable.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) fail because the cycle T1 → T2 → T1 is real: two writes to X by different transactions are in the order w2 then w1, which is the reverse of the order r1 then w2.
  • (D) fails because of step 4: the serial order T1, T2, T3 reproduces every read and the final write.

Common misconception. Stopping at the precedence graph. A cycle rules out conflict serializability, not serializability itself; a blind write is precisely the case where the extra freedom matters.

Takeaway. Blind writes are the reason view serializability is strictly weaker than conflict serializability.

DB-U4-Q35NATmedium

Consider the schedule S = r1(X); w2(X); r2(Y); w3(Y); w1(Y) of three transactions over data items X and Y. How many edges does the precedence (serialization) graph of S contain?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The precedence graph has one edge Ti → Tj for each pair of conflicting operations in which Ti executes first. Several conflicting pairs between the same two transactions still give a single edge.

Step by step.

  1. On X: r1(X) precedes w2(X), so edge T1 → T2.
  2. On Y: r2(Y) precedes w3(Y), so edge T2 → T3.
  3. On Y: r2(Y) precedes w1(Y), so edge T2 → T1.
  4. On Y: w3(Y) precedes w1(Y), so edge T3 → T1.
  5. Distinct edges: T1 → T2, T2 → T3, T2 → T1 and T3 → T1, that is 4.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Counting conflicting operation pairs instead of distinct edges, which double-counts the T2 → T1 pair, or forgetting the write–write pair w3(Y), w1(Y).

Takeaway. List the conflicting pairs, then collapse them into distinct transaction edges. (The graph has the cycle T1 → T2 → T1, so S is not conflict serializable — but the edge count is what was asked.)

timestamp-ordering-and-deadlocks · 10
DB-U4-Q21MCQeasy

In basic timestamp ordering, the read operation r(X) of a transaction T is rejected when

  1. TS(T) < RTS(X).
  2. TS(T) > WTS(X).
  3. TS(T) < WTS(X).
  4. T holds no lock on X.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Timestamp ordering keeps, for every data item, the largest timestamp of any transaction that has read it (RTS) and of any that has written it (WTS). A read by T must behave as though it happened at time TS(T): if a younger transaction has already written X, the read cannot be placed before that write, so T is aborted and restarted.

Step by step.

  1. For a read r(X), the rule compares TS(T) with WTS(X).
  2. If TS(T) < WTS(X), some younger transaction has already written X, so a read at time TS(T) would have to see that write; the schedule cannot be rearranged to allow it, so T is rejected.
  3. Otherwise the read proceeds and RTS(X) is updated to the maximum of its old value and TS(T).
  4. Hence the rejecting condition is TS(T) < WTS(X).

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) TS(T) < RTS(X) is not a rejection condition: a reader older than the last reader is always allowed.
  • (B) a read older than nothing in particular is irrelevant; the comparison that matters is against the last write.
  • (D) timestamp ordering uses no locks at all; that is precisely why it cannot deadlock.

Common misconception. Mixing up the read rule with the write rule. Read compares against WTS; write compares against both WTS and RTS (and Thomas's rule modifies the write case only).

Takeaway. Read rule: reject if TS(T) < WTS(X). Write rule: reject if TS(T) < WTS(X) or TS(T) < RTS(X), with Thomas's rule replacing the first rejection by ignoring the write.

DB-U4-Q22MCQmedium

Under basic timestamp ordering, a write w(X) by T with TS(T) < WTS(X) is rejected. Under Thomas's write rule the same operation is

  1. always rejected.
  2. ignored when RTS(X) ≤ TS(T), because the value it would write is already obsolete.
  3. executed after waiting for the younger writer to commit.
  4. ignored whenever TS(T) < WTS(X), whatever RTS(X) is.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Thomas's write rule observes that a write which is older than the current value of the item and which nobody has read in the meantime can never be observed: the value it produces is overwritten by the younger write before any read can see it. Discarding such a write keeps the schedule view-equivalent to a serial one and avoids an unnecessary abort.

Step by step.

  1. Basic rule: if TS(T) < WTS(X), a younger transaction has already written X, so T is aborted.
  2. Thomas's refinement adds a second condition: if additionally RTS(X) ≤ TS(T), no transaction has read the younger version, so T's write would be invisible anyway.
  3. In that case the write is simply skipped (an obsolete write) and T continues; only when RTS(X) > TS(T) must T be aborted, because a reader has already seen a version that T's write would have to precede.
  4. Hence Thomas's rule ignores the write when RTS(X) ≤ TS(T), which is option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the basic rule, which Thomas's rule relaxes.
  • (C) timestamp ordering never waits, so no operation is ever delayed for a commit.
  • (D) drops the RTS condition, which would discard a write that a reader has already observed.

Common misconception. Applying Thomas's rule to reads as well. It refines the write rule only; reads are still rejected whenever TS(T) < WTS(X).

Takeaway. Thomas's write rule = ignore obsolete writes. It makes timestamp ordering tolerate some out-of-order writes without aborting, and it is the reason the resulting schedules are view-serializable rather than conflict-serializable.

DB-U4-Q23MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about deadlock detection is TRUE?

  1. A cycle in the wait-for graph means that a deadlock exists, and the cycle may involve any number of transactions.
  2. A cycle in the wait-for graph is necessary but not sufficient for a deadlock.
  3. Timestamp ordering can deadlock, so it needs a wait-for graph.
  4. Wait-die and wound-wait can deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The wait-for graph has one node per active transaction and an edge Ti → Tj when Ti is waiting for a lock held by Tj. A cycle is both necessary and sufficient for deadlock, because each transaction in the cycle waits for the next and none can proceed.

Step by step.

  1. A transaction blocked on a lock is waiting for the holder to release it.
  2. If the wait-for edges form a cycle, every member of the cycle waits for another member, so none can ever proceed: deadlock.
  3. If there is no cycle, the waits form a partial order and the transaction at the end of the longest chain will eventually finish.
  4. The cycle may be of length 2 (the common case) or longer; nothing limits it to two transactions.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses necessity and sufficiency: a cycle is exactly the criterion, not merely a symptom.
  • (C) timestamp ordering never makes a transaction wait, so it cannot deadlock and needs no wait-for graph.
  • (D) wait-die and wound-wait are deadlock-prevention schemes based on timestamps; one of the two transactions is always aborted rather than allowed to wait cyclically.

Common misconception. Thinking only two-transaction deadlocks matter. Cycles of any length occur, and a cycle of length 3 is common with three-way lock orders.

Takeaway. Cycle in wait-for graph ⇔ deadlock. Detection means finding a cycle; resolution means aborting a victim in it.

DB-U4-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Basic timestamp ordering is free of deadlock because no transaction ever waits for another.
  2. Thomas's write rule ignores an obsolete write instead of aborting the writing transaction.
  3. In wait-die, an older transaction waits for a younger one, while a younger transaction that requests an item held by an older one is aborted.
  4. In wound-wait, an older transaction that requests an item held by a younger one is aborted.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Timestamp-based protocols avoid deadlock by never blocking: a transaction either proceeds, has its operation skipped, or is aborted and restarted. The two prevention schemes, wait-die and wound-wait, decide by age who waits and who dies, always in the same direction, which is what rules out cycles.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Timestamp ordering decides every operation immediately against RTS and WTS; a transaction is aborted rather than queued, so no wait-for edge ever exists.
  2. (B) True. That is the definition of Thomas's write rule.
  3. (C) True. Wait-die: the older transaction is allowed to wait for the younger; the younger is aborted (dies) if it asks for an item held by an older one.
  4. (D) False. Wound-wait is the mirror image: the older transaction wounds (aborts) the younger holder, and the younger waits for the older.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) reverses wound-wait: in wound-wait the older preempts the younger, so an older requester is never the one aborted.

Common misconception. Swapping the two schemes. Mnemonic: wait-die is 'old waits, young dies'; wound-wait is 'old wounds, young waits'.

Takeaway. Both schemes are deadlock-free because waits always go in one direction of age; both can starve, which restarts with the original timestamp are meant to bound.

DB-U4-Q25NAThard

Transactions T1, T2 and T3 start with timestamps 10, 20 and 30 respectively. Basic timestamp ordering (not Thomas's write rule) is used, and the operations below are executed in the order shown: 1. r1(X) 2. w3(X) 3. r2(X) 4. w2(Y) 5. r3(Y) 6. w1(Y). How many transactions are aborted during this execution?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every operation is checked against the read and write timestamps of its item. A read is rejected when TS(T) < WTS(X); a write is rejected when TS(T) < WTS(X) or TS(T) < RTS(X). A rejected operation aborts its transaction, whose later operations are then not executed.

Step by step.

  1. 1. r1(X): WTS(X) is empty, so the read is granted and RTS(X) = 10.
  2. 2. w3(X): TS(T3) = 30 ≥ WTS(X) and TS(T3) = 30 ≥ RTS(X) = 10, so the write is granted and WTS(X) = 30.
  3. 3. r2(X): TS(T2) = 20 < WTS(X) = 30, so the read is rejected and T2 is aborted. That is 1 abort.
  4. 4. w2(Y): T2 has already been aborted, so this operation is not executed.
  5. 5. r3(Y): TS(T3) = 30 ≥ WTS(Y) = 0, so the read is granted and RTS(Y) = 30.
  6. 6. w1(Y): TS(T1) = 10 ≥ WTS(Y) = 0 but TS(T1) = 10 < RTS(Y) = 30, so the write is rejected and T1 is aborted. That is 2 aborts.
  7. Total aborts = 2, one at step 3 and one at step 6.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Checking only WTS and forgetting the read timestamp, which is what rejects the write at step 6; or executing step 4 for an already aborted T2.

Takeaway. Simulate timestamp ordering with a table of RTS and WTS per item and check every operation against both timestamps; a rejected operation removes the transaction from the rest of the schedule.

DB-U4-Q46MCQeasy

In timestamp-based concurrency control, the timestamp of a transaction is normally assigned

  1. when the transaction issues its first write.
  2. when the transaction commits.
  3. when the transaction starts.
  4. by the order in which its operations happen to reach the scheduler.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Timestamp ordering enforces the serial order in which transactions started. The timestamp must therefore be fixed at start time and must never change, or the protocol would have no fixed serial order to enforce.

Step by step.

  1. The scheduler assigns TS(T) when T begins, typically from a counter or the system clock.
  2. Every data item carries the largest timestamp of any transaction that has read it (RTS(X)) and written it (WTS(X)).
  3. Each operation is checked against those values, and a transaction whose timestamp is too old is rejected.
  4. Because the timestamp is fixed at start, the accepted operations always reproduce the serial order of the start times.

Answer: Option (C) — at start.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The first write may come long after the transaction began and would not define a consistent order.
  • (B) A commit-time timestamp would order transactions by completion, which is not known in advance and cannot be enforced online.
  • (D) Arrival order at the scheduler is what timestamp ordering replaces, because arrival order can differ from the order in which operations were logically issued.

Common misconception. Confusing the timestamp with a lock request time. It is the start time of the transaction, assigned once.

Takeaway. One timestamp per transaction, fixed at start.

DB-U4-Q47MCQeasy

Which one of the following concurrency-control schemes is free from deadlock?

  1. Basic two-phase locking.
  2. Basic timestamp ordering.
  3. Strict two-phase locking.
  4. Two-phase locking with lock upgrades.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deadlock needs a cycle of transactions each waiting for something another holds. A protocol in which no transaction ever waits cannot form such a cycle.

Step by step.

  1. Timestamp ordering never blocks a request: it either executes the operation or rejects the transaction.
  2. With no waiting there is no wait-for graph to contain a cycle, so no deadlock.
  3. All three 2PL variants in the options do block: a transaction waits for a lock held by another.
  4. Lock upgrades make the waiting worse, since two transactions holding shared locks can each wait for the other to release.

Answer: Option (B) — basic timestamp ordering.

Why the other options are wrong. (A), (C) and (D) all allow a transaction to wait while holding locks, which is the raw material of deadlock; strictness and upgrades change the details, not the possibility.

Common misconception. Believing strictness or conservativeness of the strict kind removes deadlock. Only the conservative (pre-claiming) variant does that, and it is not among the options.

Takeaway. No waiting, no deadlock — the trade is that timestamp ordering aborts instead.

DB-U4-Q48MCQmedium

Under the wait–die scheme, transaction Ti requests a lock held by transaction Tj, and TS(Ti) < TS(Tj), so Ti is the older of the two. What does the scheme do?

  1. Ti waits for Tj to release the lock; a younger requester facing an older holder is rolled back instead.
  2. Ti is rolled back at once, and Tj waits for Ti to restart.
  3. Both transactions are rolled back, so that the deadlock cannot form.
  4. Ti waits, and Tj is rolled back the next time it requests a lock held by Ti.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Wait–die removes deadlock by never letting a younger transaction wait for an older one. The older transaction waits; the younger one dies (rolls back) and restarts with its original timestamp.

Step by step.

  1. TS(Ti) < TS(Tj) means Ti is older.
  2. An older requester is allowed to wait, so Ti blocks until Tj releases.
  3. Had the requester been younger (TS(Ti) > TS(Tj)), it would have been rolled back immediately rather than allowed to wait.
  4. Since waits always run from older to younger, a cycle of waits is impossible, so the scheme is deadlock-free.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes wound–wait, where the older transaction wounds the younger holder.
  • (C) is not part of either scheme; only one transaction is ever a victim.
  • (D) mixes the two schemes and would reintroduce cycles.

Common misconception. Swapping wait–die with wound–wait. In wait–die the older waits and the younger dies; in wound–wait the older wounds (rolls back) the younger.

Takeaway. Older waits, younger dies — and a restarted transaction keeps its old timestamp, which is why it eventually wins.

DB-U4-Q49MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about timestamp ordering are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Basic timestamp ordering is deadlock-free because a transaction never waits for a lock; a conflicting operation is rejected instead.
  2. Basic timestamp ordering can roll a transaction back even when the schedule it would have produced was serializable.
  3. Thomas’s write rule ignores an obsolete write instead of rejecting it, and so admits some schedules that basic timestamp ordering rejects.
  4. Basic timestamp ordering on its own guarantees that every schedule it produces is recoverable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Timestamp ordering buys deadlock freedom by rejecting operations instead of waiting. The price is unnecessary aborts, which Thomas’s write rule partly recovers, and the protocol says nothing about commit order, so recoverability needs a separate rule.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. Requests are checked against RTS and WTS and either executed or refused; nothing blocks, so no cycle can form.
  2. (B) is true. The checks are local to one data item, so a transaction can be aborted even though the schedule as a whole would have been serializable.
  3. (C) is true. If w(X) arrives with TS(T) < WTS(X), its value would be overwritten at once and can be ignored rather than causing a rollback, which admits more schedules.
  4. (D) is false. Basic timestamp ordering allows a transaction to read a value written by an uncommitted transaction, so a schedule it accepts can be non-recoverable unless the reads are additionally deferred.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming a serializable protocol is automatically recoverable. Serializability orders operations; recoverability orders commits, and the two are independent.

Takeaway. Timestamp ordering: no deadlock, extra aborts, no built-in recoverability.

DB-U4-Q50NAThard

The read and write timestamps of both X and Y are 0, and TS(T1) = 10, TS(T2) = 20, TS(T3) = 30. The schedule is r1(X); w3(X); r2(X); w2(Y); r1(Y); w3(Y). Using basic timestamp ordering, how many of the six operations are rejected?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each operation is checked against the item’s timestamps: a read is rejected if TS(T) < WTS(X), and a write is rejected if TS(T) < RTS(X) or TS(T) < WTS(X). A rejected operation aborts its transaction; the checks for the other operations still follow the same rule.

Step by step.

  1. r1(X): 10 ≥ WTS(X) = 0, accepted. Now RTS(X) = 10.
  2. w3(X): 30 ≥ RTS(X) = 10 and 30 ≥ WTS(X) = 0, accepted. Now WTS(X) = 30.
  3. r2(X): 20 < WTS(X) = 30, so T2 would read a value written by a younger transaction. Rejected (1).
  4. w2(Y): 20 ≥ RTS(Y) = 0 and 20 ≥ WTS(Y) = 0, accepted. Now WTS(Y) = 20.
  5. r1(Y): 10 < WTS(Y) = 20, rejected (2).
  6. w3(Y): 30 ≥ RTS(Y) = 0 and 30 ≥ WTS(Y) = 20, accepted. Now WTS(Y) = 30.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Checking a read only against WTS but forgetting to update RTS, or checking a write against WTS only. Both timestamps must be maintained on every accepted operation.

Takeaway. Older operations that arrive late are the ones rejected: here two reads by older transactions, both of them too late.

two-phase-locking-2pl-strict-rigorous · 10
DB-U4-Q16MCQeasy

In the two-phase locking protocol, which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. A transaction may release a lock and later acquire a new lock, provided it still holds at least one lock.
  2. Two-phase locking prevents deadlock.
  3. A transaction has a growing phase in which it only acquires locks and a shrinking phase in which it only releases locks.
  4. A transaction may acquire a shared lock during its shrinking phase.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two-phase locking splits each transaction's life into a growing (lock-acquisition) phase followed by a shrinking (lock-release) phase; the boundary is the lock point. Once the first unlock happens, no further lock may be granted to that transaction.

Step by step.

  1. Phase 1, growing: the transaction acquires locks and holds all of them; the number of locks held is non-decreasing.
  2. Phase 2, shrinking: the transaction releases locks and acquires none; the number of locks held is non-increasing.
  3. The instant at which the transaction holds its maximum number of locks is the lock point, and it separates the two phases.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) contradicts the protocol: any acquisition after the first release destroys the two-phase structure and the serializability guarantee.
  • (B) is false; 2PL can deadlock because two transactions may each hold a lock the other needs.
  • (D) is false for the same reason as (A): no lock of any kind may be acquired in the shrinking phase.

Common misconception. Believing 2PL removes deadlock. It guarantees conflict serializability and nothing about deadlock; deadlock avoidance needs ordering, wait-die or wound-wait.

Takeaway. 2PL gives serializability, not deadlock-freedom; the lock point is the moment of maximum held locks.

DB-U4-Q17MCQeasy

Which one of the following is guaranteed by the basic two-phase locking protocol?

  1. Freedom from deadlock.
  2. Recoverability of the schedule.
  3. Freedom from starvation.
  4. Conflict serializability of the schedule.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Basic 2PL guarantees exactly one thing: every schedule it produces is conflict-serializable. It does not order commits, so it does not give recoverability; it does not order lock requests, so it can deadlock; it does not prevent repeated restarts, so it can starve a transaction.

Step by step.

  1. Under 2PL the lock point of each transaction defines a total order that every conflicting pair respects.
  2. Conflicting operations are therefore ordered as they would be in the serial order of the lock points.
  3. That is precisely conflict serializability, and it is the whole of what basic 2PL promises.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2PL can deadlock; classic example: T1 locks X then requests Y while T2 locks Y then requests X.
  • (B) basic 2PL allows a transaction to release a lock before commit, so another transaction may read uncommitted data and commit first: not recoverable.
  • (C) a transaction may be repeatedly chosen as victim and never finish.

Common misconception. Crediting 2PL with more than it delivers. Strict 2PL adds cascadelessness and recoverability; rigorous 2PL adds more; neither adds deadlock-freedom.

Takeaway. Basic 2PL = conflict serializability only. Strict 2PL = + cascadeless/recoverable. Rigorous 2PL = + serial order by commit time.

DB-U4-Q18MCQmedium

A transaction performs the operations LS(X), LX(Y), U(X), LS(Z), U(Y), U(Z), where LS is a shared lock, LX an exclusive lock and U an unlock. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. The sequence obeys basic two-phase locking.
  2. The sequence violates two-phase locking, because a lock is acquired after a lock has been released.
  3. The sequence obeys basic two-phase locking but not strict two-phase locking.
  4. The sequence violates two-phase locking only because it holds two locks at the same time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The test for 2PL is purely structural: scan the operation sequence and check that no lock acquisition follows any release. Holding several locks simultaneously is not a violation; it is what the growing phase looks like.

Step by step.

  1. T1 acquires X and Y, then releases X at the third operation: the shrinking phase has begun.
  2. The fourth operation acquires Z, a lock taken after a release, which is exactly what 2PL forbids.
  3. Hence the sequence is not two-phase, and therefore is neither strict nor rigorous two-phase.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) claim the sequence obeys basic 2PL, which the lock-after-unlock step contradicts.
  • (D) Holding several locks at once is legal and expected during the growing phase; the violation is the re-acquisition.

Common misconception. Looking only at how many locks are held. 2PL is about the shape of the sequence (all acquisitions, then all releases), not the number held at any instant.

Takeaway. To test 2PL, find the first unlock and check that no lock request follows it.

DB-U4-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about two-phase locking are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Basic two-phase locking produces only conflict-serializable schedules.
  2. Strict two-phase locking holds every exclusive lock until the transaction commits or aborts.
  3. Rigorous two-phase locking holds every lock, shared as well as exclusive, until the transaction commits or aborts.
  4. Two-phase locking guarantees that no deadlock can occur.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three variants differ only in when locks are released. Basic 2PL releases anywhere after the lock point; strict 2PL keeps all exclusive locks to the end; rigorous 2PL keeps all locks to the end. The later the release, the stronger the recovery guarantee, and none of them addresses deadlock.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. That is the serialization theorem for 2PL.
  2. (B) True. Holding exclusive locks to commit is what makes strict 2PL cascadeless: no other transaction can read or overwrite uncommitted data.
  3. (C) True. Rigorous 2PL extends the rule to shared locks, which makes the serial order agree with the commit order.
  4. (D) False. 2PL is deadlock-prone by construction, since locks are held while further locks are requested.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the only false statement: deadlock is prevented by lock ordering, wait-die, wound-wait or timestamp ordering, not by 2PL.

Common misconception. Assuming the strongest variant of 2PL also removes deadlock. Stronger release rules strengthen recoverability only.

Takeaway. basic ⊂ strict ⊂ rigorous in terms of what they guarantee, and deadlock-freedom is in none of them.

DB-U4-Q20NAThard

Three transactions are shown with the full sequence of lock and unlock operations each performs on data items X and Y, where LS is a shared lock, LX an exclusive lock and U an unlock.
T1: LS(X), U(X), LX(X), U(X)
T2: LX(X), U(X)
T3: LX(X), LX(Y), U(X), U(Y)
How many of the three transactions obey two-phase locking?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A transaction obeys 2PL when, scanning its operation sequence from left to right, no lock acquisition occurs after the first release. A transaction that locks once and unlocks once, or that locks everything and then releases everything, is in two-phase form.

Step by step.

  1. T1: acquires X, releases X, then acquires X again. A lock is requested after a release, so the growing phase is restarted: violates 2PL.
  2. T2: acquires X and releases it. There is a growing phase (one lock) and a shrinking phase (one unlock) with no re-acquisition: obeys 2PL.
  3. T3: acquires X and Y, then releases X and Y. All acquisitions precede all releases: obeys 2PL.
  4. Two of the three transactions are in two-phase form, so the answer is 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Judging T3 by how many locks it holds, or judging T1 by its last operation only. The test is positional: the first release closes the growing phase for good.

Takeaway. 2PL is checked by the position of the first unlock relative to every later lock request.

DB-U4-Q41MCQeasy

Under strict two-phase locking, when may a transaction release an exclusive lock that it holds?

  1. As soon as it has finished using the item, even if it has not yet committed.
  2. Only when it commits or aborts.
  3. At the end of its growing phase, provided that all its other locks are already held.
  4. Whenever the lock manager decides to grant a conflicting request.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Strict 2PL keeps every exclusive lock until the end of the transaction, which is what stops anyone from reading or overwriting an uncommitted value. That is also why strict 2PL removes cascading rollback.

Step by step.

  1. 2PL allows a growing phase, in which locks are acquired, followed by a shrinking phase, in which they are released.
  2. Strict 2PL adds one restriction: no exclusive lock may be released before the transaction commits or aborts.
  3. The shrinking phase of a strict transaction therefore coincides with its termination.
  4. Releasing earlier would let another transaction read or overwrite a value that might still be rolled back.

Answer: Option (B) — at commit or abort only.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) That is plain lock-based execution, which permits dirty reads.
  • (C) Releasing at the end of the growing phase is basic 2PL, not strict 2PL.
  • (D) Locks are released by their holder, never by the lock manager on someone else’s behalf.

Common misconception. Thinking the shrinking phase must start before commit. In strict 2PL it does not start at all until the transaction ends.

Takeaway. Strict 2PL = 2PL + hold exclusive locks to the end.

DB-U4-Q42MCQeasy

Which one of the following protocols guarantees both conflict serializability and freedom from cascading rollback?

  1. Basic two-phase locking.
  2. Conservative two-phase locking.
  3. Two-phase locking with lock upgrades but without the strictness rule.
  4. Strict two-phase locking.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Serializability and recoverability are separate guarantees. 2PL in any form gives conflict serializability; only the strict form also keeps exclusive locks to the end and so prevents dirty reads.

Step by step.

  1. Basic 2PL: conflict serializable, but locks may be released before commit, so cascading rollback is possible.
  2. Conservative 2PL: deadlock-free and conflict serializable, but it says nothing about when locks are released.
  3. Strict 2PL: 2PL plus hold-exclusive-locks-to-the-end, so no transaction can read an uncommitted value.
  4. No dirty read means no cascading rollback; 2PL means conflict serializable. Both guarantees together belong to strict 2PL.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong. (A) and (C) permit early release, and (B) is silent about release timing; all three can therefore allow cascading rollback.

Common misconception. Believing 2PL alone is enough. Serializability does not stop a reader from reading uncommitted data.

Takeaway. Conflict serializability ← 2PL; no cascading rollback ← strictness. Strict 2PL supplies both.

DB-U4-Q43MSQhard

Which of the following statements about two-phase locking and its variants are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Rigorous two-phase locking holds every lock, shared as well as exclusive, until the transaction commits or aborts, so it is a special case of strict two-phase locking.
  2. Conservative two-phase locking acquires all of a transaction’s locks before it starts, which removes deadlock at the cost of less concurrency.
  3. Basic two-phase locking guarantees freedom from deadlock.
  4. Strict two-phase locking guarantees conflict-serializable schedules.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Keep the guarantees apart: 2PL buys conflict serializability, strictness buys recoverability without cascades, and only the conservative variant buys deadlock freedom — by paying for it with concurrency.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. Rigorous 2PL releases all locks together at commit; since it also holds exclusive locks to the end, it satisfies the strict rule and is the stronger special case.
  2. (B) is true. If every lock is obtained up front, a transaction never waits while holding a lock, so a cycle cannot form. The price is that locks are held while they are still unused.
  3. (C) is false. Basic 2PL is deadlock-prone: T1 can hold X and wait for Y while T2 holds Y and waits for X.
  4. (D) is true. That is the theorem behind 2PL: any schedule produced by a 2PL-compliant set of transactions is conflict serializable.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the growing and shrinking phases make deadlock impossible. They only fix the order of acquisition and release; two transactions can still each hold what the other wants.

Takeaway. 2PL ⇒ serializable; strict ⇒ no cascades; conservative ⇒ no deadlock.

DB-U4-Q44MCQmedium

Under two-phase locking, which one of the following is TRUE about changing the mode of a lock that the transaction already holds?

  1. An upgrade from shared to exclusive must happen in the growing phase, and a downgrade from exclusive to shared may only happen in the shrinking phase.
  2. An upgrade and a downgrade may both happen in either phase.
  3. An upgrade must happen in the shrinking phase and a downgrade in the growing phase.
  4. Lock conversions are not permitted in two-phase locking at all.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An upgrade asks for more privilege, which is an acquisition, so it belongs to the growing phase. A downgrade gives privilege back, which is a release, so it belongs to the shrinking phase.

Step by step.

  1. The growing phase may only add locks or strengthen them.
  2. An upgrade S(X) → X(X) strengthens a lock, so it must occur while the transaction is still growing.
  3. The shrinking phase may only drop locks or weaken them.
  4. A downgrade X(X) → S(X) weakens a lock, so it can occur only after the growing phase has ended.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong. (B) would let a transaction strengthen a lock after it had begun releasing others, which is exactly what 2PL forbids. (C) inverts the two phases. (D) is false: upgrades are standard practice and are what let a transaction read an item and then write it.

Common misconception. Treating an upgrade as "not a new lock request". It is one, and it must respect the phase rule — and it can also deadlock two transactions that hold shared locks and both try to upgrade.

Takeaway. Strengthen while growing, weaken while shrinking.

DB-U4-Q45NATmedium

The wait-for graph of a system holds six transactions and seven edges, and contains exactly two disjoint cycles, one of length 3 and one of length 2. Under deadlock detection with abort-based victim selection, what is the minimum number of transactions that must be aborted to make the graph acyclic?(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A deadlock is a cycle in the wait-for graph, and aborting one transaction on a cycle breaks that cycle. Disjoint cycles need one victim each, because no single transaction lies on both.

Step by step.

  1. The graph has two cycles and they are disjoint, so they share no transaction.
  2. One abort on the 3-cycle removes all three of its edges and leaves the 2-cycle untouched.
  3. The 2-cycle still needs its own victim.
  4. Two aborts therefore suffice, and one cannot: a single victim lies on at most one of the two cycles.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Taking the length of a cycle as the number of victims, or the total edge count. Only the number of disjoint cycles matters.

Takeaway. Minimum victims = number of disjoint cycles in the wait-for graph.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 5 — Indexing, B-Trees & Storage
b-trees-and-b-plus-trees-order-capacity · 15
DB-U5-Q13MCQeasy

In a B+ tree index, the record pointers that lead to the actual data records appear

  1. in every node of the tree.
  2. only in the leaf nodes.
  3. only in the root node.
  4. only in the internal nodes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A B+ tree separates navigation from retrieval: internal nodes hold only keys and child pointers, and every data pointer lives in a leaf.

Step by step.

  1. Internal nodes guide the search downward using separator keys; they store no record pointers.
  2. Leaves hold the keys together with the pointers to the records (or to the record blocks).
  3. Because all data pointers are at one level, every search costs the same number of block accesses.

Answer: Option (B) — only in the leaf nodes

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) That describes a B tree, where data pointers may also sit in internal nodes.
  • (C) The root holds at most a few separator keys and child pointers.
  • (D) Internal nodes carry keys and child pointers only, which is exactly what keeps the fan-out high.

Common misconception. Confusing the B tree and the B+ tree. In a B tree a data pointer can appear in any node; in a B+ tree the data pointers are confined to the leaves.

Takeaway. B tree: keys and data pointers everywhere. B+ tree: keys everywhere, data pointers only at the bottom, leaves chained for ranges.

DB-U5-Q14NATmedium

A B+ tree internal node holds p tree pointers and p − 1 search keys. The block size is 1024 bytes, a key is 10 bytes and a pointer is 6 bytes. What is the largest p that fits in one internal node?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The order of a B+ tree is limited by how many pointer-plus-key pairs fit in a block. An internal node pays for p pointers and p − 1 keys.

Step by step.

  1. Space used = 6p + 10(p − 1) = 16p − 10 bytes.
  2. Require 16p − 10 ≤ 1024, so 16p ≤ 1034 and p ≤ 64.625.
  3. p is an integer, so the largest value is 64.

Answer: 64 (the value of p, not the byte count)

Common misconception. Forgetting the p − 1 and writing 16p ≤ 1024, which gives 64 as well here but fails on other numbers; or rounding 64.625 up to 65, which does not fit.

Takeaway. Write the node's byte equation first, then floor. The p − 1 key count is what distinguishes an internal node from a leaf.

DB-U5-Q15MCQmedium

In the same B+ tree, a leaf node holds up to p keys, p record pointers, and one extra pointer to the next leaf. Keys are 10 bytes, pointers 6 bytes, and a block is 1024 bytes. The largest number of keys in a leaf is

  1. 63
  2. 64
  3. 62
  4. 85
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A leaf pays for p keys, p record pointers, and one additional sibling pointer that internal nodes do not have. That extra pointer is what makes the leaf capacity differ from the internal fan-out.

Step by step.

  1. Space used = 10p + 6p + 6 = 16p + 6 bytes.
  2. Require 16p + 6 ≤ 1024, so 16p ≤ 1018 and p ≤ 63.625.
  3. The largest integer is 63.

Answer: Option (A) — 63 keys

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 64 is the internal-node fan-out; the leaf's sibling pointer costs one slot, so a leaf holds one fewer.
  • (C) 62 looks like a safe round-down of 63.625 taken one step too far; the true floor is 63.
  • (D) 85 ignores the record pointers and the sibling pointer entirely.

Common misconception. Reusing the internal-node answer. The leaf has one extra pointer and no p − 1 discount, so its capacity is a separate calculation.

Takeaway. Internal node: p pointers, p − 1 keys. Leaf: p keys, p record pointers, 1 sibling pointer. Two equations, two capacities.

DB-U5-Q16NAThard

A B+ tree has three levels: a root, one level of internal nodes, and leaves. Every internal node including the root holds at most 64 tree pointers, and every leaf holds at most 63 records. What is the largest number of records the tree can index?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Capacity is a product along the path: each internal node multiplies the number of children, and each leaf contributes its record capacity.

Step by step.

  1. The root has at most 64 children, so there are at most 64 internal nodes on the middle level.
  2. Each of those has at most 64 children, so there are at most 64 × 64 = 4,096 leaves.
  3. Each leaf holds at most 63 records, so the tree indexes at most 4,096 × 63 = 258,048 records.

Answer: 258,048 records

Common misconception. Multiplying 64 × 64 × 64, which uses the pointer count as if it were the leaf record count. Leaves store records, not pointers, and their capacity is 63.

Takeaway. Count leaves first, then multiply by leaf capacity. Internal levels contribute fan-out; only leaves contribute records.

DB-U5-Q17MCQeasy

A B-tree has order p = 5, meaning a node holds at most 5 tree pointers. The maximum number of keys in one node is

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a B-tree of order p a node has at most p children and, between those children, at most p − 1 keys: k1 P1 k2 P2 … Pp−1 kp−1 Pp.

Step by step.
