  1. A node is laid out as k1 P1 k2 P2 … Pp−1 kp−1 Pp: every child pointer after the first is preceded by one key.
  2. So the key count is always one less than the pointer count.
  3. With p = 5 pointers the maximum key count is 5 − 1 = 4.

Answer: Option (B) — 4 keys

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 3 is a slip from halving the order, ⌈5/2⌉, which has nothing to do with the key count.
  • (C) 5 is the pointer count, which is the order, not the key count.
  • (D) 6 exceeds the pointer count, which is impossible in a B-tree node.

Common misconception. Quoting the order as the key capacity. The order counts children; keys are always one fewer.

Takeaway. Order p → at most p children and p − 1 keys. Both B trees and B+ tree internal nodes obey this.

DB-U5-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE of B+ trees? (Select all that apply.)

  1. All leaves are at the same level.
  2. Internal nodes contain only keys and pointers, never data records.
  3. Because the leaves are linked in sequence, range queries are efficient.
  4. An internal node of order p can hold at most p keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A B+ tree is a balanced search structure whose internal nodes exist only to route searches, with all data at the leaf level and the leaves chained together.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: every leaf is at the same depth, which is what keeps every search the same length.
  2. (B) True: internal nodes carry separator keys and child pointers only.
  3. (C) True: once a leaf is reached, a range scan walks the sibling chain instead of re-descending the tree.
  4. (D) False: an internal node of order p holds at most p − 1 keys, because it has p children.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the only false statement; it uses the pointer count as the key count.

Common misconception. Accepting “p keys” for an internal node. The order counts children, so keys are p − 1.

Takeaway. Balance, separator-only internal nodes and linked leaves are the three defining features of a B+ tree; the p versus p − 1 distinction follows from the child-pointer structure.

DB-U5-Q19MSQmedium

A B+ tree has order p = 5: every node holds at most 5 tree pointers, a non-root internal node holds at least ⌈p/2⌉ = 3 pointers, and a non-root leaf holds between ⌈(p − 1)/2⌉ = 2 and p − 1 = 4 keys. Which statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A non-root internal node holds at least 2 keys.
  2. A non-root internal node holds at most 4 keys.
  3. A non-root leaf holds at least 2 keys.
  4. A non-root internal node can hold 5 keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Occupancy bounds follow from the child-pointer bounds: keys are always one fewer than children, and leaves obey their own bound given in the stem.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: at least 3 pointers means at least 3 − 1 = 2 keys.
  2. (B) True: at most 5 pointers means at most 5 − 1 = 4 keys.
  3. (C) True: the stem states the leaf minimum is 2 keys.
  4. (D) False: 5 keys would need 6 pointers, and the node holds at most 5.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the only false statement; it confuses the key bound with the pointer bound.

Common misconception. Applying the pointer bounds directly to keys. Every internal-node statement has to be shifted down by one.

Takeaway. Fix the pointer bounds from p, then subtract one for every key statement. Leaves are separate: they have no child pointers, only record pointers.

DB-U5-Q20MCQhard

A B+ tree indexes 1,000,000 records. A leaf holds at most 63 records and an internal node at most 64 pointers. Assuming no buffering of any node, how many block accesses does a point search need?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of a B+ tree search is the height of the tree in nodes: one access per level from the root down to a leaf.

Step by step.

  1. Leaves needed = ⌈1,000,000 ÷ 63⌉ = 15,874.
  2. Level above = ⌈15,874 ÷ 64⌉ = ⌈248.03⌉ = 249 nodes.
  3. Next level = ⌈249 ÷ 64⌉ = 4 nodes.
  4. Next level = ⌈4 ÷ 64⌉ = 1 node, the root — stop.
  5. Levels = root, 4, 249, 15,874 leaves = 4 levels, so 4 block accesses.

Answer: Option (B) — 4 block accesses

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 3 under-counts: three levels cover only 64 × 64 × 63 ≈ 258,048 records.
  • (C) and (D) over-count by adding levels the fan-out does not require.

Common misconception. Counting only the leaf access, or assuming one access per 64 records. The count is the number of tree levels, and a million records need only four.

Takeaway. Build the tree bottom-up: leaves first, then divide by the fan-out until one root remains. The number of divisions plus one is the search cost.

DB-U5-Q38MCQeasy

A B+ tree internal node holds p tree pointers and p − 1 search keys. The block size is 1024 bytes, a key is 10 bytes and a pointer is 8 bytes. The largest p that fits in one internal node is

  1. 57
  2. 58
  3. 64
  4. 68
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The order of a B+ tree is fixed by the block size: one node holds p pointers and p − 1 keys, and the total must fit inside one block.

Step by step.

  1. Space = 8p + 10(p − 1) = 18p − 10 bytes.
  2. Require 18p − 10 ≤ 1024, so 18p ≤ 1034 and p ≤ 57.44.
  3. The largest integer is 57; check: 8(57) + 10(56) = 456 + 560 = 1016 ≤ 1024, while p = 58 needs 464 + 570 = 1034 > 1024.

Answer: Option (A) — p = 57

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) p = 58 needs 1034 bytes, ten bytes more than the block holds.
  • (C) 64 is the answer for a 10-byte key with a 6-byte pointer, not an 8-byte pointer.
  • (D) 68 ignores the key bytes entirely.

Common misconception. Forgetting that a node with p pointers has p − 1 keys, or dividing the block size by the pointer size alone.

Takeaway. Solve the inequality p·ptr + (p − 1)·key ≤ block, then floor.

DB-U5-Q39MCQeasy

A B+ tree has order p = 7, so every node holds at most 7 tree pointers. A non-root internal node must hold at least ⌈p/2⌉ = 4 pointers. The minimum number of keys in such a node is

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In every B+ tree node the number of keys is one less than the number of tree pointers. Occupancy bounds therefore shift by exactly one when translated from pointers to keys.

Step by step.

  1. Minimum pointers = ⌈7/2⌉ = 4.
  2. Keys = pointers − 1 = 3.
  3. For symmetry the maximum is p − 1 = 6 keys, from 7 pointers.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 keys

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2 keys corresponds to 3 pointers, below the ⌈p/2⌉ = 4 floor.
  • (C) 4 is the pointer floor, not the key floor.
  • (D) 6 is the maximum number of keys, not the minimum.

Common misconception. Quoting the pointer bound as the key bound; a node with k keys has k + 1 pointers.

Takeaway. keys = pointers − 1, always, in every internal node of a B-tree or B+ tree.

DB-U5-Q40MCQmedium

Which one of the following is a difference between a B-tree and a B+ tree of the same order?

  1. A B-tree links its leaves in sequence, while a B+ tree does not.
  2. A B+ tree allows its nodes to be under-full, while a B-tree does not.
  3. A B+ tree stores records only in its leaves, while a B-tree may store records in internal nodes.
  4. A B-tree can index only primary keys, while a B+ tree can index only secondary keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a B-tree every node carries keys and data (or data pointers), so a search may end at an internal node. In a B+ tree internal nodes carry only keys and pointers; all data pointers sit in the leaves, which are chained together.

Step by step.

  1. A B-tree node = keys + data pointers; a search stops as soon as the key is found in any node.
  2. A B+ tree node = keys + tree pointers only; every search runs down to a leaf, and the leaves hold all record pointers.
  3. Because the leaves form a linked list, range queries walk the leaf level sequentially — a B-tree must re-traverse the tree instead.

Answer: Option (C) — data lives only in the leaves of a B+ tree

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) It is the B+ tree, not the B-tree, that links its leaves.
  • (B) Both structures enforce minimum occupancy; under-full nodes are merged in either.
  • (D) Either structure can serve as a primary, clustering or secondary index.

Common misconception. Reversing the leaf-link property. The chained leaves belong to the B+ tree, and that chain is what makes range scans cheap.

Takeaway. B+ tree: all data at the leaf level, leaves linked, every search costs the full height. B-tree: data anywhere, search may stop early.

DB-U5-Q41MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE of a B+ tree of order p? (Select all that apply.)

  1. An internal node holds at most p − 1 keys.
  2. A leaf node holds at most p keys.
  3. A non-root internal node holds at least ⌈p/2⌉ tree pointers.
  4. All leaves are at the same level, so the tree is balanced.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Order p fixes the maximum number of tree pointers in a node at p. Keys are one fewer, and the minimum occupancy of a non-root node is half the maximum, rounded up.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — p pointers admit at most p − 1 keys.
  2. (B) FALSE — a leaf holds at most p − 1 keys (or p − 1 record values); p would require p + 1 pointers, one of which is the sibling link.
  3. (C) TRUE — that is the occupancy floor that keeps the tree at least half full.
  4. (D) TRUE — every leaf is at the same depth, which is what makes every search cost the same number of block accesses.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Including the next-leaf pointer, a full leaf holds p − 1 keys, p record pointers and one sibling pointer.

Common misconception. Applying the internal-node capacity p − 1 keys to the leaves without accounting for the extra next-leaf pointer, or the reverse.

Takeaway. Internal node: p pointers, p − 1 keys. Leaf: p − 1 keys and p record pointers, plus one sibling pointer.

DB-U5-Q42MSQmedium

Consider a B+ tree used as an index on a file of 1,000,000 records. Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Increasing the order p reduces the height of the tree.
  2. A range query is answered by locating the first leaf and then following the leaf sibling pointers.
  3. With no node buffered, a point search costs one block access per level of the tree.
  4. Deleting a record can never reduce the number of levels in the tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The height of a B+ tree is what a search pays for, and the height falls as the fan-out rises. The linked leaf level is what makes a range query cost one descent plus a sequential walk.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — with a larger p each node covers more keys, so fewer levels are needed for the same record count.
  2. (B) TRUE — the sibling chain visits every leaf in key order, so the range is read sequentially.
  3. (C) TRUE — the root, each internal level and the leaf are each one block, so the cost equals the height.
  4. (D) FALSE — an under-full node is merged, and if the root loses its last key the root is removed and the height drops by one.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) Merging on deletion can empty the root; the tree then shrinks by a level.

Common misconception. Treating the height of a B+ tree as fixed. It grows on splits and shrinks on merges.

Takeaway. Height drives search cost; leaf links drive range cost; both change with insertions and deletions.

DB-U5-Q43MCQhard

A B+ tree has order p = 5 and three levels: a root, one level of internal nodes, and leaves. A non-root internal node holds at least ⌈p/2⌉ = 3 pointers, a non-root leaf holds at least ⌈(p − 1)/2⌉ = 2 records, and the root holds at least 2 pointers. What is the smallest number of records such a tree can index?

  1. 6
  2. 18
  3. 24
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A minimum-capacity count multiplies the smallest fan-out at every level. The root is special: it needs only two children, not ⌈p/2⌉.

Step by step.

  1. Root: at least 2 pointers → 2 children at the internal level.
  2. Each of those 2 internal nodes: at least 3 pointers → 2 × 3 = 6 leaves.
  3. Each of those 6 leaves: at least 2 records → 6 × 2 = 12 records.

Answer: Option (D) — 12 records

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 6 counts the leaves, not the records they hold.
  • (B) 18 applies ⌈p/2⌉ = 3 to the root as well; the root may have as few as 2 children.
  • (C) 24 uses the maximum fan-out at some level instead of the minimum.

Common misconception. Applying the ⌈p/2⌉ occupancy floor to the root. The root is exempt: with a single key it has two children.

Takeaway. Minimum records = (root fan-out) × ⌈p/2⌉^(h−2) × ⌈(p−1)/2⌉, with the root counted as 2.

DB-U5-Q44NAThard

A B-tree of order p stores at most p tree pointers and p − 1 keys in every node. The block size is 4096 bytes, a key is 16 bytes and a pointer is 8 bytes. What is the largest order p that fits in one node?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Node capacity is the largest p satisfying p·pointer + (p − 1)·key ≤ block size. It is an inequality, so the answer is a floor, never a rounding.

Step by step.

  1. Space per node = 8p + 16(p − 1) = 24p − 16 bytes.
  2. Require 24p − 16 ≤ 4096, so 24p ≤ 4112 and p ≤ 171.33.
  3. Largest integer p = 171; check: 8(171) + 16(170) = 1368 + 2720 = 4088 ≤ 4096, while p = 172 needs 1376 + 2736 = 4112 > 4096.

Answer: 171 (the order, an integer; 4088 of the 4096 bytes are used)

Common misconception. Using p keys instead of p − 1, or rounding 171.33 up to 172 — which overflows the block by 16 bytes.

Takeaway. Write the inequality first, solve for p, floor it, then check both p and p + 1.

dense-vs-sparse-indexing · 12
DB-U5-Q07MCQeasy

Which index can decide whether the value 47 occurs in the indexed field without reading the data file at all?

  1. A sparse index on the ordering key.
  2. A dense index on the search key.
  3. A primary index on the ordering key.
  4. None — every index must read the data file to answer an existence query.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dense index holds an entry for every search-key value present in the file, so the index alone decides existence. A sparse index holds entries for only some values, so a missing value proves nothing.

Step by step.

  1. In a dense index, every value that occurs in the file appears as a key in the index.
  2. If 47 is absent from the dense index, it is absent from the file; if it is present, it occurs.
  3. In a sparse index the entry for 47 may simply not have been stored, even though a record with 47 exists, so the index cannot answer the question alone.

Answer: Option (B) — a dense index

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A sparse index omits most values, so absence from the index is not absence from the file.
  • (C) A primary index is sparse by construction.
  • (D) False: a dense index is exactly the structure that answers an existence query from the index alone.

Common misconception. Believing every index answers existence queries. A sparse index answers “where should I look”, not “does it exist”.

Takeaway. Dense means complete: one entry per value, so membership tests are index-only. Sparse means navigational: you land on a block and then look.

DB-U5-Q08MCQeasy

A file of 20,000 records is stored with 8 records per block and is sorted on the search key. A sparse index stores one entry per block. How many entries does the index have?

  1. 2,500
  2. 20,000
  3. 2,000
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A sparse index on the ordering key keeps one entry per block, so its size is the block count, not the record count.

Step by step.

  1. Blocks in the file = 20,000 ÷ 8 = 2,500.
  2. A sparse index on the ordering key keeps one entry per block, so it has 2,500 entries rather than the 20,000 a dense index would need.
  3. Each entry carries the key of the block's first record and a pointer to the block.

Answer: Option (A) — 2,500 entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 20,000 is the record count, which is the size of a dense index, not of a sparse one.
  • (C) 2,000 comes from dividing the records by 10 instead of by the blocking factor of 8.
  • (D) 8 is the blocking factor itself, not a count of entries.

Common misconception. Multiplying by the blocking factor instead of dividing, or answering with the record count out of habit because most index questions are about dense indexes.

Takeaway. For a sparse index, convert records to blocks first; the index lives at block granularity.

DB-U5-Q09MCQeasy

Compared with a dense index on the same ordered file, a sparse index on the ordering key

  1. has fewer entries and may need a scan of the located block to reach the record.
  2. has more entries but avoids scanning the block.
  3. has the same number of entries and the same search cost.
  4. cannot be used in a multilevel structure.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Sparsity trades index size against a final in-block scan. The sparse index is smaller because it skips records; the price is that the entry points at a block, not at a record.

Step by step.

  1. A dense index has one entry per record; a sparse index has one entry per block, so it has far fewer entries and occupies fewer blocks.
  2. A sparse entry identifies the block that must contain the key, so the search finishes with a scan inside that block.
  3. Hence: smaller index, one extra in-block scan.

Answer: Option (A)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) A sparse index has fewer entries, not more.
  • (C) The entry counts differ by the blocking factor, so the costs cannot be identical.
  • (D) False: multilevel indexes are normally built as sparse structures over the level below.

Common misconception. Assuming sparsity is free. It buys index size with a block scan; a dense index buys direct record access with index size.

Takeaway. Every index question is a size-versus-access trade. Say which side the stem is rewarding before choosing.

DB-U5-Q10MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE of dense and sparse indexes? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A sparse index on a search key can be built only when the file is ordered on that key.
  2. A dense index on a search key can be built on a file stored in any order.
  3. A dense index always occupies fewer blocks than a sparse index on the same file.
  4. A secondary index on a non-key field that does not order the file must be dense.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Sparsity is possible only when the file's order lets the index skip records. Without that order the index must be dense to be usable at all.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: skipping a record is safe only if the ordering guarantees that the record's key lies between the entry's key and the next entry's key.
  2. (B) True: a dense index carries an entry per record, so it does not depend on where the records sit.
  3. (C) False: dense means more entries, so it occupies more blocks, not fewer.
  4. (D) True: with no ordering there is nothing to skip, so every record needs an entry.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the only false statement; it inverts the size relationship.

Common misconception. Believing a dense index is the “better” structure in every respect. It is the only possible structure when the file is unordered, and the more expensive one when it is ordered.

Takeaway. Ordering is the permission slip for sparsity. No ordering, no sparsity, and the index is dense.

DB-U5-Q11MCQhard

A sparse index is built over 10,000 data blocks. Each index entry is 12 bytes and index blocks hold 1024 bytes. The index is made multilevel until a single top block remains. How many index blocks are there in total?

  1. 118
  2. 120
  3. 121
  4. 122
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Same rule as any multilevel index: entries per block by floor, blocks per level by ceil, stop at the level that fits in one block, then sum the levels.

Step by step.

  1. Entries per index block = ⌊1024 ÷ 12⌋ = 85.
  2. Level 1 over 10,000 blocks: ⌈10,000 ÷ 85⌉ = ⌈117.65⌉ = 118 blocks.
  3. Level 2 over 118 entries: ⌈118 ÷ 85⌉ = ⌈1.39⌉ = 2 blocks.
  4. Level 3 over 2 entries: ⌈2 ÷ 85⌉ = 1 block — stop.
  5. Total = 118 + 2 + 1 = 121.

Answer: Option (C) — 121 blocks

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 118 stops at the first level.
  • (B) 120 is 118 + 2 and omits the top block.
  • (D) 122 over-counts by one, typically from rounding 117.65 up twice.

Common misconception. Rounding the entries-per-block value up instead of down. A block that holds 85 full entries plus a fragment of a 86th holds 85 entries.

Takeaway. Floor for capacity, ceil for blocks. Write both roundings explicitly before adding the levels.

DB-U5-Q12NATmedium

A file has 40,000 records. A dense index is built on its key with one entry per record, each entry 14 bytes, in index blocks of 1024 bytes. How many blocks does the dense index occupy?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dense index has one entry per record, so the entry count is the record count; the block count is then entries divided by entries-per-block, rounded up.

Step by step.

  1. Dense entries = 40,000.
  2. Entries per block = ⌊1024 ÷ 14⌋ = ⌈73.14⌋ = 73.
  3. Blocks = ⌈40,000 ÷ 73⌉ = ⌈547.9⌉ = 548.

Answer: 548 (an integer; 73 × 547 = 39,931 < 40,000, so 547 blocks are not enough)

Common misconception. Rounding 547.9 down to 547. A dense index must store every entry, so the last, partly filled block is still a block.

Takeaway. Check the rounding with a multiplication: if blocks × entries-per-block < entries, one more block is needed.

DB-U5-Q32MCQeasy

A sparse index on the search key of a file can be built only when

  1. the file is physically ordered on that key
  2. the key values are unique
  3. the file occupies at most one block
  4. the index is made multilevel
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Sparsity means that some search-key values have no index entry. That is safe only if the file order guarantees that a missing value lies between the values of two neighbouring entries, which requires the file to be ordered on the indexed field.

Step by step.

  1. A sparse entry is a "starting point": it locates the block from which a scan begins.
  2. The scan only finds the record if all values between two entries lie in the blocks that follow the first entry — that is exactly what physical ordering guarantees.
  3. Without ordering, an unindexed value could sit anywhere in the file, so a sparse index would miss it.

Answer: Option (A) — the file must be physically ordered on that key

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Uniqueness is not needed: a sparse index on an ordered non-key field is a clustering index.
  • (C) A one-block file needs no index at all; the condition is about ordering, not size.
  • (D) Multilevel indexes are built on top of an existing first-level index and do not create the ability to be sparse.

Common misconception. Thinking sparsity depends on the key being unique. It depends on the file order, which is why a clustering index on an ordered non-key field is also sparse.

Takeaway. Sparsity ⇔ the indexed field orders the file.

DB-U5-Q33MCQeasy

A file of 45,000 records is stored with 9 records per block and is ordered on its search key. A sparse index stores one entry per block. How many entries does the index have?

  1. 45,000
  2. 5,000
  3. 9
  4. 4,500
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A sparse index on the ordering key keeps one entry per data block, because the block's first key identifies every record in that block.

Step by step.

  1. Blocks = 45,000 ÷ 9 = 5,000.
  2. One entry per block gives 5,000 entries.

Answer: Option (B) — 5,000 entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 45,000 is the record count, which would be a dense index.
  • (C) 9 is the number of records per block, not a count of entries.
  • (D) 4,500 divides by 10 rather than by the 9 records per block.

Common misconception. Dividing by a round number instead of by the stated records-per-block.

Takeaway. Sparse entries on an ordering key = number of data blocks = ⌈records ÷ records-per-block⌉.

DB-U5-Q34MCQeasy

For a successful point search on the ordering key, a sparse index and a dense index on the same key both cost one index search plus one data-block read. The sparse index needs no extra block read because

  1. a sparse index never needs to read the data file
  2. the sparse index stores the full record in each entry
  3. the extra scan happens inside the data block that was already read
  4. the dense index needs two data-block reads
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A sparse entry points at the block whose key range contains the search value. That block has to be read anyway, and the remaining scan takes place inside it, so no additional I/O is added.

Step by step.

  1. The index search ends at the largest entry whose key is ≤ the target.
  2. That entry supplies the address of the data block where the target must lie.
  3. Reading that block is the one data-block access the search needs; scanning it costs CPU time, not another block access.
  4. A dense index reaches the same block through an exact entry, so its cost is identical.

Answer: Option (C) — the scan is absorbed by the data block that is read anyway

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Every index search ends with a data-block read; no index avoids it.
  • (B) An index entry stores a search value and a pointer, never the full record.
  • (D) A dense index needs exactly one data-block read, not two.

Common misconception. Adding an extra block access for the in-block scan. The scan is a CPU cost inside a block that the search reads regardless.

Takeaway. Sparse and dense indexes have the same I/O for a point search; sparsity buys index size, not search time.

DB-U5-Q35MCQhard

A file of 60,000 records is stored with 15 records per block and is ordered on its key. A dense index on the key uses 10-byte keys and 6-byte pointers in 1024-byte blocks. How many blocks does the dense index occupy?

  1. 937
  2. 63
  3. 4,000
  4. 938
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dense index on a key holds one entry per record, so its block count is ⌈records ÷ entries-per-block⌉. Compare that with the sparse index on the same file, which holds one entry per block.

Step by step.

  1. A dense index has one entry per record = 60,000 entries.
  2. One entry = 10 + 6 = 16 bytes, so one index block holds ⌊1024 ÷ 16⌋ = 64 entries.
  3. Dense blocks = ⌈60,000 ÷ 64⌉ = ⌈937.5⌉ = 938.
  4. For contrast the sparse index would need 60,000 ÷ 15 = 4,000 entries, i.e. ⌈4,000 ÷ 64⌉ = 63 blocks.

Answer: Option (D) — 938 blocks

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 937 truncates 937.5 and loses the partly filled final block.
  • (B) 63 is the size of the sparse index on the same file, one entry per block.
  • (C) 4,000 is the sparse index's entry count, not the dense index's block count.

Common misconception. Confusing entry counts with block counts, or truncating instead of taking the ceiling.

Takeaway. Dense index entries = records; sparse index entries = blocks. Divide each by entries-per-block and ceil.

DB-U5-Q36MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE of dense and sparse indexes? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A sparse index holds fewer entries than a dense index on the same ordered file.
  2. A dense index can be built on a file stored in any order.
  3. A sparse index always answers an existence query without reading the data file.
  4. A sparse index on a non-ordering field is impossible.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Density is about whether every search value has an entry; sparsity is about whether the file order lets the index skip records. Ordering is the resource that sparsity spends.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — a sparse index drops entries that the file order makes redundant, so it has fewer of them (and therefore fewer blocks).
  2. (B) TRUE — a dense index does not rely on the file order at all; that is why it is the only option on an unordered file.
  3. (C) FALSE — an existence query on a sparse index must still read the candidate block to see whether the value is actually present.
  4. (D) TRUE — with no ordering, an unindexed value could be anywhere in the file.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) Sparsity means some values have no entry, so absence from the index is not absence from the file; the block must be read.

Common misconception. Treating a sparse index as a complete membership test. It is not: it narrows the search to one block, nothing more.

Takeaway. Dense → membership decidable from the index. Sparse → always one confirming block read.

DB-U5-Q37NATmedium

A file of 30,000 records is stored with 12 records per block and is ordered on its search key. A sparse index has one entry per block; each entry is 12 bytes and index blocks are 1024 bytes. How many blocks does the index occupy?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A sparse index on the ordering key has one entry per data block. Its own block count then follows from the entry size and the block size.

Step by step.

  1. Data blocks = 30,000 ÷ 12 = 2,500, so the index has 2,500 entries.
  2. One block holds ⌊1024 ÷ 12⌋ = 85 entries.
  3. Blocks = ⌈2,500 ÷ 85⌉ = ⌈29.41⌉ = 30.

Answer: 30 (the last block is partly empty but is still allocated)

Common misconception. Taking ⌊2,500 ÷ 85⌋ = 29 and dropping the partial final block.

Takeaway. Two ceilings are hidden here: one for data blocks and one for index blocks.

primary-secondary-clustering-indexes · 12
DB-U5-Q01MCQeasy

A file of 30,000 records is stored in 1,000 blocks and is physically sorted on its search key. A primary index is built on that key. The index stores one entry for each

  1. record in the file.
  2. block in the file.
  3. distinct value of the search key.
  4. page of the index itself.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A primary index is a sparse index on the ordering key field of a file that is physically sorted on that key. Because the file is sorted, one entry per block is enough: the entry carries the key of the first record in the block together with a pointer to that block.

Step by step.

  1. The file is sorted on the search key, so the records of one block occupy a contiguous run of key values and the block's first key identifies the whole block.
  2. A single index entry per block therefore locates any key in that block after one extra read of the block.
  3. With 30,000 records in 1,000 blocks the primary index has 1,000 entries.

Answer: Option (B) — one entry per block

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) One entry per record is a dense index; the ordering already makes those extra entries redundant for a primary index.
  • (C) One entry per distinct value describes a clustering index on a non-key field, or a dense secondary index on a key field.
  • (D) A page of the index is a unit inside the index, not a unit of the indexed file.

Common misconception. Treating the primary index as a dense index. The physical ordering is the whole reason the index may be sparse; drop the ordering and the same question has a different answer.

Takeaway. Primary index = ordered key + sparse = one entry per block. When a stem says “sorted on the key”, expect a block-level count, not a record-level one.

DB-U5-Q02MCQeasy

A file of 30,000 records in 1,000 blocks is sorted on a non-key field dept that has 500 distinct values. A clustering index on dept has how many entries?

  1. 500
  2. 1,000
  3. 30,000
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A clustering index is built on a non-key field that orders the file. Several records may share a value, so the index holds one entry per distinct value, pointing at the first block that holds that value.

Step by step.

  1. The file is sorted on dept, not on a key, so equal values sit together but a value may span many records and several blocks.
  2. The index therefore cannot be per record (too many) and cannot be per block (a value may span blocks, and one block may hold two values).
  3. It is per distinct value of the ordering field: 500 distinct values give 500 entries.

Answer: Option (A) — 500 entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 1,000 is the block count, which is the entry count of a primary index on the key, not of a clustering index on a non-key field.
  • (C) 30,000 is the record count, the entry count of a dense secondary index.
  • (D) A single entry would only be right if the field had one distinct value.

Common misconception. Assuming every index on the ordering field is block-level. That holds only when the field is a key; with duplicates the entry count collapses to the number of distinct values.

Takeaway. Three counts, three indexes: per block for a primary index, per distinct value for a clustering index, per record for a dense secondary index.

DB-U5-Q03NATmedium

A file has 50,000 records with 10 records per block, and is sorted on its search key. A single-level primary index uses entries of a 10-byte key plus a 6-byte block pointer, and index blocks are 1024 bytes. How many blocks does the primary index occupy?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A primary index has one entry per data block. Its size in blocks follows from the number of entries that fit in one index block, which is the block size divided by the entry size.

Step by step.

  1. Data blocks = 50,000 ÷ 10 = 5,000, so the primary index has 5,000 entries.
  2. One entry = 10 + 6 = 16 bytes, so an index block holds ⌊1024 ÷ 16⌋ = 64 entries.
  3. Blocks needed = ⌈5,000 ÷ 64⌉ = ⌈78.125⌉ = 79.

Answer: 79 (an integer number of blocks; the last block is partly empty but is still allocated)

Common misconception. Using ⌊5000 ÷ 64⌋ = 78 and forgetting the partial final block. Allocation is in whole blocks, so the count always rounds up.

Takeaway. Index sizing is two steps: floor for entries per block, ceil for blocks. Reversing the two roundings is the commonest arithmetic slip in this topic.

DB-U5-Q04MCQeasy

Which of the following can place all records that share a value of the indexed field in one contiguous run of blocks?

  1. A dense secondary index on a non-ordering field.
  2. A clustering index on a non-key ordering field.
  3. A hash index on the search key.
  4. A B+ tree index on a non-ordering field.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Contiguity of equal values is a property of the physical file order, not of the index structure. Only an index on the field the file is sorted by can promise it.

Step by step.

  1. A clustering index is defined on a non-key field that orders the file, so all records with one value of that field are stored next to each other.
  2. Every other index here is built on a field that does not order the file, so equal values may be scattered over the whole file.
  3. Hence only the clustering index guarantees the contiguous run.

Answer: Option (B) — a clustering index on a non-key ordering field

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A dense secondary index is by definition on a field that does not order the file; its entries point to scattered records.
  • (C) A hash index sends a key to a bucket, which says nothing about where equal values of a different field sit.
  • (D) A B+ tree orders its own leaves by key, not the data file; the records themselves stay wherever they were written.

Common misconception. Reading “index on a field” as “file sorted on that field”. An index and the file it indexes are ordered independently, and only the clustering index ties them together.

Takeaway. Ask what orders the file. If the answer is the indexed field, the index is primary (key) or clustering (non-key); otherwise it is secondary and nothing is contiguous.

DB-U5-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about single-level indexes are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A primary index is a sparse index built on the ordering key field of the file.
  2. A clustering index can have several records under one index entry, because the indexed field is not a key.
  3. A secondary index can be built only on a candidate key of the file.
  4. An index on a field that does not order the file must be dense, with one entry for every record.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Indexes are classified on two axes: the field they are built on (key or non-key) and whether that field orders the file. The four combinations are the primary, clustering and secondary indexes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True by definition: the file is sorted on the key, so the index needs only the first key of each block.
  2. (B) True: the indexed field is non-key, so one value may cover many records and one entry points at the first of them.
  3. (C) False. A secondary index may be built on any field; on a non-key field it is dense and keeps several records per entry.
  4. (D) True: with no ordering to exploit, the index cannot skip records, so it must carry an entry for each one.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the only false statement: “secondary” means “not on the ordering field”, not “on a key only”.

Common misconception. Reading “secondary” as a synonym for “on a non-key field”, or as “on a key only”. It names the field's relation to the file order, and nothing else.

Takeaway. Label an index by answering two questions: is the field a key, and does the file's order follow it? Those two bits generate all the standard properties.

DB-U5-Q06MCQhard

A file has 5,000 data blocks and a primary index whose entries are 16 bytes, stored in 1024-byte blocks. The index is made multilevel, repeatedly indexing the previous level, until a single top block remains. How many index blocks does the whole multilevel structure contain?

  1. 79
  2. 81
  3. 82
  4. 5,000
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multilevel index is a sparse index over the previous level, so each level is sized by the same rule: entries divided by entries-per-block, rounded up. Stop when a level fits in one block.

Step by step.

  1. Entries per index block = ⌊1024 ÷ 16⌋ = 64.
  2. Level 1 over 5,000 data blocks: ⌈5,000 ÷ 64⌉ = 79 blocks.
  3. Level 2 over 79 entries: ⌈79 ÷ 64⌉ = 2 blocks.
  4. Level 3 over 2 entries: ⌈2 ÷ 64⌉ = 1 block — a single top block, so stop.
  5. Total = 79 + 2 + 1 = 82 blocks.

Answer: Option (C) — 82 blocks

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 79 counts only the first level and ignores the levels built above it.
  • (B) 81 is 79 + 2, i.e. the top block is forgotten.
  • (D) 5,000 is the number of data blocks, not of index blocks.

Common misconception. Stopping one level too early, or adding only the first level. The structure is complete only when the topmost level is a single block.

Takeaway. Size each level separately and add them; the loop ends at the first level whose block count is 1, and that level counts too.

DB-U5-Q26MCQeasy

A file of employee records is physically ordered on the non-key field dept. An index is built on dept so that all records of one department are reachable through one index entry. This index is a

  1. clustering index
  2. primary index
  3. dense secondary index
  4. hash index
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three single-level index types are named by what the file is ordered on and by whether the indexed field is a key. A primary index is built on the field that orders the file when that field is the key; a clustering index is built on the field that orders the file when that field is not a key; a secondary index is built on a field that does not order the file at all.

Step by step.

  1. dept orders the file, so the index is not secondary.
  2. dept is not a key — many records share a value — so the index is not primary.
  3. An index on the ordering non-key field is a clustering index, with one entry per distinct value.

Answer: Option (A) — clustering index

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) A primary index is on the ordering field only when that field is the key; here the field has duplicates, so it is a clustering index.
  • (C) A dense secondary index is used when the file is not ordered on the indexed field.
  • (D) A hash index places records by a computed bucket address and destroys any physical ordering.

Common misconception. Calling any index on the ordering field a primary index. The word "primary" refers to the ordering key; once the ordering field is not a key the correct name is clustering.

Takeaway. Ordering + key → primary; ordering + non-key → clustering; not ordering → secondary (and then it must be dense).

DB-U5-Q27MCQeasy

A file of 12,000 records occupies 800 blocks and is ordered on the non-key field city, which has 120 distinct values. A clustering index on city holds how many entries?

  1. 800
  2. 120
  3. 12,000
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A clustering index has one entry per distinct value of the indexed field. Each entry carries that value together with a pointer to the first block of the run of blocks that hold the records of that value.

Step by step.

  1. The index is on a non-key ordering field, so it is a clustering index.
  2. A clustering index does not need one entry per block: all blocks of one value are contiguous, so one pointer reaches the whole run.
  3. The number of entries is therefore the number of distinct values, 120.

Answer: Option (B) — 120 entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 800 entries is the rule for a primary index, one entry per block; a clustering index needs only one entry per value.
  • (C) 12,000 entries is a dense index, one entry per record — never possible here, since the field is not a key.
  • (D) 12 is the number of blocks per distinct value on average, not the entry count.

Common misconception. Applying the primary-index rule "one entry per block" to every index on an ordering field.

Takeaway. Entry count: primary index = blocks; clustering index = distinct values; dense secondary index = records (or distinct values with a bucket of pointers).

DB-U5-Q28MCQeasy

A secondary index is built on a field that neither orders the file nor is a key, so many records share each value. The standard organisation for such an index is

  1. one entry per record, each entry storing the whole record
  2. one entry per data block, holding the first key of that block
  3. one entry per distinct value, whose pointer leads to a block of record pointers
  4. no index at all, because a non-key field cannot be indexed
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. On a field that does not order the file, an index cannot skip records, so every record must be reachable from it. When the field is also not a key, storing one entry per record would repeat the same value many times, so the standard secondary index keeps one entry per distinct value and makes that entry point to a block of record pointers.

Step by step.

  1. The field is not the ordering field, so a sparse index is impossible — the file order cannot cover unindexed values.
  2. The field is not a key, so a value repeats. Repeating the value in one entry per record wastes index space.
  3. The usual fix is an extra level: one index entry per distinct value, pointing at a block that holds the pointers of all records with that value.

Answer: Option (C) — one entry per distinct value pointing to a block of record pointers

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) An entry never stores the whole record; an index entry stores a search value and a pointer.
  • (B) One entry per block with the block's first key is the rule for a primary index, which requires the file to be ordered on the indexed field.
  • (D) Non-key fields are indexed routinely; that is exactly what a secondary index is for.

Common misconception. Assuming a secondary index always has one entry per record. That is true for a secondary index on a key; with heavy duplication the distinct-value organisation with pointer blocks is the standard one.

Takeaway. Dense is forced by the absence of ordering; distinct-value entries plus pointer blocks are how the index stays compact when values repeat.

DB-U5-Q29MCQhard

A file has 100,000 records with 20 records per block and is ordered on its key. A primary index has one entry per data block; each entry is 20 bytes and an index block holds 2048 bytes. The index is made multilevel, repeatedly indexing the previous level, until the top level fits in a single block. How many blocks does the whole multilevel index contain?

  1. 50
  2. 52
  3. 5,001
  4. 51
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multilevel index treats each level as an ordered file and indexes it, so the entry count of one level is the block count of the level below. The total is the sum of the levels, including the single top block.

Step by step.

  1. Data blocks = 100,000 ÷ 20 = 5,000, so the first level has 5,000 entries.
  2. Entries per index block = ⌊2048 ÷ 20⌋ = 102.
  3. First level = ⌈5,000 ÷ 102⌉ = ⌈49.02⌉ = 50 blocks.
  4. Second level = ⌈50 ÷ 102⌉ = 1 block, so the structure stops here.
  5. Total = 50 + 1 = 51 blocks.

Answer: Option (D) — 51 blocks

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 50 counts only the first level and forgets the top block that indexes it.
  • (B) 52 invents a level: 50 already fits in one block, so there is no third level.
  • (C) 5,001 adds one to the number of data blocks; the index is far smaller than the file.

Common misconception. Summing the levels but forgetting the top block, or adding a level that is not needed because the previous level already fits in one block.

Takeaway. Total multilevel blocks = ⌈N/b⌉ + ⌈⌈N/b⌉/b⌉ + … , stopping as soon as a level equals 1.

DB-U5-Q30MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about single-level indexes on a file of records are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A clustering index is built on a non-key field that orders the file.
  2. A file may have more than one primary index at a time.
  3. A secondary index on a field that does not order the file must reach every record, so it cannot be sparse.
  4. A primary index entry stores the key of the first record in a block together with a pointer to that block.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The index types are defined by the field they are built on and by that field's relation to the file order. "Primary" is tied to the single ordering key; sparsity is possible only where the physical order covers the records between two index entries.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — a clustering index is precisely the index on the field that orders the file when that field is not a key.
  2. (B) FALSE — a file has exactly one ordering key, so it has at most one primary index.
  3. (C) TRUE — without ordering, an index entry cannot cover the records between entries, so every record must be reachable, i.e. the index is dense (possibly with pointer blocks).
  4. (D) TRUE — that is the definition of a sparse primary-index entry.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Two primary indexes would need two different physical orderings of the same file.

Common misconception. Believing a file can carry several primary indexes, or that a secondary index may be sparse.

Takeaway. One ordering key → at most one primary index. No ordering → no sparsity.

DB-U5-Q31NATmedium

A dense secondary index is built on the search key of a file of 10,000 records. Each index entry holds a 12-byte key and a 6-byte block pointer, and index blocks are 1024 bytes. How many blocks does the index occupy?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dense index on a key has one entry per record. The number of blocks follows from how many whole entries fit in one block, and the last partly filled block is still allocated.

Step by step.

  1. A dense index on the key has 10,000 entries.
  2. One entry = 12 + 6 = 18 bytes, so one block holds ⌊1024 ÷ 18⌋ = 56 entries.
  3. Blocks = ⌈10,000 ÷ 56⌉ = ⌈178.57⌉ = 179.

Answer: 179 (an integer number of blocks; the final block is partly empty but is still allocated)

Common misconception. Rounding down to 178 and dropping the partial final block, or forgetting that the pointer also occupies space in the entry.

Takeaway. Always ceil the block count: ⌈entries ÷ entries-per-block⌉.

static-and-dynamic-hashing · 11
DB-U5-Q21MCQeasy

In static hashing, a bucket that receives more records than it can hold is handled by

  1. doubling the number of buckets and rehashing every record.
  2. chaining the excess records in an overflow bucket.
  3. moving the whole file to a second hash area.
  4. rejecting the insert.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Static hashing fixes the number of buckets when the file is created. Overflow is therefore handled inside the fixed structure, normally by chaining overflow buckets to the full bucket.

Step by step.

  1. The bucket address is h(k) and the bucket set never grows, so a full bucket cannot be split.
  2. The standard remedy is a linked chain of overflow buckets attached to the full bucket.
  3. The main bucket is read first; the chain is scanned only if the record is not found there, so overflow degrades search time without changing the address function.

Answer: Option (B) — overflow chaining

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Doubling the bucket count is what dynamic hashing does; in static hashing the count is fixed at creation.
  • (C) Moving the file is not a collision strategy at all.
  • (D) A hash file is expected to absorb overflow, not refuse inserts.

Common misconception. Describing extendible or linear hashing when the stem says static. The whole point of “static” is that the bucket array never changes size.

Takeaway. Static hashing: fixed buckets, overflow chains, performance that decays as the file grows. Dynamic hashing exists to remove that decay.

DB-U5-Q22NATmedium

A static hash file has 10 buckets, each able to hold 3 records. Keys 1 to 40 are inserted with the hash function h(k) = k mod 10. How many records end up in overflow buckets?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With a modulo hash the keys spread evenly over the buckets by residue class; overflow is the excess above bucket capacity in each class.

Step by step.

  1. Keys with k mod 10 = r for r = 0, 1, …, 9 are the keys r, r + 10, r + 20, r + 30 (with 10, 20, 30, 40 for r = 0).
  2. Each residue class holds exactly 4 of the keys 1–40, so every one of the 10 buckets receives 4 records.
  3. Each bucket holds 3, so each sends 1 record to overflow: 10 buckets × 1 = 10.

Answer: 10 records in overflow buckets

Common misconception. Dividing 40 by 10 and concluding there is no overflow, or forgetting the residue class r = 0, which contains 10, 20, 30 and 40.

Takeaway. Enumerate residue classes before counting. Every class here has the same multiplicity, which is what makes the arithmetic a single multiplication.

DB-U5-Q23MCQeasy

In extendible hashing, the directory doubles in size when

  1. any bucket overflows.
  2. a bucket whose local depth equals the global depth overflows.
  3. the load factor of the file exceeds one half.
  4. a record is deleted from a bucket.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Extendible hashing keeps a directory of 2d entries, where d is the global depth. A bucket has its own local depth; only a bucket that is already using all d bits must force the directory to grow.

Step by step.

  1. A bucket with local depth < global depth is shared by several directory entries, so it can be split without touching the directory.
  2. A bucket with local depth = global depth has no spare directory bit, so splitting it requires one more bit for the whole file.
  3. Hence the directory doubles exactly when such a bucket overflows: global depth d becomes d + 1 and the directory size 2d becomes 2d+1.

Answer: Option (B)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A bucket whose local depth is smaller than the global depth splits without any directory change.
  • (C) Extendible hashing reacts to bucket overflow, not to a global load-factor threshold; that is closer to linear hashing's trigger.
  • (D) Deletions may allow buckets to merge, but they never double the directory.

Common misconception. Thinking every overflow doubles the directory. Most overflows are absorbed by splitting a shared bucket; only the deepest buckets force growth.

Takeaway. Compare the local and global depths before predicting a directory change. Equal depths mean doubling; a smaller local depth means a plain split.

DB-U5-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In static hashing the number of buckets is fixed when the file is created.
  2. Linear hashing grows the file one bucket at a time and needs no directory.
  3. Extendible hashing keeps a directory of pointers that may double in size.
  4. Extendible hashing never needs overflow buckets.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three hash organisations differ in how they accommodate growth: static hashing does not, linear hashing adds buckets one at a time, extendible hashing adds directory bits.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the bucket array of a static hash file is chosen once and never resized, so growth shows up as overflow chains.
  2. (B) True: linear hashing adds the next bucket in sequence and uses a split pointer rather than a directory.
  3. (C) True: the directory of 2d entries doubles when the global depth increases.
  4. (D) False: a bucket can still overflow when its local depth is below the global depth, and overflow chains are still permitted.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the only false statement. Extendible hashing reduces overflow, it does not abolish it.

Common misconception. Reading “dynamic” as “no overflow ever”. Dynamic schemes bound the damage; they do not remove the need for overflow handling.

Takeaway. Learn the growth mechanism of each scheme: none (static), one bucket (linear), one bit of directory (extendible). Everything else follows.

DB-U5-Q25MCQhard

An extendible hash table has global depth 2, so its directory holds 4 entries. A record is inserted into bucket A, whose local depth is 2, and A overflows. A split is performed. After the split, the global depth and the directory size are

  1. 2 and 4.
  2. 3 and 8.
  3. 3 and 4.
  4. 4 and 16.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The global depth d fixes the directory size at 2d. A bucket can be split without touching the directory only if its local depth is less than d; equality forces a doubling.

Step by step.

  1. Bucket A has local depth 2 and the global depth is 2, so A already uses every directory bit.
  2. There is no spare bit with which to distinguish two halves of A, so the global depth must increase to 3.
  3. The directory size becomes 23 = 8 entries, and A's two halves get local depth 3.

Answer: Option (B) — global depth 3 and 8 directory entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The directory cannot stay at 4, because A has no bit left to split on.
  • (C) Global depth 3 forces 23 = 8 entries; a directory of 4 would contradict the new depth.
  • (D) One split raises the depth by exactly one, not by two.

Common misconception. Splitting the bucket and leaving the directory alone. That is only legal when the local depth is strictly below the global depth.

Takeaway. One split raises the global depth by at most one. Check local against global first: equal means double the directory, smaller means split in place.

DB-U5-Q45MCQeasy

In extendible hashing with global depth d, the number of directory entries is

  1. 2d
  2. d
  3. the number of buckets
  4. twice the number of buckets
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The directory is indexed by the first d bits of the hash value, where d is the global depth, so it has one entry per bit pattern — 2d of them.

Step by step.

  1. Global depth d means the directory is addressed by d hash bits.
  2. The number of distinct d-bit patterns is 2^d.
  3. Several directory entries may point at the same bucket when that bucket's local depth is less than d.

Answer: Option (A) — 2^d entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) d is the number of bits used, not the number of entries.
  • (C) The directory is usually larger than the bucket count; that is the whole point of local depths.
  • (D) There is no fixed ratio of 2 between directory entries and buckets.

Common misconception. Equating directory size with bucket count. A bucket of local depth less than the global depth is shared by 2^(d − local depth) directory entries.

Takeaway. directory entries = 2^global depth; entries per bucket = 2^(global − local).

DB-U5-Q46MCQeasy

An extendible hash table has global depth 3 and a bucket B with local depth 2. The number of directory entries that point to B is

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bucket of local depth ℓ is addressed by ℓ bits, while the directory is addressed by d ≥ ℓ bits. The 2^(d − ℓ) directory entries that share the same ℓ low-order bits all point at that bucket.

Step by step.

  1. Global depth d = 3, local depth ℓ = 2.
  2. Entries sharing bucket B = 2^(d − ℓ) = 2^(3 − 2) = 2.
  3. Those two directory entries differ only in the most significant bit used by the directory.

Answer: Option (B) — 2 directory entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 1 entry per bucket happens only when the local depth equals the global depth.
  • (C) 4 = 2^(3 − 1) would be the count for a bucket of local depth 1.
  • (D) 8 is the whole directory.

Common misconception. Reading the local depth as a count of directory entries instead of as a number of hash bits.

Takeaway. Pointers to a bucket = 2^(global depth − local depth).

DB-U5-Q47MSQmedium

Which of the following are TRUE of static hashing? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A bucket overflow in a static hash file is resolved by doubling the number of buckets.
  2. The number of buckets is fixed when the file is created.
  3. Records that do not fit in their home bucket are kept in overflow buckets.
  4. As the file grows, the average overflow chain grows and the search cost rises.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Static hashing allocates a fixed bucket array up front. Overflow is absorbed by chaining, so the file can grow without limit but the chains grow with it, and the constant expected search cost of hashing is lost.

Step by step.

  1. (A) FALSE — doubling the bucket array and rehashing everything is precisely what static hashing does not do; that is the dynamic-hashing remedy.
  2. (B) TRUE — the bucket count is chosen at file creation from the expected file size.
  3. (C) TRUE — overflow buckets chained to the home bucket hold the excess records.
  4. (D) TRUE — as the number of records per bucket exceeds the bucket capacity, the chains lengthen and the average number of block accesses per search rises.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Rehashing the whole file is what linear and extendible hashing add; static hashing cannot do it cheaply.

Common misconception. Mixing static hashing with dynamic hashing. Static = fixed buckets + overflow chains; dynamic = grow the file, no long chains.

Takeaway. Static hashing trades unbounded chain growth for a fixed, predictable bucket array.

DB-U5-Q48NATmedium

An extendible hash table has global depth 3, so its directory has 8 entries. Bucket C has local depth 1. How many directory entries point to bucket C?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of directory entries sharing one bucket is 2 raised to the difference between the global depth and that bucket's local depth.

Step by step.

  1. Global depth d = 3, local depth ℓ = 1.
  2. Pointers = 2^(d − ℓ) = 2^(3 − 1) = 2² = 4.
  3. Bucket C is addressed by one hash bit, so four of the eight directory entries — the ones agreeing in that bit — point at it.

Answer: 4 directory entries

Common misconception. Answering 2 (using 2^ℓ) or 8 (the whole directory). The exponent is the difference of the two depths.

Takeaway. Pointers to a bucket = 2^(global depth − local depth); the exponents subtract.

DB-U5-Q49NATmedium

A linear hash file starts with 8 buckets. A split is performed after every 4 insertions: one new bucket is appended to the file and the split pointer advances to the next bucket. After 40 insertions, how many buckets does the file have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Linear hashing grows the file one bucket at a time, driven by a load threshold rather than by a directory. The number of buckets is the initial count plus the number of splits performed.

Step by step.

  1. Splits = 40 ÷ 4 = 10.
  2. Buckets = 8 + 10 = 18.
  3. Because splits are triggered one at a time in round-robin order, no bucket ever needs a chain as long as it would in a static file.

Answer: 18 buckets

Common misconception. Multiplying the insertions by the split rate instead of dividing, or forgetting to add the initial 8 buckets.

Takeaway. Linear hashing: buckets = initial buckets + number of splits; growth is one bucket at a time and needs no directory.

DB-U5-Q50MCQhard

An extendible hash table has global depth 2, so its directory has 4 entries, and every bucket currently has local depth 2. A record is inserted into one bucket and that bucket overflows. After the split of that bucket, the global depth and the number of directory entries are

  1. 2 and 4
  2. 2 and 5
  3. 3 and 8
  4. 3 and 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bucket can be split without touching the directory only if its local depth is below the global depth. When the two are equal there is no spare directory entry, so the directory must double first.

Step by step.

  1. The overflowing bucket has local depth 2, equal to the global depth 2.
  2. A split would need two directory entries per hash prefix, but only one currently points at this bucket.
  3. So the directory doubles: global depth 2 → 3, entries 4 → 8.
  4. Each old bucket is now referenced by two entries, and the overflowing bucket is split into two, each with local depth 3.

Answer: Option (C) — global depth 3 and 8 directory entries

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A split is impossible while local depth equals global depth; the directory must grow first.
  • (B) A directory is a power-of-two array indexed by hash bits; it cannot grow by one entry.
  • (D) Global depth 3 forces 2³ = 8 entries, not 4.

Common misconception. Splitting the bucket without doubling the directory when local depth already equals global depth. That is exactly the condition that triggers the doubling.

Takeaway. Directory doubles ⇔ the overflowing bucket's local depth equals the global depth; otherwise only that bucket's local depth increases.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 1 (ER Models & Relational Algebra), Set 1 of 2 of the unit's second pass through the rotation. All four subtopics are covered: ER diagrams, cardinality and weak entities (7); the relational model and its constraints (6); relational algebra with selection, projection, join and division (6); and tuple and domain relational calculus (6). Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • ER diagrams, cardinality and weak entities. The notation that carries meaning: a double rectangle for a weak entity set, a double diamond for its identifying relationship, a dashed underline for a partial key, a double edge for total participation and a number or letter on the edge for cardinality. Then the translation rules, where the count of relations is decided by construct rather than by entity: strong sets, weak sets, many-to-many relationships and multivalued attributes each cost one relation, while 1:N, 1:1 and identifying relationships cost none. Finally the arity trap: a ternary relationship is a set of triples and its constraints cannot be recovered from three binary relationships.
  • Relational model and constraints. What each constraint actually forbids: a primary key as unique plus not-null, a foreign key as a conditional check that is vacuous on null, a CHECK as a row-local predicate that may not read another table, and referential actions that decide whether a delete is refused, cascaded or nulled. Then the counting that follows: a superkey as any set containing a candidate key, so 2^(n−k) superkeys for one key of size k, and inclusion–exclusion when two candidate keys overlap.
  • Relational algebra. The six primitives and what each derived operator costs to write: intersection as R − (R − S), division as π_A(R) − π_A((π_A(R) × S) − R), theta join as σ_θ(R × S), natural join as product, equality filter and a final projection that drops the duplicate common attribute. Then the algebraic laws that are safe to use: selection commutes with itself and distributes over union, join is commutative, projection does not distribute over join. And the boundary: transitive closure is not expressible, because a fixed expression cannot iterate to a fixed point.
  • Tuple and domain relational calculus. The two calculi and their equivalence: tuple variables range over tuples, domain variables over attribute values, and with the same safety restriction both equal the basic algebra. Then the translation habits: a range atom plus a predicate is a selection, an existential over a second relation is a join, and a universal implication over a relation is division. Finally safety itself, where a bare negated atom ranges over the whole universe and must be replaced by a bounded formula.
  • Connections. Unit 1 is the vocabulary the rest of the subject is written in: Unit 2's SQL is a concrete syntax for these algebra expressions, Unit 3's normal forms are properties of the schemas this unit produces, Unit 4's schedules act on the tuples these relations hold, and Unit 5's indexes are the access paths a query optimiser chooses between when it evaluates a join or a division.

Source categories

Each question carries a data-source tag. STANDARD_TEXTBOOK marks questions built on the standard treatment of the relational model and relational languages (Silberschatz–Korth–Sudarshan, Ramakrishnan–Gehrke, Elmasri–Navathe), written in original wording. ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS marks independently designed GATE-style practice. No question here is presented as an official GATE question: no ER, relational-model, relational-algebra or relational-calculus previous-year item has been verified against an official paper and answer key, so none is claimed.

Provenance: no question here is presented as an official GATE question unless its year, paper and official answer key are recorded in the Question Bank tab. All other items are practice questions written for this series.
